to be the poset of matrices with the domination relation.

We fix the parameters
\lambda^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality we take
d^{\prime}
to be large enough so that
{d}=2^{2d^{\prime}}
(this requires that
d^{\prime}\geqslant\log K+\log\log\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}
for the constant
K>0
in
Theorem
3.10
).

Recall the partial order defined in
Section
2.4
on matrices where we write
A\leqslant M
for two matrices
A,M\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
if
\rank(M)=\rank(A)+\rank(M-A)
.

Fix
Y
, and let
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{SP}(Y)
. Let
W\in Y^{\prime}(\ell)
be a subspace (where
\ell\leqslant d^{\prime}
). In the next two subsections we describe the two steps of the proof: decomposition and upper bounding expansion.

3.2.1
Step I: decomposition

We will show that a link of a subspace
W\in Y^{\prime}(\ell)
decomposes to a tensor product of simpler components.

For this we must define two types of graphs, relying on the domination relation of matrices and the direct sum.

Definition 3.11

(Subposet graph)
.

Let
m>0
and let
U\in\mathcal{M}(4m)
. The graph
\mathcal{T}^{U}=(V,E)
has vertices
V=\mathcal{M}^{U}(2m)
and edges
\{A,B\}
such that there exists
C\in\mathcal{M}(m)
such that
C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\in\mathcal{M}^{U}(3m)
.

For any two
U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(4m)
of rank
4m
, the graphs
\mathcal{T}^{U_{1}}\cong\mathcal{T}^{U_{2}}
so we denote this graph by
\mathcal{T}^{m}
when we do not care about the specific matrix.

Definition 3.12

(Disjoint graph)
.

Let
m,i>0
and let
D\in\mathcal{M}(i)
. The graph
\mathcal{H}_{D,m}=(V,E)
has vertices
V=\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(2m)
and edges
\{A,B\}
such that there exists
C\in\mathcal{M}(m)
such that we have
C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(3m)
.

For any
n
and two
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(i)
, the graphs
\mathcal{H}_{D_{1},m}\cong\mathcal{H}_{D_{2},m}
so we denote this graph by
\mathcal{H}_{i,m}
when we do not care about the specific matrix.

The first component in the decomposition in the link of
W
is the subposet graph.
The second component is the sparsified link graph. This graph depends on
Y
, unlike the previous component. In particular, the matrices in this component will have row and column spaces in
Y
. For the rest of this section we fix
m=\frac{{d}}{2^{\ell+2}}
. Let
Z_{W}
be any matrix whose row span and column span are
\row(Z_{W})=\sum_{M\in W}\row(M)
and
\col(Z_{W})=\sum_{M\in W}\col(M)
. Note that both
\row(Z_{W})
and
\col(Z_{W})
have dimension
(1-\frac{1}{2^{\ell}})d
(that is,
d-4m
). The following definition will only depend on these row and column spaces, but it will be easier in the analysis to give the definition using this matrix
Z_{W}
.

Definition 3.13

(Sparsified link graph)
.

The graph
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
is the graph with vertex set

V=\left\{A\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m)\left|\;\vphantom{A\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m)\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\;\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\in Y\left({d}-2m\right)}\right.\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\;\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\in Y\left({d}-2m\right)\right\}.

The edges are all
\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
such that there exists a matrix
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(4m)
such that
\row(U)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\;\col(M)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\in Y({d})
, and
\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
are an edge in
\mathcal{T}^{U}
.

Formally, the distribution over edges in the sparsified link graph
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
is the following.

1.

Sample
w_{1}\in Y_{\row(Z_{W})}({d})
and
w_{2}\in Y_{\col(Z_{W})}({d})
according to their respective distributions.

2.

Uniformly sample a matrix
U
such that
\row(Z_{W})\oplus\row(U)=w_{1}
and
\col(Z_{W})\oplus\col(U)=w_{2}
.

3.

Sample an edge in
\mathcal{T}^{U}
.

We can now state our decomposition lemma:

Lemma 3.14
.

Y^{\prime}_{W}\cong\underbrace{(\mathcal{T}^{m}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{m}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}^{m})}_{2^{\ell}-1\text{ times}}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
.

This lemma is proven in
Section
3.3
.

3.2.2
Step II: upper bounding expansion

It remains to show that
\mathcal{T}^{m}
and
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
are expander graphs. We prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma 3.15
.

There exists an absolute constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

\lambda(\mathcal{T}^{m})\leqslant\gamma^{m}
for all
m\leqslant{d}
.

In particular, there is an absolute constant
K_{1}>0
such that the following holds: Let
\lambda^{\prime}>0
. If
d^{\prime}\geqslant K_{1}+\log\log\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
{d}\geqslant 2^{2d^{\prime}}
, then

\lambda(\mathcal{T}^{m})\leqslant\lambda^{\prime}.

Lemma 3.16
.

There exists a constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that the following holds. Let
Y
be a
[{d},n,D,\lambda]
-poset for
\lambda<\frac{1}{4{d}}
, and let
W\in Y^{\prime}(\ell)
where
Y^{\prime}=\mathcal{SP}(Y)^{\leqslant d^{\prime}}
and
\ell\leqslant d^{\prime}
. Then

\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{Y,W})\leqslant\gamma^{m}
.

In particular, there is an absolute constant
K_{2}>0
such that the following holds: Let
\lambda^{\prime}>0
. If
d^{\prime}\geqslant K_{2}+\log\log\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}
,
{d}\geqslant 2^{2d^{\prime}}
and
\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{4{d}}
, then

\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{Y,W})\leqslant\lambda^{\prime}.

The constant
K
in
Theorem
3.10
is chosen to be
2^{\max(K_{1},K_{2})}
. We now give an high level explanation of the proofs of
Lemma
3.15
and
Lemma
3.16
, and afterwards we use them to prove
Theorem
3.10
.

The graph
\mathcal{T}^{m}
does not depend on
Y
and the proof of
Lemma
3.15
relies only on the properties of the matrix domination poset. Therefore it is deferred to
Appendix
D
. In a bird’s-eye view, we use
Lemma
2.9
, the decomposition lemma, to decompose the graph into smaller subgraphs, and show that every subgraph is close to a uniform subgraph.

We prove
Lemma
3.16
in
Section
3.4
. Its proof also revolves around
Lemma
2.9
, only this time the subgraphs in the decomposition come from (pairs of) subspaces in
Y
. Observe that in the first step in sampling an edge in
Definition
3.13
, one first samples a pair
(w_{1},w_{2})\in Y_{\row(Z_{W})}({d})\times Y_{\col(Z_{W})}({d})
. The subgraph induced by fixing
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
in the first step is isomorphic to a graph that contains
all
rank
2m
matrices in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n^{\prime}}
for some
n^{\prime}
, that are a direct sum with some matrix
Z_{W}
(independent of
Y
). Thus every such subgraph is analyzed using the same tools and ideas as in the proof of
Lemma
3.15
.

In order to apply
Lemma
2.9
, we also need to show that the decomposition graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
expands (see its definition in
Section
2.1.1
). This is the bipartite graph where one side is the vertices in
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
, and the other side are the pairs of subspaces
Y_{\row(Z_{W})}({d})\times Y_{\col(Z_{W})}({d})
. A matrix
A\in\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
is connected to
(w_{1},w_{2})
if
\row(A)\subseteq w_{1}
and
\col(A)\subseteq w_{2}
.

To analyze this graph, we reduce its expansion to the expansion of containment graphs in
Y
. We make the observation that we only care about
\row(A)
and
\col(A)
, since if
A
and
A^{\prime}
have the same rowspan and colspan, then they also have the same neighbors. To say this in more detail,
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}\cong\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{Q}
where
\mathcal{Q}
is some complete bipartite graph, and
\mathcal{B}
replaces the matrices in
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
with
Y_{\row(Z_{W})}({d}-2m)\times Y_{\col(Z_{W})}({d}-2m)
(here every matrix
A
is projected to
(\row(A),\col(A))
in
\mathcal{B}
).
The graph
B^{\prime}
itself is a tensor product of the containment graph of
Y_{\row(Z_{W})}
and
Y_{\col(Z_{W})}
. Fortunately, as we discussed in
Section
2.3
, this graph is an excellent expander, which concludes the proof.

Given these components we prove
Theorem
3.10
.

Proof of
Theorem
3.10
.

Let us verify all parameters
[d^{\prime},n^{\prime},D^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}]
. The dimension
d^{\prime}
is clear from the construction.

The size
|Y^{\prime}(1)|\leqslant|Y(1)|^{2\cdot\frac{{d}}{2}}
since it is enough to specify
{d}
vectors to construct a rank
\frac{{d}}{2}
matrix
M=\sum_{j=1}^{\frac{{d}}{2}}e_{j}\otimes f_{j}
(of course we have also double counted since many decompositions correspond to the same matrix). Thus
\log|Y^{\prime}(1)|\leqslant{d}\cdot D=D^{\prime}
.

Let us verify that the ambient space is
n^{\prime}=n^{2}
dimensional, or in other words that
Y^{\prime}(1)
really spans all
n\times n
matrices (recall that we assumed without loss of generality that
Y
’s ambient space is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
). For this it is enough to show that there are
n^{2}
linearly independent matrices inside
Y^{\prime}(1)
. Let
e_{1},e_{2}\dots,e_{n}\in Y(1)
be
n
linearly independent vectors. The ambient space of
Y
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. Let us show that
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
is spanned by the matrices inside
Y^{\prime}(1)
. Indeed, choose some subspaces
w_{i},w_{j}\in Y({d})
such that
e_{i}\in w_{i},e_{j}\in w_{j}
. Then
Y^{\prime}(1)
contains all rank
\frac{{d}}{2}
matrices in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n^{2}}
whose row and column spans are inside
w_{i},w_{j}
. These matrices span all matrices whose row and column spans are in
w_{i},w_{j}
and in particularly they span
e_{i}\otimes e_{j}
.

Finally let us show that all links are
\lambda^{\prime}
-expanders. Fix
W\in Y^{\prime}(\ell)
. By
Lemma
3.14

Y^{\prime}_{W}\cong(\mathcal{T}^{m})^{\otimes 2^{\ell}-1}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}

so
\lambda(Y^{\prime}_{W})\leqslant\max\{\lambda(\mathcal{T}^{m}),\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{Y,W})\}
.
Both are
\lambda
-expanders by
Lemma
3.15
and
Lemma
3.16
when the conditions of the theorem are met.

To sum up,
Y^{\prime}
is a
[d^{\prime},n^{\prime},D^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}]
-expanding poset.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

For the readers who are familiar with
[
Gol23
]
, we remark that contrary to that paper, we do not use the strong trickling down machinery developed in
[
KT21
]
.
[
KT21
]
show that expansion in the top-dimensional links trickles down to the links of lower dimension (with a worse constant, but still sufficient for our theorem). A priori, one may think that using
[
KT21
]
could simplify the proof. However, in our poset all links have the same abstract structure, so we might as well analyze all dimensions at once. Moreover, one observes that in many cases the bound on the expansion of these posets actually
improves
for lower dimensions.

3.3
Proof of the decomposition lemma -
Lemma
3.14

The proof of
Lemma
3.14
uses the following claim proven in
[
Gol23
]
.

Claim 3.18

(
[
Gol23
, Proposition 55]
)
.

Let
Y^{all}=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G})^{\leqslant d^{\prime}}
and let
W\in Y^{all}(\ell)
. Then there exists matrices of
Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{2^{\ell-1}}
of rank
\frac{{d}}{2^{\ell}}
, whose direct sum is denoted by
Z_{W}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}Z_{i}
, such that the link graph

Y^{all}_{W}\cong\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}\mathcal{T}^{Z_{j}}\right)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z_{W},m}.

This is not just an abstract isomorphism. The details are as follows. First,
[
Gol23
]
observes that given
W=Im(\widehat{s})
, for some admissible
s
, the set
Im(s)\coloneqq\left\{Z_{j}\left|\;\vphantom{Z_{j}\exists x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell}\setminus\{0\},s(x)=Z_{j}}\right.\exists x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell}\setminus\{0\},s(x)=Z_{j}\right\}
does not depend on
s
, only on
U
. We denote it by
Q_{W}=\{Z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}
.

Intuitively, the following proposition follows from the ’Hadamard-like’ construction of the Grassmann, and the intersection patterns of Hadamard code.

Proposition 3.19

(
[
Gol23
, Lemmas 59,61,62]
)
.

Let
Y^{all}=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G})^{\leqslant d^{\prime}}
, let
W\in Y^{all}(\ell)
, let
Q_{U}=\{Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{2^{\ell}-1}\}
be as above, with
Z_{W}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}Z_{j}
. For every matrix
N
such that
W+\mathrm{span}(N)\in Y^{all}_{U}(\ell+1)
there exists unique matrices
A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{2^{\ell}}
such that

1.

\rank(A_{j})=\frac{{d}}{2^{\ell+1}}
.

2.

N=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}}A_{j}
.

3.

For
j=1,2,\dots,2^{\ell}-1
the matrices
A_{j}\in\mathcal{T}^{Z_{j}}
. That is,
A_{j}\leqslant Z_{j}
.

4.

The matrix
A_{2^{\ell}}\in\mathcal{H}_{Z_{W},m}
. That is,
\row(A_{2^{\ell}})
(resp.
\col(A_{2^{\ell}})
) intersects trivially with
\row(Z_{W})
(resp.
\col(Z_{W})
).

5.

The function
N\mapsto(A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{2^{\ell}})
is an isomorphism

Y^{all}_{W}\cong\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}\mathcal{T}^{Z_{j}}\right)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z_{W},m}

We restate and reprove this proposition in
Section
7
. The proof our decomposition lemma is short given the one from
[
Gol23
]
. For this (and this only) we need to slightly alter
Definition
3.8
and
Definition
3.13
so that we take into account two posets instead of one. Let
Y_{1},Y_{2}
be two posets of the same dimension
{d}
.

Definition 3.20

(Two-poset admissible function, generalizing
Definition
3.7
)
.

An admissible function
with respect to
Y_{1},Y_{2}
to be a function
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
as in
Definition
3.7
with the distinction that the row and column spaces of the matrix
M_{s}=\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)
are

\row(M_{s})\in Y_{1}(2^{{d^{\prime}}}-2^{{d^{\prime}}-i}),\;\col(M_{s})\in Y_{2}(2^{{d^{\prime}}}-2^{{d^{\prime}}-i}).

Definition 3.21

(Two-poset matrix poset, generalizing
Definition
3.8
)
.

We denote by
\mathcal{SP}(Y_{1},Y_{2})
the matrix poset defined in
Definition
3.8
such that the admissible functions are with respect to
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
.

Definition 3.22

(Two-poset sparsified link graph, generalizing
Definition
3.13
)
.

For
W\in\mathcal{SP}(Y_{1},Y_{2})
we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{Y_{1},Y_{2},W}
the graph in
Definition
3.13
, with the distinction that all sums of row spaces are required to be in
Y_{1}
and all column spaces are required to be in
Y_{2}
(instead of them all being in the same poset
Y
).

Proof of
Lemma
3.14
.

Fix
W
and its corresponding
Q_{W}=\{Z_{1},Z_{2},\dots,Z_{2^{\ell}-1}\}
and
Z_{W}
. Conditioned on
W
, the first step of the sampling distribution in
Definition
3.8
is the same as sampling
w_{1}\supset\row(Z_{W})
and
w_{2}\supset\col(Z_{W})
(where
w_{1},w_{2}\in Y({d})
). Fixing the pair
w_{1},w_{2}
and conditioning on all three
W,w_{1},w_{2}
, sampling an edge in the link
Y^{\prime}_{U}
is the same as sampling an edge in the link of
W
in the complex
\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}))
. As both
\mathcal{G}(w_{1})
and
\mathcal{G}(w_{2})
are isomorphic,
\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}))\cong\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{1}))\cong\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}))
12
12

12

Recall that
\mathcal{G}(w_{i})
is the Grassmann poset containing all subspaces of
w_{i}
.
. We are back to the complete Grassmann case and are allowed to use
Proposition
3.19
.
Let
\mathcal{B}(W,w_{1},w_{2})
be the subgraph obtained when fixing and conditioning on
W,w_{1},w_{2}
. By
Proposition
3.19
, we can decompose this graph to a tensor product

\mathcal{B}(W,w_{1},w_{2})\cong\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{2^{\ell}-1}\mathcal{T}^{Z_{i}}\right)\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W}.

The first
2^{\ell}-1
components are independent of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
; they are constant among all conditionals (and are all isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}^{m}
). Hence this is isomorphic to
(\mathcal{T}^{m})^{\otimes 2^{\ell}-1}\otimes\mathcal{R}
where
\mathcal{R}
is the graph corresponding to a subgraph of
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
obtained by conditioning the edge-sampling process of
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
to sampling
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
in the first step. Thus the graph
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W}
and the lemma is proven.
∎

3.4
Expansion of the sparsified link graph

In this section we use the decomposition lemma to reduce the analysis of the sparsified link graph to the case of the complete Grassmann and prove
Lemma
3.16
.

We use the following claim on the complete Grassmann.

Claim 3.23
.

Let
Y^{all}=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G})^{\leqslant d^{\prime}}
be the poset of rank
\frac{{d}}{2}
matrices for a sufficiently large
d^{\prime}
. Let
W\in Y^{all}(\ell)
and let
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathcal{G}({d})
be such that
\row(Z_{W})\subseteq w_{1},\col(Z_{W})\subseteq w_{2}
. Then
\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W)})\leqslant\gamma^{m}
for some universal constant
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

We prove this claim in the next section.

Proof of
Lemma
3.16
.

Fix the subspace
W\in Y^{\prime}(\ell)
and recall the notation
w_{R}=\row(Z_{W}),w_{C}=\col(Z_{W})
. We intend to use
Lemma
2.8
. The local decomposition we use is the following
\tau=(T,\mu_{T})
. For every pair
(w_{1},w_{2})\in Y_{w_{R}}({d})\times Y_{w_{C}}({d})
we define the graph
G_{(w_{1},w_{2})}
which is the subgraph obtained by conditioning on
(w_{1},w_{2})
being sampled in the first step of
Definition
3.13
. The distribution
\mu_{T}
is the distribution that samples
w_{1},w_{2}\in Y_{W}({d})
independently. By definition of
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
this is indeed a local-decomposition.

We observe that the subgraph of
\mathcal{L}_{Y,W}
obtained by fixing
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
in the first step is just
G_{w_{1},w_{2}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W}
and hence by
Claim
3.23
it is a
\gamma^{m}
-expander.

We describe the local-to-global graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
in
Lemma
2.8
.

1.

T=Y_{w_{R}}({d})\times Y_{w_{C}}({d})
,

2.

V=\left\{A\left|\;\vphantom{A\rank(A)=2m,\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W})\in Y,\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\in Y\left({d}-2m\right)}\right.\rank(A)=2m,\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W})\in Y,\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\in Y\left({d}-2m\right)\right\}
,

and is an edge between
(w_{1},w_{2})\in T
and
A\in V
if
\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W})\subseteq w_{1}
and
\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})\subseteq w_{2}
.

Observe that we only care about
\tau(A)=(\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W}))
, and any two
A_{1},A_{2}
with
\tau(A_{1})=\tau(A_{2})
, have the same neighbor set. In other words, this graph is isomorphic to the bipartite tensor product
\mathcal{B}_{R}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{C}\otimes\mathcal{Q}_{1,k}
where
\mathcal{B}_{R}
is the containment graph between
Y_{w_{R}}({d})
and
Y_{w_{R}}({d}-2m)
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{C}
and
w_{C}
), and
\mathcal{Q}_{1,k}
is the complete bipartite graph with
k
vertices on one side and a single vertex on the other side. The number
k
is the number of matrices
A
such that
(\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W}))=(u_{1},u_{2})
, for any pair of fixed subspaces
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. By
Claim
2.14
and the fact that
Y
is
\lambda
-expanding, the graph
G
is a
\left(0.61+{d}\lambda\right)^{\frac{1}{2}({d}-2m)}\leqslant\gamma^{m}
, where
\gamma<1
is some constant and the last inequality is because
\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{4d}
.

By
Lemma
2.8
,
\lambda(\mathcal{L}_{Y,U})\leqslant\lambda(G)^{2}+O(\gamma^{m})=\gamma^{m}
.
∎

4
Johnson complexes and local spectral expansion

In this section we construct a family of simplicial complexes which we call Johnson complexes whose degree is polynomial in their size.
The underlying
1
-skeletons of Johnson complexes are abelian Cayley graphs
\mathrm{Cay}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},\binom{[n]}{\varepsilon n})
.
We prove in this section that these complexes are local spectral expanders and in particular the links of all faces except for the trivial face have spectral expansion strictly smaller than
\frac{1}{2}
.
We then compare the Johnson complexes with the
2
-dimensional local spectral expanders constructed from random geometric graphs
[
Liu+23
]
and show that the former complexes can be viewed as a derandomization of the latter random complexes.
On the way we also define Johnson Grassmann posets
\{Y_{\varepsilon,n}\}_{n}
, analogous to the matrix posets given in
Section
3.1
, and prove in
Section
4.4
that Johnson complexes are exactly
X(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},\beta(Y_{\varepsilon,n}))
, which are Cayley complexes whose vertex links are basifications of
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
s (
Definition
3.4
).

Remark 4.1
.

Later in
Section
7
, we shall define the induced Grassmann posets which generalize both the Johnson Grassmann posets and the matrix Grassmann posets, and we shall provide a unified approach to show local spectral expansion for induced Grassmann posets. However in this section, we will present a more direct proof of local expansion that is specific to Johnson complexes.

We name our construction Johnson complexes due to their connection to Johnson graphs. In particular, all the vertex links in a Johnson complex are generalization of Johnson graphs to simplicial complexes. So we start by giving the definition of Johnson graphs and then provide the construction of Johnson complexes.

Definition 4.2

(Johnson graph)
.

Let
n>k>\ell
be integers. The adjacency matrix Johnson graph
J(n,k,\ell)
is the (uniformly weighted) graph whose vertices are all sets of size
k
inside
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and two sets
t_{1},t_{2}
are connected if
\lvert t_{1}\cap t_{2}\rvert=\ell
.

Next we define Johnson complexes. We shall use
wt(x)
to denote the Hamming weight of a binary string
x
.

Definition 4.3

(Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
)
.

Let
k
and be any integer. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
be such that
2^{k}\;|\;\varepsilon n
. Let
S_{\varepsilon,n}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\left|\;\vphantom{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}wt(x)=\varepsilon n}\right.wt(x)=\varepsilon n\right\}
(abbreviate as
S_{\varepsilon}
when
n
is clear from context). The
\varepsilon
-weight Johnson complex
X=X_{\varepsilon,n}
is the
k
-dimensional simplicial complex defined as follows.

X(0)=\left\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\left|\;\vphantom{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}wt(x)\text{ is even}}\right.wt(x)\text{ is even}\right\},

X(1)=\left\{\{x,x+s\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x,x+s\}x\in X(0),s\in S_{\varepsilon}}\right.x\in X(0),s\in S_{\varepsilon}\right\},

X(k)=\left\{\{x,x+s_{1},\dots,x+s_{k}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x,x+s_{1},\dots,x+s_{k}\}x\in X(0),\forall i\in[k]\,s_{i}\in S_{\varepsilon},\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq T\subseteq[k]\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in T}s_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n}\right.x\in X(0),\forall i\in[k]\,s_{i}\in S_{\varepsilon},\forall\,\emptyset\subsetneq T\subseteq[k]\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in T}s_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n\right\}.

For the rest of this work, we fix
k,n,\varepsilon
as above.

It turns out that the weight constraints on
k
-faces in a Johnson complex gives rise to a Hadamard-like intersection pattern for elements in the link faces. Any
(k-1)
-face in the link of
x
can be written as
\{x+s_{1},\dots,x+s_{k}\}
. If we identify each
s_{i}
with the set of nonzero coordinates in it, these
k
sets always satisfy that any two distinct sets intersect at exactly
\varepsilon n/2
coordinates, and in general any
\ell\leqslant k
distinct
s_{i}
s intersect at
\varepsilon n/2^{i-1}
coordinates. We note that this is similar to the intersection pattern of
k
distinct codewords in a Hadamard code.

The main result of this section is proving that all links of this complex are good expanders.

Lemma 4.4

(Local spectral expansion of Johnson complexes)
.

For any
0\leqslant m\leqslant k-2
, the link graph of any
m
-face in the
k
-dimensional Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
is a two-sided
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{m+1}}\right)
-spectral expander. In particular,
X_{\varepsilon,n}
is a
k
-dimensional two-sided
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k-1}}\right)
-local spectral expander.

The key observation (
Proposition
4.7
) towards showing this lemma is that the link graphs of
m
-faces are the tensor product of Johnson graphs.

Once this decomposition lemma is proven, we can use the second eigenvalue bounds for Johnson graphs to show that the links are
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{m+1}}\right)
-spectral expanders. We note that this construction breaks the barrier
\frac{1}{2}
link expansion barrier of known elementary constructions of local spectral HDXs (discussed in the introduction). Because our construction breaks this barrier, using the Oppenheim’s trickle down theorem
[
Opp18
]
, we
directly get that the
1
-skeleton of the Johnson complex is also a
(1-2\varepsilon)
-spectral expander.

We now outline the rest of this section. First, we prove the link decomposition lemma
Proposition
4.7
. Then using this lemma we show that Johnson complexes are basifications of Johnson Grassmann posets. Afterwards, we apply this lemma to prove the main result. Finally, we conclude this section by comparing the density parameters of the Johnson complexes with those of spectrally expanding random geometric complexes.

4.1
Link decomposition

In this part we prove the link decomposition result.
Note that by construction the links of all vertices
X_{\varepsilon,n}
are isomorphic. So it suffices to focus on the link of
\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
(denoted by
X_{\mathbf{0}}
) to show local spectral expansion for
X_{\varepsilon,n}
.
The link decomposition is a consequence of the Hadmard-like intersection patterns of the faces in
X_{\mathbf{0}}
as explained earlier.

For the rest of the section, we use
C_{i}\subset[n]
to denote the set of nonzero coordinates in a vector
x_{i}
and use
M
to denote
2^{m}
. We now state the face intersection pattern lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
m
be an integer.
Given a set
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and a subset
B\subset[m]
, define

C_{B}=\bigcap_{i\in B}C_{i}\bigcap_{j\not\in B}\overline{C_{j}}\,\text{ and }c_{B}=|C_{B}|\penalty\ .

Then
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
if and only if for any subset
B\subset[m]
,

c_{B}=\begin{cases}\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ &B\neq\emptyset\\
n-\frac{M-1}{M}\cdot 2\varepsilon n&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,.

Proof.

We first verify that every set
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
satisfying the
M
cardinality constraints above is in
X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
. Recall that by definition
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
if and only if they satisfy the
M-1
weight equations

\forall S\subset[m],S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n.

Observe that the set of nonzero coordinates in
\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}
are precisely
j\in[n]
that are nonzero in odd number of vectors in
\left\{x_{i}\left|\;\vphantom{x_{i}i\in S}\right.i\in S\right\}

\text{Set}\left(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\bigoplus_{i\in S}C_{i}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq[m],\\
\lvert B\cap S\rvert\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}C_{B}\penalty\ ,

and that by definition the set
\{C_{B}\}_{B\subset[m]}
is a partition of
[n]
. Therefore the weights can be rewritten as

\forall S\subset[m],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq[m],\\
\lvert B\cap S\rvert\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}c_{B}\penalty\ .

(4.1)

Furthermore, for any nonempty subset
S
there are
M/2
nonempty sets
B\subset[m]
whose intersection with
S
has odd cardinality: let
t=|S|
, then the number of
S
’s subsets of odd cardinality is

\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\binom{t}{i}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{i\leqslant t}\binom{t}{i}-\left(\sum_{i\text{ even}}\binom{t}{i}-\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\binom{t}{i}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{2}((1+1)^{t}-(1-1)^{t})=2^{t-1}.

So the total number of such
B
is
2^{t-1}\cdot 2^{m-t}=M/2
.
Therefore if
c_{B}=\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}
when
B\neq\emptyset
, then
wt(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i})=M/2\cdot 2\varepsilon n/M=\varepsilon n
. Thus under this condition
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
.

Next we prove by induction that any
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
satisfies the conditions on
c_{B}
. The statement trivially holds when
m=0
. Suppose the same statement holds for all
i
-faces where
i\leqslant m-2
.

By (
4.1
), we can deduce that every
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-2)
satisfies:

\forall S\subset[m-1],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B}=\varepsilon n\penalty\ .

(4.2)

Then by the induction hypothesis we know that

c_{B}=\frac{4\varepsilon n}{M}
for
B\neq\emptyset
is the unique solution to the set of constraints above.

Now consider the set of constraints for the
(m-1)
-face
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
.
There are in total
M
variables and we denote them
\{c_{B}^{\prime},c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}\}_{B\subset[m-1]}
where
c_{B}^{\prime}=\lvert C_{B}\cap\overline{C_{m}}\rvert
and
c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\lvert C_{B}\cap C_{m}\rvert
.
The new variables are related to variables in
\{c_{B}\}_{B\subset[m-1]}
via the identity
c_{B}=c_{B}^{\prime}+c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}
.
So (
4.2
) can be expanded as:

\forall S\subset[m-1],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B}=\sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B}^{\prime}+\sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\varepsilon n\penalty\ .

(4.3)

Additionally, via (
4.1
) the weight constraints
wt\left(x_{m}+\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n
can be rewritten as:

\forall S\subset[m-1],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B}^{\prime}+\sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ even}}c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\varepsilon n\penalty\ .

(4.4)

Finally using the constraint
wt(x_{m})=\varepsilon n
, we obtain equations:

\forall S\subset[m-1],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}+\sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ even}}c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\lvert C_{m}\rvert=\varepsilon n\penalty\ .

(4.5)

From (
4.3
),(
4.4
),and (
4.3
) we can deduce that

\forall S\subset[m-1],\,S\neq\emptyset,\penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{B:|B\cap S|\text{ odd}}c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\varepsilon n/2\,.

(4.6)

The
M/2-1
constraints in (
4.6
) are exactly the same as the set of constraints in (
4.2
) except that the RHS values are
\varepsilon n/2
instead of
\varepsilon n
. Therefore by the induction hypothesis, the unique solution is
\forall B\neq\emptyset,\,c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}
. As a result
c_{B}^{\prime}=c_{B}-c_{B\cup\{m\}}^{\prime}=\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}
. Finally we also have
c_{m}^{\prime}=\varepsilon n-(M/2-1)\cdot\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}=\frac{2\varepsilon n}{M}
and
c_{\emptyset}^{\prime}=c_{\emptyset}-c_{m}^{\prime}=n-\frac{M-1}{M}\cdot 2\varepsilon n
. Thus we complete the induction step.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
m
be an integer and
M=2^{m}
.
For any
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(m-1)
,
x_{m}
’s nonzero coordinate set
C_{m}
can be decomposed into the disjoint union of
M/2
sets each of size
2\varepsilon n/M
as follows,

C_{m}=\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}_{B\subseteq[m-1]}C_{B\cup\{m\}}\text{, where }C_{B\cup\{m\}}=\bigcap_{i\in B\cup\{m\}}C_{i}\bigcap_{j\not\in B\cup\{m\}}\overline{C_{j}}\penalty\ .

Applying this coordinate decomposition to vertices and edges in the link graph of any face in
X_{\mathbf{0}}
, we obtain the following characterization of the graphs.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
0\leqslant m\leqslant k-2
be an integer and let
M=2^{m}
. For any
m
-face in
X_{\varepsilon,n}
, the underlying graph of its link is isomorphic to

J(\frac{2\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)^{M-1}\otimes J(cn,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)
where

c=1-\frac{M-1}{M}\cdot 2\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Give an
m
-face
\{x,x+x_{1},\dots,x+x_{m}\}
in
X_{\varepsilon,n}
, we note that by construction its link is isomorphic to the link of
\{0,x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
. So without loss of generality we only consider
m
-faces of the form above and we denote such a face by
t
.
The by definition the vertex set in the link of
t
can be written as

X_{t}(0)=\left\{x_{m+1}\left|\;\vphantom{x_{m+1}\forall\emptyset\subsetneq S\subset[m+1],\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n}\right.\forall\emptyset\subsetneq S\subset[m+1],\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\right)=\varepsilon n\right\}\penalty\ .

By the decomposition lemma
Corollary
4.6
, we have the following isomorphism between the link vertices and collections of disjoints sets in
\binom{n}{\varepsilon n/M}
. First recall the notation
C_{B}=\bigcap_{i\in B}C_{i}\bigcap_{j\in[m]\setminus B}\overline{C_{j}}
. Then the isomorphism
\phi:X_{t}(0)\to\prod_{B\subseteq[m]}\binom{C_{B}}{\varepsilon n/M}
is given by

\phi(x_{m+1})=\{C_{m+1}\cap C_{B}\}_{B\subset[m]}\penalty\ .

We use
\phi(x_{m+1})[B]
to denote the set
C_{m+1}\cap C_{B}
.

Similarly, using
Corollary
4.6
we can write the edge set as:

X_{t}(1)=\left\{\{x_{m+1},x^{\prime}_{m+1}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x_{m+1},x^{\prime}_{m+1}\}x_{m+1},x^{\prime}_{m+1}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(0),\penalty\ \forall B\subset[m],\penalty\ \lvert\phi(x_{m+1})[B]\cap\phi(x^{\prime}_{m+1})[B]\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2M}}\right.x_{m+1},x^{\prime}_{m+1}\in X_{\mathbf{0}}(0),\penalty\ \forall B\subset[m],\penalty\ \lvert\phi(x_{m+1})[B]\cap\phi(x^{\prime}_{m+1})[B]\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2M}\right\}\,.

Therefore the adjacency matrix of
t
’s link graph has entries:

A_{t}\left[x_{m+1},x^{\prime}_{m+1}\right]=\prod_{B\subset[m]}\mathbb{1}\left[\lvert\phi(x_{m+1})[B]\cap\phi(x^{\prime}_{m+1})[B]\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2M}\right]\,.

The product structure of
A_{t}
gives rise to a natural decomposition into tensor products of matrices
H_{B}\in\{0,1\}^{\binom{C_{B}}{\varepsilon n/M}\times\binom{C_{B}}{\varepsilon n/M}}
where

\forall\penalty\ U,V\in\binom{C_{B}}{\varepsilon n/M},\,H_{B}[U,V]=\mathbb{1}\left[\lvert U\cap V\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2M}\right]\,.

Furthermore since every nonempty
B\subset[m]
has
\lvert C_{B}\rvert=c_{B}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{M}n
(
Lemma
4.5
),
H_{B}
is the adjacency matrix of the Johnson graph
J(\frac{2\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)
.
In the case
B=\emptyset
,
\lvert C_{B}\rvert=cn
(
Lemma
4.5
), and so
H_{\emptyset}
is the adjacency matrix of
J(cn,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)
. In conclusion, the link graph of
t
is isomorphic to the tensor product
J(\frac{2\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)^{M-1}\otimes J(cn,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)
.
∎

4.2
Local spectral expansion

We now finish the local spectral expansion proof for Johnson complexes using
Proposition
4.7
together with the following Johnson graph eigenvalue bounds and the trickle-down theorem.

Theorem 4.8
.

[
DL98
]

Let
A
be the unnormalized adjacency matrix of
J(n,k,\ell)
. The eigenvalues of
A
are
\lambda_{0},\dots,\lambda_{k}
where

\lambda_{t}=\sum_{i=0}^{t}(-1)^{t-i}\binom{k-i}{\ell-i}\binom{n-k+i-t}{k-\ell+i-t}\binom{t}{i}\,.

We note that in the theorem above
\lambda_{i}
are unnormalized eigenvalues of the Johnson graphs. In the rest of the paper when we talk about spectral expansion, we consider normalized eigenvalues whose values are always in
[-1,1]
.

We specifically care about the second eigenvalues of the Johnson graphs with
n\geqslant 2k
. The case of
n=2k
is proved in
[
Kos23
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

[
Kos23
]

The Johnson graph
J(2k,k,k/2)
’s eigenvalues satisfy
\max_{t=1,\dots,k}\frac{\lvert\lambda_{t}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}\leqslant\frac{\lvert\lambda_{2}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}=\frac{1}{2k-2}
.

In other words, the graph
J(2k,k,k/2)
is a two-sided
\frac{1}{2k-2}
-spectral expander.

For the case
n>(2+\eta)k
, we show the following lemma (proof in
Appendix
E
):

Lemma 4.10
.

For any constant
\eta>0
, the Johnson graph
J((2+\eta)k,k,k/2)
’s eigenvalues satisfy that
\max_{t=1,\dots,k}\frac{\lvert\lambda_{i}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}
. In other words the graph is a
\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}
-spectral expander.

The trickle-down theorem states that local spectral expansion implies expansion of all links.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
Opp18
]
)
.

Let
\lambda\geqslant 0
. Let
X
be a
2
-dimensional connected simplicial complex and assume that for any vertex
v\in X(0)
, the link
X_{v}
is a two-sided
\lambda
-spectral expander, and that the
1
-skeleton of
X
is connected. Then the
1
-skeleton of
X
is a two-sided
\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}
-spectral expander.
In particular,
X
is a two-sided
\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}
local spectral expander.

Now we are ready to prove the main result of this section.

Proof of
Lemma
4.4
.

Let
t
be any
m
-face in the complex. By
Proposition
4.7
, the link graph of
t
is
G_{t}=J(\frac{2\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)^{M-1}\otimes J(cn,\frac{\varepsilon}{M}n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2M}n)
.
Since eigenvalues of
A_{1}\otimes A_{2}
are
\left\{\lambda_{1}\cdot\lambda_{2}\left|\;\vphantom{\lambda_{1}\cdot\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}\in\mathsf{Spec}(A_{1}),\penalty\ \lambda_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}(A_{2})}\right.\lambda_{1}\in\mathsf{Spec}(A_{1}),\penalty\ \lambda_{2}\in\mathsf{Spec}(A_{2})\right\}
,
by
(
2.1
)
the second largest eigenvalues satisfy
\lambda(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})=\max(\lambda(A_{1}),\lambda(A_{2}))
. Therefore the link graph
G_{t}
of
t
has
\lvert\lambda\rvert_{2}(G_{t})\leqslant\max\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon n/M-2},\frac{1-2\varepsilon}{2(1-(2-M^{-1})\varepsilon)}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1-2\varepsilon}{1-(2-M^{-1})\varepsilon}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{M}\right)
(via
Lemma
4.9
and
Lemma
4.10
). Therefore the
k
-dimensional Johnnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
is a two-sided
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{k-1}}\right)
-local spectral expander.

Furthermore, applying the trickle-down theorem to the
2
-skeleton
X^{\leqslant 2}_{\varepsilon,n}
we get that the underlying graph of the complex is a two-sided
\frac{(1-2\varepsilon)/(2-2\varepsilon)}{1-(1-2\varepsilon)/(2-2\varepsilon)}=(1-2\varepsilon)
-spectral expander.
∎

4.3
Comparison to local spectral expanders from random geometric complexes

In this part, we compare the structure of the Johnson complexes to that of the random geometric complexes from
[
Liu+23
]
.
We observe that the former can be viewed as a derandomization of the latter.

The random
2
-dimensional geometric complex (RGC)
\mathrm{Geo}_{d}(N,p)
is defined by sampling
N
i.i.d. points
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
from the uniform distribution over the
d
-dimensional unit sphere, adding an edge between
ij
if
\langle x_{i},x_{j}\rangle\geqslant\tau
where
\tau
is picked so that the marginal probability of an edge is
p
, and adding a
2
-face if the three points are pairwise connected. In the following parameter regime the complexes have expanding vertex links.

Theorem 4.12

(Rephrase of Theorem 1.6 in
[
Liu+23
]
)
.

For every
\delta<\frac{1}{2}
, there exists
C_{\delta}
and
c=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\log(1/\delta)}\right)
such that when
H\sim\mathrm{Geo}_{d}(N,N^{-1+c})
for
d=C_{\delta}\log N
, with high probability every vertex link of
H
is a two-sided
(1-2\delta)
-local spectral expander whose vertex links are
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\delta\right)
-spectral expanders.

The Johnson complex
X:=X_{\varepsilon,n}
with
N=\lvert X(0)\rvert
can be viewed as an approximate derandomization of the RGCs in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
over
N
vertices. Note that the points in
X(0)
(which are boolean vectors) embed in the natural way to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Furthermore, these points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
all lie on a sphere of radius
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}
centered at
[\frac{1}{2},\dots,\frac{1}{2}]
. Two points are connected in
X
if the
L_{2}
distance between their images in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is exactly
\sqrt{\varepsilon n}
. The distinction from edges in RGCs is that in Johnson complexes points of distance
<\sqrt{\varepsilon n}
are not connected. Albeit the analysis in
[
Liu+23
]
shows that most neighbors of a vertex have distance
\approx\sqrt{\varepsilon n}
so this is not a significant distinction. Similar to RGCs, three points in
X
form a
2
-face if they are pairwise connected. By
Lemma
4.4
the resulting
2
-dimensional Johnson complex
X
has degree
N^{h(\varepsilon)}
13
13

13

h
is the binary entropy function
h(p)=p\cdot\log\frac{1}{p}+(1-p)\log\frac{1}{1-p}
.
and its vertex links are
\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)
-spectral expanders.

Remark 4.13
.

We further observe that while both constructions yield local spectral expanders with arbitrarily small polynomial vertex degree, the link graphs of the Johnson complexes are much denser. Use
N
to denote the number of vertices in the complexes. Then in the Johnson complexes the link graphs have vertex degree
\mathsf{poly}(N)
while in the RGCs the link graphs have average vertex degree
\mathsf{polylog}(N)
.
We remark that one can use a graph sparsification result due to
[
CH07
]
to randomly subsample the
2
-face in
X_{\varepsilon,n}
while preserving their link expansion. As a result we obtain a family of random subcomplexes of the Johnson complexes that are local spectral expanders with similar vertex degree and link degree as the random geometric complexes. Detailed descriptions and proofs are given in
Appendix
F
.

4.4
Johnson Grassmann posets

In this part, we take a small digression to define the Johnson Grassmann posets
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
and show that their basifications are exactly Johnson complexes
X_{\varepsilon,n}
.

Definition 4.14

(Johnson Grassmann posets
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
)
.

Let
d=\log 2\varepsilon n
. For every
i\in[d]
, define the admissible function set as

\mathcal{S}_{i}=\left\{s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\left|\;\vphantom{s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\forall v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\penalty\ wt(s(v))=2^{d-i},\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\right)=2^{d}-2^{d-i}}\right.\forall v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\penalty\ wt(s(v))=2^{d-i},\penalty\ wt\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\right)=2^{d}-2^{d-i}\right\}.

Define the Hadamard encoding
\widehat{s}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
of
s
as

\widehat{s}(y)=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}\langle y,v\rangle s(v).

For each admissible function
s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
, use
\mathcal{G}_{s}
to denote the Grassmann poset
\left\{Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\;U\text{ is a subspace}}\right.U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\;U\text{ is a subspace}\right\}

Then the Johnson Grassmann poset is
Y_{\varepsilon,n}=\bigcup_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{d}}\mathcal{G}_{s}
.

We first verify that these posets are well-defined.

Claim 4.15
.

For all
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
,

Y_{\varepsilon,n}(i)=\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}
.

Proof.

The claim trivially holds for
i=d
. For
i<d
, we need to prove that (1) for every
t\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
, there exist
s\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
and
i
-dimensional subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{d}_{2}
such that
Im(\widehat{t})=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
and (2) for every
s\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
, any subspace
W\subset Im(\widehat{s})
is in
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
.

We first prove (1). Given any admissible function
t\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
, for every
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}
and
u\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d-i}
, use
e_{t(v),u}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}_{2}
to denote the vector that is nonzero only at the
u
-th nonzero coordinate of
t(v)
. Here we identify the vector
u
in a natural way with a number in
[2^{d-i}]
. Use
z
to denote the sum
z=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}t(v)
. Set
U
to be the subspace that spans the first
i
coordinates in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
, and set
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
as follows

\forall v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},u\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d-i},\penalty\ s(v,u)=\begin{cases}e_{t(v),u}\penalty\ &v\neq 0,\\
e_{\vec{1}-z,u}\penalty\ &\text{o.w.}\end{cases}

Then it is straightforward to verify that
s\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
and that
Im(\widehat{t})=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
.

Next we check (2). Note that the codespace
Im(\widehat{s})
is a
d
-dimensional subspace in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
whose nonzero elements have weight
2^{d}/2=\varepsilon n
. Furthermore, consider any linear subspace
W\subset Im(\widehat{s})
of dimension
j<d
. Since
\widehat{s}
is an isomorphism, there exists a
j
-dimensional linear subspace
U\subset\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
such that
W=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
which is the image of
\widehat{s}
restricted to the domain
U
. Let
\psi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d\times j}
be a linear map from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{j}
to
U
, and
\phi\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{j\times d}
be such that
\phi\psi=Id
. Define the function
s_{U}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{j}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
as follows

s_{U}(v)=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in U^{\perp}}s(\psi v+v^{\prime}).

By construction,
s_{U}
is admissible and furthermore for every
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{j}

\widehat{s}_{U}(y)=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{j}\setminus\{0\}}\langle y,v\rangle\cdot\sum_{v^{\prime}\in U^{\perp}}s(\psi v+v^{\prime})=\sum_{v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\langle y,\phi v^{\prime\prime}\rangle s(v^{\prime\prime})=\sum_{v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\langle\phi^{\top}y,v^{\prime\prime}\rangle s(v^{\prime\prime})=\widehat{s}(\phi^{\top}y).

We derive that
Im(\widehat{s}_{U})=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})=W
. Thus
W\in Y_{\varepsilon,n}(j)
and so the claim is proven.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

We observe that
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
is a subposet of the complete Grassmann poset
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})
, and is obtained by applying the sparsification operator
\mathcal{SP}
to
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})^{\leqslant\varepsilon n}
.

We now prove that
X_{\varepsilon,n}
is the basification of
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
using the link decomposition result.

Claim 4.17
.

The
k
-dimensional Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
is the
k
-skeleton of the basification of
Y_{\varepsilon,n}
.

Proof.

To prove the claim it suffices to show that any
\mathrm{span}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})\in Y_{\varepsilon,n}(k)
if and only if all non-empty subset
T\subseteq[k]
has
wt(\sum_{i\in T}x_{i})=\varepsilon n
. In this proof we abuse the notation a bit and identify a vector in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
with the set of its nonzero coordinates.

For the if direction, given
\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k}\}
, construct
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that
s(v)=\cap_{i=1}^{k}x_{i}^{v_{i}}
where
x_{i}^{v_{i}}
is the set of nonzero coordinates of
x_{i}
if
v_{i}=1
, and the set of zero coordinates of
x_{i}
if
v_{i}=0
. By
Lemma
4.5
, all such
s(v)
’s have weights exactly
\varepsilon n/2^{k-1}
. By construction their nonzero coordinates are disjoint from each other’s. So
s
is an admissible function. Furthermore, let
\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{k}\}
be elementary vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{s}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\langle e_{i},v\rangle s(v)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{v,\text{ s.t.}v_{i}=1}x_{i}\cap_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{v_{j}}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\cap\bigcup_{v_{-i}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{k-1}}\cap_{j\neq i}x_{j}^{v_{j}}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\cap[n]=x_{i}

Therefore
\mathrm{span}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k})=Im(\widehat{s})\in Y_{\varepsilon,n}(k)
.

For the only if direction, we can easily check that any
Im(\widehat{s})\in Y_{\varepsilon,n}(k)
contains only vectors of weight
\varepsilon n
. So given any basis
\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k}\}
of
Im(\widehat{s})
, their nonzero linear combinations all have weight
\varepsilon n
. Now the proof is complete.
∎

5
Coboundary expansion and a van Kampen lemma

In this section we give the necessary preliminaries on coboundary expansion and also introduce a version of the famous van Kampen Lemma
[
Van33
]
that shall be used to prove coboundary expansion in the next section.

5.1
Preliminaries on coboundary expansion

We define coboundary expansion for general groups, closely following the definitions and notation in
[
DD23a
]
. For a more thorough discussion on the connection between coboundary expansion and topology, we refer the reader to
[
DD23a
]
. In the introduction we defined coboundary expansion as a notion of local testability of a code (which was a subspace of the functions on the edges). Instead of defining coboundary expansion for only
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-valued functions, we give the most general version of coboundary expansion, which uses arbitrary groups.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex for
d\geqslant 2
and let
\Gamma
be any group. Let
\overset{\to}{X}(1)
be the directed edges in
X
and
\overset{\to}{X}(2)
be the directed
2
-faces. For
i=-1,0
let

C^{i}(X,\Gamma)=\{f:X(i)\to\Gamma\}
. We sometimes identify
C^{-1}(X,\Gamma)\cong\Gamma
. For
i=1,2
let

C^{1}(X,\Gamma)=\left\{f:\overset{\to}{X}(1)\to\Gamma\left|\;\vphantom{f:\overset{\to}{X}(1)\to\Gamma f(u,v)=f(v,u)^{-1}}\right.f(u,v)=f(v,u)^{-1}\right\}

and

C^{2}(X,\Gamma)=\left\{f:\overset{\to}{X}(2)\to\Gamma\left|\;\vphantom{f:\overset{\to}{X}(2)\to\Gamma\forall\pi\in Sym(3),(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})\in\overset{\to}{X}(2)\;f(v_{\pi(0)},v_{\pi(1)},v_{\pi(2)})=f(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})^{\sign(\pi)}}\right.\forall\pi\in Sym(3),(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})\in\overset{\to}{X}(2)\;f(v_{\pi(0)},v_{\pi(1)},v_{\pi(2)})=f(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})^{\sign(\pi)}\right\}.

be the spaces of so-called
anti symmetric
functions on edges and triangles. For
i=-1,0,1
we define operators
{\delta}_{i}:C^{i}(X,\Gamma)\to C^{i+1}(X,\Gamma)
by

1.

{\delta}_{-1}:C^{-1}(X,\Gamma)\to C^{0}(X,\Gamma)
is
{\delta}_{-1}h(v)=h(\emptyset)
.

2.

{\delta}_{0}:C^{0}(X,\Gamma)\to C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
is
{\delta}_{0}h(vu)=h(v)h(u)^{-1}
.

3.

{\delta}_{1}:C^{1}(X,\Gamma)\to C^{2}(X,\Gamma)
is
{\delta}_{1}h(vuw)=h(vu)h(uw)h(wv)
.

Let
Id=Id_{i}\in C^{i}(X,\Gamma)
be the function that always outputs the identity element. It is easy to check that
{\delta}_{i+1}\circ{\delta}_{i}h\equiv Id_{i+2}
for all
i=-1,0
and
h\in C^{i}(X,\Gamma)
. Thus we denote by

Z^{i}(X,\Gamma)=\ker{\delta}_{i}\subseteq C^{i}(X,\Gamma),

B^{i}(X,\Gamma)={\textrm{Im}}{\delta}_{i-1}\subseteq C^{i}(X,\Gamma),

and have that
B^{i}(X,\Gamma)\subseteq Z^{i}(X,\Gamma)
.

Henceforth, when the dimension
i
of the function
f
is clear from the context we denote
{\delta}_{i}f
by
{\delta}f
.

Coboundary expansion is a property testing notion so for this we need a notion of distance. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{i}(X,\Gamma)
. Then

\dist(f_{1},f_{2})=\ProbOp_{{s\in\overset{\to}{X}(i)}}\left[f_{1}(s)\neq f_{2}(s)\right].

(5.1)

We also denote the weight of the function
{\mathrm{wt}}(f)=\dist(f,Id)
.

Definition 5.1

(
1
-dimensional coboundary expander)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex for
d\geqslant 2
. Let
\beta>0
. We say that
X
is a
1
-dimensional
\beta
-coboundary expander if for every group
\Gamma
, and every
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
there exists some
g\in C^{0}(X,\Gamma)
such that

\beta\dist(f,{\delta}g)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f).

(5.2)

In this case we denote
h^{1}(X)\geqslant\beta
.

This definition in particular implies that
B^{1}=Z^{1}
for any coefficient group.

Coboundary expansion generalizes edge expansion

We note that
1
-dimensional coboundary expanders are generalizations of edge expanders to simplicial complexes of dimension
\geqslant 2
. A graph
G=(V,E)
is a
1
-dimensional simplicial complex. For
\Gamma=\mathbb{F}_{2}
we can analogously define function spaces
C^{-1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2}),C^{0}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})
,
C^{1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and operators
{\delta}_{-1}
and
{\delta}_{0}
over
G
.

Observe that
B^{0}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})
consists of constant functions over the vertices.
Using the notations above we can write the definition of a
0
-dimensional coboundary expander as follows.

Definition 5.2

(
0
-dimensional coboundary expander in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
)
.

A
1
-dimensional simplicial complex
G=(V,E)
is a
0
-dimensional
\beta
-coboundary expander in
\mathbb{F}_{2}
if for every
f\in C^{0}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})
there exists some
g\in C^{-1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})
such that

\beta\dist(f,{\delta}_{-1}g)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}_{0}f).

(5.3)

Let us check that this is equivalent to the definition of
\beta
-edge expanders in terms of graph conductance.
Viewing every function
f

as a partition of
V
into two sets
V_{f}
and
\overline{V_{f}}
and viewing
{\delta}_{0}f
as the indicator function of whether an edge is in the cut between
V_{f}
and
\overline{V_{f}}
, we derive that

\min_{g\in C^{-1}(G,\mathbb{F}_{2})}\dist(f,{\delta}_{-1}g)=\min\left(\frac{|V_{f}|}{|V|},\frac{|\overline{V_{f}}|}{|V|}\right),\penalty\ {\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}_{0}f)=\frac{|E(V_{f},\overline{V_{f}})|}{|E|}.

Then the condition
(
5.3
)
is equivalent to the statement that for every vertex partition
(V_{f},\overline{V_{f}})
of the graph
G=(V,E)
,

\beta\min\left(\frac{|V_{f}|}{|V|},\frac{|\overline{V_{f}}|}{|V|}\right)\leqslant\frac{|E(V_{f},\overline{V_{f}})|}{|E|}.

This is the standard definition of a
\beta
-edge expander.

Coboundary Expansion and Local Testability

One should view coboundary expansion as a property testing notion. We call
B^{1}(X,\Gamma)\subseteq C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
a code (as a subset, no linearity is assumed in the general case). Given a function in the ambient space
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
one cares about its Hamming distance to the code, that is, how many edges of
f
need to be changed in order to get a word in
B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
. Denote this quantity
\dist(f,B^{1}(X,\Gamma))
. Coboundary expansion relates this quantity to another quantity, the fraction of triangles
\{u,v,w\}
such that
{\delta}f(uvw)\neq Id
, namely
{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)
.

We note that if
f={\delta}g\in B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
, namely
f
satisfies the property of being in the function space, then one can verify that
{\delta}{\delta}g=Id
, or in other words, that
{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f)=0
. In a
\beta
-coboundary expander we get a robust inverse of this fact. That is, that if
f
is
\varepsilon
-far from any function in
B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
, then its coboundary is not equal to the identity on at least a
\beta\varepsilon
-fraction of the triangles.

This suggests the following natural test for this property.

1.

Sample a random triangle
\{u,v,w\}\in X(2)
.

2.

Accept if and only if
f(uv)=f(uw)\cdot f(wv)
(or equivalently
{\delta}f(uvw)=Id
).

Note that
{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f(uvw))
is the probability that
f
fails the test. Saying that a complex is a coboundary expander is saying that this test is sound, namely
X
is a
\beta
-coboundary expander if and only if for every
f:\overset{\to}{X}(1)\to\Gamma

\dist(f,B^{1}(X,\Gamma))\leqslant\frac{1}{\beta}\ProbOp_{{uvw}}\left[f\text{ fails the test}\right].

(5.4)

Over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, this view of coboundary expansion was first observed by
[
KL14
]
, who used it for testing whether matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
have low rank. This view also appeared in
[
DM22
]
for a topological property testing notion called cover-stability. In later works, this test was as a component in the analysis of agreement tests
[
GK22
,
DD23
,
BM23
]
. We also refer the reader to
[
BM23
,
DD23b
]
for additional connections of coboundary expansion and local testing in the context of unique games problems and to
[
CL23
]
for its connection to group (te)stability.

5.2
Contractions, cycles and cones

One general approach to proving coboundary expansion is the cone method invented by
[
Gro10
]
and used in many subsequent works to bound coboundary expansion of various complexes
[
LMM16
,
KM18
,
KM19
,
KO21
,
DD23a
]
. The following adaptation of the cone method to functions over non-abelian groups is due to
[
DD23b
]
. We adopt their definition of cones, and after presenting the definition, we will explain the motivation behind it as well as its connection to isoperimetric inequalities of the complex.

We use ordered sequences of vertices to denote paths in
X
, and
\circ
to denote the concatenation operator
16
16

16

If
P=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
and
Q=(u_{0}=v_{m},u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
then their concatenation is
P\circ Q=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m},u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
.
, and for any path
P
, we use
P^{-1}
to denote the reverse path.
Fixing a simplicial complex
X
, we define two symmetric relations on paths in
X
.

(BT)

We say that
P_{0}\overset{(BT)}{\sim}P_{1}
if
P_{i}=Q\circ(u,v,u)\circ Q^{\prime}
and
P_{1-i}=Q\circ(u)\circ Q^{\prime}
where
i\in\{0,1\}
.

(TR)

We say that
P_{0}\overset{(TR)}{\sim}P_{1}
if
P_{i}=Q\circ(u,v)\circ Q^{\prime}
and
P_{1-i}=Q\circ(u,w,v)\circ Q^{\prime}
for some triangle
uwv\in X(2)
and
i=0,1
.

Let
\sim
be the smallest equivalence relation that contains the above relations (i.e. the transitive closure of two relations). We comment that these are also the relations defining the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X,v_{0})
of the complex
X
(see e.g.
[
Sur84
]
).

We also want to keep track of the number of
(TR)
equivalences used to get from one path to another. Towards this we denote by
P\sim_{1}P^{\prime}
if there is a sequence of paths
(P=P_{0},P_{1},...,P_{m}=P^{\prime})
and
j\in[m-1]
such that:

1.

P_{j}\overset{(TR)}{\sim}P_{j+1}
and

2.

For every
j^{\prime}\neq j
,
P_{j^{\prime}}\overset{(BT)}{\sim}P_{j^{\prime}+1}
.

I.e. we can get from
P
to
P^{\prime}
by a sequence of equivalences, where exactly one equivalence is by
(TR)
.

Similarly, for
i>1
we denote by
P\sim_{i}P^{\prime}
if we get from
P
to
P^{\prime}
using
i
triangles.

A contraction is a sequence of equivalent
cycles
that ends with a single point. More formally,

Definition 5.3
.

Let
C=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{t},v_{0})
be a cycle around
v_{0}
. A contraction
S
is a sequence of cycles
S=(C_{0}=C,C_{1},...,C_{m}=(v_{0}))
such that
C_{i}\sim_{1}C_{i+1}
for every
i=0,1,\dots,m-1
. If the contraction contains
m
cycles, we say it uses
m
-triangles, or simply that it is an
m
-contraction.

A
m
-contraction can be viewed as a witness to
C_{0}\sim_{m}(v_{0})
.

Before giving the definition of cones which are used to prove coboundary expansion, we provide some intuition behind the connection between coboundary expansion and cones.
In order to prove
(
5.4
)
for a complex
X
, we need to upper bound the distance from any function
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
to the codespace
B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
(with
{\delta}_{0}
being the encoding function). This is done by constructing a message
g\in C^{0}(X,\Gamma)
from
f
and
\mathcal{P}
a family of paths from one vertex
v_{0}
to every other vertex.
Note that every edge together with the two paths in
\mathcal{P}
from
v_{0}
to the edge’s endpoints form a cycle.
The ratio between the probability that
f
fails the test and the distance from
f
to the codespac is bounded by these cycles’ length of contraction.
The vertex
v_{0}
, the family of paths, and the contractions of the induced cycles constitutes a cone.
The connection between the cone and coboundary expansion of
X
is captured by
Theorem
5.6
and a detailed proof can be found in
[
DD23b
]
.

Definition 5.4

(cone)
.

A cone is a triple
\mathcal{A}=(v_{0},\{P_{u}\}_{u\in X(0)},\{S_{uw}\}_{uw\in X(1)})
such that

1.

v_{0}\in X(0)
.

2.

For every
u\in X(0)\setminus\{v_{0}\}
,
P_{u}
is a walk from
v_{0}
to
u
. For
u=v_{0}
, we take
P_{v_{0}}=(v_{0})
to be the cycle with no edges from
v_{0}
to itself.

3.

For every
uw\in X(1)
,
S_{uw}
is a contraction of
P_{u}\circ(u,w)\circ P_{w}^{-1}
.

The sequence
S_{uw}
depends on the direction of the edge
uw
. We take as a convention that
S_{wu}
just reverses all cycles of
S_{uw}
(which is also a contraction Of the reverse cycle). Thus for each edge it is enough to define one of
S_{uw},S_{wu}
.

The maximum length of contraction of
S_{uw}
among all edges
uw
is called the cone area.

Definition 5.5

(Cone area)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a cone. The
area
of the cone is

Area(\mathcal{A})=\max_{uw\in X(1)}\lvert S_{uw}\rvert-1.

We note that in previous works this was known as the
diameter
or
radius
of a cone
[
KO21
,
DD23b
]
. We chose the term “area” here since it aligns with the geometric intuition behind triangulation.

Gromov is the first to use the area of the cone to prove coboundary expansion for abelian groups.
The following theorem which generalizes Gromov’s approach to general groups is by
[
DD23b
]
.

Theorem 5.6

(
[
DD23b
, Lemma 1.6]
)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial complex such that
Aut(X)
is transitive on
k
-faces. Suppose that there exists a cone
\mathcal{A}
with area
R
. Then
X
is a
\frac{1}{\binom{k+1}{3}\cdot R}
-coboundary expander for any coefficient group
\Gamma
.

5.3
Contraction via van Kampen diagrams

In this subsection we prove a version of van Kampen’s lemma for our ‘robust’ context. Previously, we defined the area of a cycle in the cone to be the length of its contraction sequence. The van Kampen lemma reduces the problem of bounding the contraction length of a cycle to counting the number of faces in a planar graph.

The van Kampen diagrams are an essential tool in geometric group theorem
[
Lyn+77
, Chapter V]
. They were introduced by van Kampen in 1933 in the context of the
word problem
in groups defined by presentations
[
Van33
]
, and is a powerful visual tool for exhibiting equivalences of words of some given group presentation. They are also used to bound the number of relations required to prove that a word is equal to identity (such a bound is called the
Dehn function
of a presentation).
The van Kampen diagrams are plane graphs that embed into the presentation complex of a group.
The van Kampen lemma states that a word is equivalent to the identity if and only if it forms a cycle in the presentation complex which can be tiled by faces in a van Kampen diagram. Furthermore the number of inner faces of the diagram provides an upper bound on the relations needed to prove that the word equals to identity.
The advantage of these diagrams is that they bound the length of a sequential process by the size of static objects.

We need a similar tool in the context of abstract complexes, which is a simple generalization of the above setup. Our main motivation for this statement is give a simple and intuitive way to bound the area of cones.
Previous works that use the cones, give sequences of contractions, explaining which triangle goes after another (see e.g.
[
DD23b
]
).
This is sufficient for the proof.
However, in all known examples, the ordering is just a formality, and the proofs were really describing a tiling
(i.e. the contraction was really just a set of faces that formed a disk whose boundary is the cycle we needed to contract). So in this section we give justification to just drawing out the tiling via a van Kampen diagram instead of giving the full sequence of contraction.

A plane graph
H=(V,E)
is a planar graph together with an embedding. We will denote by
H
both the abstract graph and the embedding.

The van Kampen lemma has a few components. First we have our simplicial complex
X
and a cycle
C_{0}
in
X
. In addition, we have a
diagram
. This
diagram
contains:

1.

A plane graph
H=(V,E)
that is
2
-vertex connected.

2.

A labeling
\psi:V\to X(0)
such that for every edge
\{u,v\}\in E
, either
\psi(u)=\psi(v)
, or
\{\psi(u),\psi(v)\}\in X(1)
.

It is easy to see that a walk in
H
is mapped by
\psi
to a walk in
X
(possibly with repeated vertices).

Let
F=\{f_{0},f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}
be the set of faces of
H
with
f_{0}
being the
outer
face. For every face let
\tilde{C}_{i}
be the cycle bounding
f_{i}
, and let
C_{i}=\psi(\tilde{C}_{i})
. To be completely formal one needs to choose a starting point and an orientation to the cycle, but we ignore this point since the choice is not important to our purposes.

(a)

A
2
-dimensional complex
X

(b)

A van Kampen diagram
H,F
of
X

Figure 1
:
A van Kampen diagram for
X,C_{0}

We are now ready to state our van Kampen lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
X,C_{0},H,
and
F
be as above. Assume that for every
i=1,2,\dots,\ell
the cycle
C_{i}
has an
m_{i}
-triangle contraction. Then there exists an
m_{0}
-triangle contraction for
C_{0}
where
m_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}m_{i}
.

A proof of the lemma can be found in
Appendix
G
. Here we give a simply example to demonstrate how to use this lemma.
Consider the
2
-dimensional complex
X
in
Figure
1(a)
with
9
vertices and
10

2
-faces (all triangles in the underlying graph are
2
-faces in
X
). Let
C_{0}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{6},x_{5},x_{7},x_{9},x_{4},x_{1})
be a cycle in
X
.

A van Kampen diagram
H,F
for
X,C_{0}
is given in
Figure
1(b)
.
H
has
9
vertices and
4
faces
F=\{f_{0},f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
. We can easily check that
H
is
2
-vertex connected and has the natural labeling
\psi(v_{i})=x_{i}
for
i\in[9]\setminus\{8\}
and
\psi(v_{8})=x_{4}
. The labeling satisfies that
\psi(\tilde{C}_{i})=C_{i}
for
i=0,1,2,3
.

Furthermore, its clear from the figure that
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
have
2
-triangle,
3
-triangle, and
2
-triangle contractions respectively. So by the van Kampen lemma,
C_{0}
has a
2+3+2=7
-triangle contraction.

5.4
Cone areas and isoperimetric inequalities

In this subsection we explain the connection between cone areas and isoperimetric inequality. In a
2
-dimensional simplicial complex, an isoperimetric inequality is an upper bound on the area of a cycle in terms of its length.
In our context this is the following.

Definition 5.8

(Isoperimetric inequality)
.

Let
X
be a
2
-dimensional simplicial complex and let
R>0
. We say that
X
admits an
R
-isoperimetric inequality if for every cycle
C
with
\ell
edges there exists an
R\ell
-triangle contraction

Area(C)\leqslant R\cdot Len(C).

Isoperimetric inequalities are particularly useful in presentation complexes of Cayley graphs in geometric and combinatorial group theory
[
Lyn+77
]
. Cones are closely related to isoperimetric inequalities as was already observed by
[
Gro10
]
.

We formalize the connection with this proposition.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
X
be a simplicial complex with diameter
D
and let
R>0
. Then

1.

If
X
admits an
R
-isoperimetric inequality, then
X
has a cone whose area is at most
(2D+1)R
.

2.

If
X
has a cone with area
a
then
X
admits an
a
-isoperimetric inequality.

Here the diameter of a complex is the diameter of the underlying graph.

Sketch.

For the first item, we construct a cone as follows. We fix an arbitrary vertex
v_{0}\in X(0)
, and as for the paths
P_{u}
, we take arbitrary shortest paths from
v_{0}
to
u
. Any cycle of the form
P_{u}\circ(u,w)\circ P_{w}^{-1}
(where
\{u,w\}\in X(1)
) has at most
2D+1
edges. We take the smallest contraction of this cycle as
S_{uw}
. By the isoperimetric inequality,
S_{uw}
uses at most
(2D+1)R
triangles.

As for the second item, let
\mathcal{A}
be a cone with area
a
and let
C
be a cycle with
m+1
edges
(w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{m},w_{0})
. Let
P_{0},P_{1},\dots,P_{m}
be the corresponding paths from
v_{0}
to each of the vertices in
w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{m}
. By
Lemma
5.7
, we can contract
C
via the van Kampen diagram in
Figure
2
. We note that the boundary cycle of every inner face in the diagram corresponds to
P_{w_{i}}\circ(w_{i},w_{i+1})\circ P_{w_{i+1}}
(where
i\in\mathbb{Z}/(m+1)
) therefore its area is at most
a
. We deduce the isoperimetric inequality
Area(C)\leqslant a\cdot(m+1)=a\cdot Len(C)
.

Figure 2
:
The second item of
Proposition
5.9

∎

5.5
Substitution and composition

Later in
Section
6.1
, we will reduce the question of bounding the cone area of some complex
X
to bounding that of
X^{\prime}
which contains
X
as a subcomplex. The relevant definitions and auxiliary proposition for this approach are given below.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
X^{\prime}
be any complex and let
X
be a subcomplex of
X^{\prime}
with
X(0)=X^{\prime}(0)
. A substitution is a set
\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{uv}\left|\;\vphantom{P_{uv}uv\in X^{\prime}(1)}\right.uv\in X^{\prime}(1)\right\}
of paths
P_{uv}
from
u
to
v
in
X
such that
P_{uv}=P^{-1}_{vu}
(so it suffices to give one path for each pair of vertices). We say that
\mathcal{P}
is an
m
-substitution if all paths have length at most
m
.

We note that the underlying complexes
X^{\prime},X
of a substitution are implicit in the notation, but they will always be clear in context. Via the substitution, every path in
X^{\prime}
has a corresponding path in
X
:

Definition 5.11
.

Let
X^{\prime},X
be as above and let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{uv}\left|\;\vphantom{P_{uv}uv\in X^{\prime}(1)}\right.uv\in X^{\prime}(1)\right\}
be a substitution. Let
Q=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
be a path in
X^{\prime}
. The composition
\tilde{Q}
is the path

\tilde{Q}=P_{v_{0}v_{1}}\circ P_{v_{1}v_{2}}\dots\circ P_{v_{m-1}v_{m}}\text{ in }X.

The following proposition captures the key property of this substitution.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
X^{\prime}
be any complex and let
X
be a subcomplex of
X^{\prime}
containing all vertices of
X^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a substitution. Assume that for every triangle
t=(u,v,w)\in X^{\prime}(2)
, the composition
\tilde{C_{t}}
of the
3
-cycle
C_{t}=(u,v,w,u)
has an
m
-triangle contraction in
X
. Then for every cycle
C
in
X^{\prime}
that has an
\ell
-triangle contraction in
X^{\prime}
, the composition cycle
\tilde{C}
in
X
has an
\ell m
-triangle contraction .

To prove this proposition, we need the following claim about the contraction property of two cycles with shared vertices.

Claim 5.13
.

Let
C_{s}=P_{1}\circ Q^{-1}
and
C_{t}=P_{2}\circ Q^{-1}
be two cycles in
X
such that
Q
is nonempty. If their difference cycle
C_{\Delta}=P_{1}\circ P_{2}^{-1}
has an
m
-contraction, then there is an
m
-contraction from
C_{s}
to
C_{t}
.

We delay the proof of this claim and prove the proposition assuming this claim.

Proof of
Proposition
5.12
.

Let
S=(C_{0}=C,C_{1},\dots,C_{\ell})
be the contraction in
X^{\prime}
. Let
\tilde{C}_{0},\tilde{C}_{1},\dots,\tilde{C}_{k}
be their compositions in
X
. If
C_{i}\sim_{1}C_{i+1}
via a TR relation
(u,v)\leftrightarrow(u,w,v)
and some BT relations in
X^{\prime}
, by
Claim
5.13

\tilde{C}_{i}\sim_{m}\tilde{C}_{i+1}
via the
m
-triangle contraction of
P_{uv}\circ P_{uw}\circ P_{wv}
in
X
.
Since there are
\ell

(TR)
steps in the contraction of
C
, we have
\tilde{C}\sim_{\ell m}\tilde{C}_{\ell}
. Lastly, since
C_{\ell}
is a point, by definition of composition so is
\tilde{C}_{\ell}
. So the proposition is proven.
∎

Proof of
Claim
5.13
.

We first contract
C_{s}
to the intermediate cycle
P_{1}\circ P_{2}^{-1}\circ P_{2}\circ Q^{-1}=C_{\Delta}\circ C_{t}
via
(BT)
relations. Then we contract
C_{\Delta}
to a point without touching the part
C_{t}
. This procedure gives a
m
-contraction from
C_{s}
to
C_{t}
.
∎

6
Coboundary expansion

Our main result in this section is proving that the Johnson complexes
X_{\varepsilon,n}
and their vertex links are coboundary expanders via
Theorem
5.6
. In addition, in
Section
6.3
, we also show that the vertex links of the complexes constructed by
[
Gol23
]
are coboundary expanders.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
satisfy that
4\,|\,\varepsilon n
. Then the
2
-dimensional Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon}
is a
1
-dimensional
\Omega(\varepsilon)
-coboundary expander.

To show the statement via
Theorem
5.6
, it boils down to show that
Aut(X_{\varepsilon})
is transitive on
2
-faces and that there exists a cone
\mathcal{A}
with area
\Theta(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
.
The first claim is straightforward. The automorphism group
Aut(X_{\varepsilon})
contains all permutations of coordinates and all translations by even-weight vectors. In particular, it is transitive on all triangles. So it is enough to bound the cone area of
X_{\varepsilon}
using the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2
.

The Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon}
has a cone
\mathcal{A}
with area
\Theta(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
.

We note that by
Proposition
5.9
, this immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 6.3
.

The complex
X_{\varepsilon}
admits an
\Theta(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
-isoperimetric inequality.
∎

In addition, we also show that every vertex link of
X_{\varepsilon}
is a coboundary expander.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\frac{1}{4}\geqslant\varepsilon>0
satisfies that
8\,|\,\varepsilon n
. In the
3
-dimensional Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon}
, every vertex link
(X_{\varepsilon})_{v}
is a
1
-dimensional
\frac{1}{85}
-coboundary expander.

Remark 6.5
.

Experts may recall the local-to-global theorem in
[
EK16
]
which states that if a complex is both a good local-spectral expander and its vertex links are
1
-dim coboundary expanders then the complex is also a
1
-dim coboundary expander. So one may suspect that the coboundary expansion theorem
Theorem
6.1
can be derived from coboundary expansion of links (
Theorem
6.4
) and local-spectral expansion of
X_{\varepsilon}
(
Lemma
4.4
). However, this approach fails since the local-spectral expansion of
X_{\varepsilon}
is not good enough for applying the local-to-global theorem. So instead, we prove coboundary expansion of
X_{\varepsilon}
by directly using the cone method.

By link isomorphism, we can focus on the link of
\mathbf{0}
without loss of generality. Then the automorphism group
Aut(X_{\mathbf{0}})
contains all permutations of coordinates and so is transitive over
X_{\mathbf{0}}(2)
(
Lemma
4.5
). By
Theorem
5.6
it is enough to show that there exists a cone with area
85
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
0<\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
. Any vertex link
(X_{\varepsilon})_{v}
has a cone with area at most
85
.

As above, by
Proposition
5.9
, this implies:

Corollary 6.7
.

A link of a vertex
v\in X_{\varepsilon}
admits an
85
-isoperimetric inequality.
∎

6.1
Constructing a bounded area cone for
X_{\varepsilon}

In this subsection we give a proof of
Lemma
6.2
. We take a two-step approach to showing that
X_{\varepsilon}
has a cone of bounded area.
First we define a related complex
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
which contains
X_{\varepsilon}
as a subcomplex, and construct a cone for
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with bounded area. Next, we replace every edge in
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
with a path in
X_{\varepsilon}
between the same two endpoints. Then effectively the edges forming a triangle in
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
become a cycle in
X_{\varepsilon}
. The second step is then to find a bounded-size tiling for every such cycle inside
X_{\varepsilon}
.

The key proposition used in this approach is as follows.

Proposition 6.8

(Restatement of
Proposition
5.12
)
.

Let
X^{\prime}
be any complex and let
X
be a subcomplex of
X^{\prime}
containing all vertices. Let
\mathcal{P}
be a substitution. Assume that for every triangle
t=\{u,v,w\}\in X^{\prime}(2)
, the composition
\tilde{C_{t}}
of the
3
-cycle
C_{t}=(u,v,w)
has an
m
-triangle contraction in
X
.
Then for every cycle
C
in
X^{\prime}
that has an
\ell
-triangle contraction in
X^{\prime}
, the composition cycle
\tilde{C}
in
X
has an
\ell m
-triangle contraction.

This proposition implies that if
for the boundary cycle
C_{t}
of every triangle
t\in X^{\prime}(2)
, its composition
\tilde{C_{t}}
has a
\Theta(1)
-triangle contraction in
X
, then the cone area of
X
is asymptotically the same as that of
X^{\prime}
.
This lemma suggests a two-step strategy towards showing
Lemma
6.2
. First we construct a
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
which has a cone of area
\Theta(1/\varepsilon)
(
Claim
6.9
is proved in
Section
6.1.2
), and then we show that the boundary of
2
-faces in
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has
\Theta(1)
-contraction in
X_{\varepsilon}
(
Claim
6.10
is proved in
Section
6.1.3
).

Claim 6.9
.

X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has a cone of area
\leqslant 16/\varepsilon
.

Claim 6.10
.

For every triangle
t=\{u,v,w\}\in X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(2)
whose corresponding boundary cycle is
C_{t}=(u,v,w,u)
, we can contract the composition
\tilde{C}_{t}
in
X_{\varepsilon}
using
O(1)
triangles.

Postponing the proofs of these lemma and claims till later subsections, we are ready to derive
Lemma
6.2
.

Proof of
Lemma
6.2
.

Claim
6.9
implies that the complex
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has cone area
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
, while
Claim
6.10
implies that for every triangle boundary
C
in
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
, its composition
\tilde{C}
has a
O(1)
-triangle contraction in
X_{\varepsilon}
. By
Lemma
6.8
we conclude that he cone area of
X_{\varepsilon}
is
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
.
∎

Before moving on, we consider two “patterns” of contractions shown in
Figure
3(a)
and
Figure
3(b)
. They are going to be used extensively in the later proofs.

Definition 6.11

(Middle vertex contraction.)
.

A cycle
C
has a middle vertex contraction in a simplicial complex
X
if there is a vertex
z
so that for every edge
\{u,w\}\in C
,
\{z,u,w\}\in X(2)
.

Claim 6.12

(Middle vertex pattern)
.

Let
C
be a length
m
cycle in a simplicial complex
X
. If
C
has a middle vertex contraction, then
C
has an
m
-triangle contraction.

Proof.

Given such a cycle
C
and a center vertex
z
, we can construct a van-Kampen diagram
(H,\psi)
for the triangles between
z
and edges in
C
, where
H
is the plane graph given by
Figure
3(a)
and
\psi
is the identity map. Note that
H
has
m
inner faces each corresponding to a triangle and an outer face whose boundary is
C
. Then by
Lemma
5.7
,
C
has an
m
-triangle contraction.
∎

Definition 6.13

(Middle path contraction.)
.

A
4
-cycle
C=(v,u,w,u^{\prime})
has a length
m
middle path contraction in a simplicial complex
X
if there is a length
m
walk
(u=u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m}=u^{\prime})
so that for every edge
\{u_{i},u_{i+1}\}
, the triangles
\{u_{i},u_{i+1},v\},\{u_{i},u_{i+1},w\}\in X(2)
.

Claim 6.14

(Middle path pattern)
.

Let
C=(v,u,w,u^{\prime})
be a
4
-cycle in a simplicial complex
X
. If
C
has a length
m
middle path contraction, then
C
has a
2m
-contraction.

Proof.

We again construct a van-Kampen diagram
(H,\psi)
for the set of triangles
\{u_{i},u_{i+1},v\},\{u_{i},u_{i+1},w\}\in X(2)
, where
H
is the plane graph given by
Figure
3(b)
and
\psi
is the identity map. Note that
H
has
2m
inner faces each corresponding to a triangle and an outer face whose boundary is
C
. Then by
Lemma
5.7
,
C
has an
2m
-triangle contraction.
∎

(a)

Middle vertex contraction

(b)

Middle path contraction

Figure 3
:
Two contraction patterns

6.1.1
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
and its cone
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

In this part, we construct a
2
-dimensional simplicial complex
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
such that
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has a
2
-substitution in
X_{\varepsilon}
.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\left|\;\vphantom{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}wt(x)\leqslant\varepsilon n,wt(x)\text{ is even}}\right.wt(x)\leqslant\varepsilon n,wt(x)\text{ is even}\right\}
. The
2
-dimensional complex
X^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
is given by

X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(0)=X(0),

X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1)=\left\{\{x,x+s\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x,x+s\}x\in X(0),s\in S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}}\right.x\in X(0),s\in S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}\right\},

X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(2)=\left\{\{x,x+s_{1},x+s_{2}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{x,x+s_{1},x+s_{2}\}x\in X(0),s_{1},s_{2},s_{1}+s_{2}\in S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}}\right.x\in X(0),s_{1},s_{2},s_{1}+s_{2}\in S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}\right\}.

Claim 6.16
.

X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
has a
2
-substitution in
X_{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

For any edge
(x,x+s)\in X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
(where
s\in S_{\leqslant\varepsilon}
), find
s_{1},s_{2}\in S_{\varepsilon}
such that
s=s_{1}+s_{2}
. Such
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
can be constructed as follows: first split
s=t_{1}+t_{2}
into two vectors of equal weight. Then take any vector
r
of weight
\varepsilon n-\frac{|s|}{2}
with disjoint support from
s
, and set
s_{i}=r+t_{i}
. Now by setting
P_{x,x+s}=(x,x+s_{1},x+s_{1}+s_{2}=x+s)
, we obtain a
2
-substitution for
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1)
in
X_{\varepsilon}
.
∎

To bound the cone area of
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
, we start by constructing a cone
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for
X_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
. In the rest of the section we drop the subscript
\varepsilon
and simply write
X
and
X^{\prime}
. Now we introduce a few definitions in order to describe
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
concisely.

Definition 6.17

(Monotone path)
.

A path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m})
in
X^{\prime}
is called
monotone
if
v_{i}\subseteq v_{i+1}
, where we identify the string with the set of coordinates that are equal to
1
.

Analogously we can define monotone cycles.

Definition 6.18

(Monotone cycle)
.

A cycle
C
is monotone if it can be decomposed to
C=P_{1}\circ P_{2}^{-1}
where
P_{1},P_{2}
are monotone paths of
equal
length.

Definition 6.19

(Lexicographic path)
.

Fix an ordering on the coordinates of vectors in
X(0)
. A path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m})
in
X^{\prime}
is called
lexicographic
if the path is monotone
and
every element in
v_{i+1}\setminus v_{i}
is larger than all elements in
v_{i}
under the given ordering.

We also define half-step lexicographic path.

Definition 6.20

(Half-step lexicographic path)
.

A path
P=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m})
in
X^{\prime}
is called a half-step lexicographic path if it is a lexicographic path from
v_{0}
