to
v_{m}
such that all but the last edge
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}
in the path satisfy that
\lvert v_{i+1}\setminus v_{i}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2}
, while the last edge satisfies
\lvert v_{m}\setminus v_{m-1}\rvert\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon n}{2}
.

Note that if
v_{0}
and
v_{m}
are connected then there is a unique half-step lexicographic path between them.

Analogously we can define half-step lexicographic cycles.

Definition 6.21

(Half-step lexicographic cycle)
.

A cycle
C
in
X^{\prime}
is half-step lexicographic if it can be decomposed to
C=P_{1}\circ(x,a)\circ(a,y)\circ P_{2}^{-1}
where
P_{1},P_{2}
are half-step lexicographic paths of
equal
length, and that
a\supseteq x\cup y
.

This is saying that in the half-step lexicographic cycle
C
, every edge
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}
in
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
satisfies that
\lvert v_{i+1}\setminus v_{i}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon n}{2}
, while the endpoints of the two paths satisfy that
\lvert a\setminus x\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
and
\lvert a\setminus y\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
.

We consider the following cone
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
:

Definition 6.22

(Cone of
X^{\prime}
)
.

Fix an ordering on the coordinates of vectors in
X(0)
. Set the base vertex
v_{0}=0
. For every
v\in X(0)\setminus\{v_{0}\}
we take
P_{v}
to be the half-step lexicographic path from
v_{0}
to
v
in
X^{\prime}
.

6.1.2
Bounding the area of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
: proof of
Claim
6.9

We prove that for every
uv\in X^{\prime}(1)
, the cycle
C=P_{u}\circ uv\circ P_{v}
has a
\Theta(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
-triangle contraction.
The strategy is to decompose
C
into a set of cycles in
X^{\prime}
and compose these cycles’ contractions together to obtain a contraction for
C
.
The cycle decomposition is done by a recursive use of
Lemma
5.7
.

In the rest of the section, we will use both
+,-
and set operations
\setminus,\cup,\cap,\overline{\phantom{v}}
(exclusion, union, intersection, and complement) over boolean strings in
X^{\prime}(0)\subset\{0,1\}^{n}
.
+,-
are entry-wise XOR for the boolean strings, while set operations are applied to the support of a string (i.e. the set of nonzero entries in the string).

The base case in the recursive argument is a monotone four-cycle, and we first show that all such cycles have
\Theta(1)
-contraction.

Claim 6.23
.

Let
C=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},v_{1}^{\prime},v_{0})
be any monotone four-cycle in
X^{\prime}
. Then
C
has a
\leqslant 4
-triangle contraction.

Proof.

By definition of monotone cycles,
v_{2}
has size
\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
and that
v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq v_{2}
. If
\lvert v_{2}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
we can contract
C
since
\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{0},v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}\}\in X^{\prime}(2)
.

Otherwise
\varepsilon n<\lvert v_{2}\rvert\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
, we intend to show that
C
has a length
\leqslant 2
middle path contraction. For these vertices we need to find a path between
v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}
so that for every edge
e
in that path
e\cup\{v_{0}\},e\cup\{v_{2}\}\in X^{\prime}(2)
. Let
a=v_{1}+v_{1}^{\prime}
. We split to cases:

1.

If
\lvert a\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
then we just take the path to be
(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime})
.

2.

Otherwise
\varepsilon n<\lvert a\rvert\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
. Then we split
v_{1}\setminus v_{1}^{\prime}=b_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}b_{2}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}\setminus v_{1}=c_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}c_{2}
both into equal-size parts as illustrated in
Figure
4
. So both
a_{1}=b_{1}+c_{1}
and
a_{2}=b_{2}+c_{2}
have size at most
\varepsilon n
. We take the path
(v_{1},v_{1}+a_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}=v_{1}+a_{1}+a_{2})
. Since the middle vertex
v_{1}+a_{1}
is contained in
v_{2}
and has size between that of
v_{1}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}
, it is connected to
v_{2}
and to
0
.

By
Claim
6.14
there is a
\leqslant 4
-triangle contraction of the cycle.
∎

Figure 4
:
Partition of
v_{1}\cup v_{1}^{\prime}
in the proof of
Claim
6.23

The general statement for all half-step lexicographic cycles is as follows.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
m\geqslant 2
and let
C=(0,b_{1},\dots,b_{m},a,c_{m},\dots,c_{1},0)
be a half-step lexicographic cycle of length
2(m+1)
. Then
C
has an
(8m-2)
-contraction.

Figure 5
:
Half-step lexicographic cycle decomposition in
Lemma
6.24

Proof.

We shall decompose
C
into
2m-1
monotone four-cycle and
2
triangles in
X^{\prime}
as illustrated in
Figure
5
. Then applying
Claim
6.23
, we deduce that
C
has a
4\cdot(2m-1)+2=8m-2
-contraction. To describe the decomposition, we define auxiliary vertices
a_{i}=b_{i}\cup c_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,m
and
a_{m+1}=a
. We next argue that
a_{i}
is connected to
b_{i},c_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
in
X^{\prime}
.

First to show that for all
i\in[m]

a_{i}
is connected to
b_{i}
and
c_{i}
, note that
\lvert b_{i}\rvert=\lvert c_{i}\rvert=i\cdot\varepsilon n/2
. Thus if
\lvert b_{i}+c_{i}\rvert\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
, then
\lvert b_{i}\setminus c_{i}\rvert=\lvert c_{i}\setminus b_{i}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
and
a_{i}=b_{i}\cup c_{i}
would be connected to both
b_{i}
and
c_{i}
. To show
\lvert b_{i}+c_{i}\rvert\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
, we observe that
\lvert c_{m}\setminus b_{m}\rvert\leqslant\lvert a\setminus b_{m}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
and analogously
\lvert b_{m}\setminus c_{m}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
. Now, assume for contradiction that for some
i<m
,
\lvert b_{i}+c_{i}\rvert>2\varepsilon n
. Similarly we deduce that
\lvert c_{i}\setminus b_{i}\rvert,\lvert b_{i}\setminus c_{i}\rvert>\varepsilon n
. Suppose without loss of generality the largest element in
a_{i}
comes from
b_{i}
then
c_{i}\setminus b_{i}
is a subset of
c_{m}\setminus b_{m}
because any element added to
b_{m}
after the
i
-th step is larger than all elements in
c_{i}
. Thus
\lvert c_{m}\setminus b_{m}\rvert>\varepsilon n
. We arrive at a contradiction. So indeed
\lvert b_{i}+c_{i}\rvert\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
, and
b_{i}
and
c_{i}
are both connected to
a_{i}
for
i\in[m]
.

To show that for all
i\in[m-1]

a_{i}
is connected to
a_{i+1}
, note that
a_{i+1}\supseteq a_{i}
and
\lvert a_{i+1}\setminus a_{i}\rvert\leqslant\lvert b_{i+1}\setminus b_{i}\rvert+\lvert c_{i+1}\setminus c_{i}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
.
To see that
a_{m}
is connected to
a
, note that
a\supseteq a_{m}\supseteq b_{m}
and so
\lvert a\setminus a_{m}\rvert\leqslant\lvert a\setminus b_{m}\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
.

Let
H
be the plane graph in
Figure
5
whose vertices are
\{0,a_{1},\dots,a_{m},b_{1},\dots,b_{m},c_{1},\dots,c_{m}\}
. By construction
H
is a van-Kampen diagram for
X^{\prime}
with
2m+2
inner faces.

Furthermore, we note that the cycle boundaries
C_{0}=(0,b_{1},a_{1},c_{1},0)
,
C_{b,i}=(b_{i},b_{i+1},a_{i+1},a_{i},b_{i})
and
C_{c,i}=(c_{i},c_{i+1},a_{i+1},a_{i},c_{i})
for
i\in[m-1]
are all monotone four cycles.
C_{b,m}=(b_{m},a,a_{m},b_{m})
and
C_{c,m}=(c_{m},a,a_{m},c_{m})
are two triangles. Applying
Lemma
5.7
we deduce that
C
has a
4\cdot(1+2(m-1))+2=8m-2
-triangle contraction. Thus we conclude the proof.

∎

(a)

C^{\prime}
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{2}

(b)

Minimal monotone cycle
C_{m}

Figure 6
:
Van-Kampen diagrams used in the proof of
Claim
6.9

Proof of
Claim
6.9
.

Let
\{u,v\}
be an edge in
X^{\prime}(1)
and
C_{uv}=P_{u}\circ(u,v)\circ P_{v}^{-1}
be the decoding cycle. Since
\{u,v\}\in X^{\prime}(1)
it holds that
\lvert u+v\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
. Thereby
\lvert u+v\rvert=\lvert u\cup v\rvert-\lvert u\cap v\rvert\geqslant\lvert u\cup v\rvert-\lvert v\rvert
and similarly
\lvert u+v\rvert\geqslant\lvert u\cup v\rvert-\lvert u\rvert
. Thus the triangle
\{v,u,u\cup v\}
is in
X^{\prime}(2)
.
Define the cycle
C^{\prime}=P_{u}\circ(u,u\cup v,v)\circ P_{v}^{-1}
, and the monotone paths
P_{1}=P_{u}\circ(u,u\cup v)
and
P_{2}=P_{v}\circ(v,v\cup u)
(and
C^{\prime}=P_{1}\circ P_{2}^{-1}
).
If both paths have equal length, then we can contract the cycle
C^{\prime}
using
(16/\varepsilon-2)
-triangles by
Lemma
6.24
as follows. We begin by constructing the following van-Kampen diagram
Figure
6(a)
to decompose
C_{uv}
into
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}=(u,u\cup v,v,u)
: let
H
be the graph with vertices
V(P_{u})\cup V(P_{v})\cup\{u,v,u\cup v\}
and edges
E(P_{u})\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}E(P_{v})\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{\{u,v\},\{u,u\cup v\},\{v,u\cup v\}\}
, where
V(P)
denote
P
’s vertex set and
E(P)
its edge set. By definition, the plane graph
H
given in
Figure
6(a)
is a van-Kampen diagram with
3
plane graph faces.

H
embeds into
X^{\prime}
as illustrated by
Figure
6(a)
. In particular, the edge boundaries of the
3
faces in
H
are mapped to
C_{uv},C^{\prime}
, and
C^{\prime\prime}
in
X^{\prime}
. Therefore by
Lemma
5.7
,
C_{uv}
has a
(16/\varepsilon-1)
contraction.

If
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are of different length, we do as follows (
Figure
6(b)
). Assume that
P_{1}
is longer. Since
\lvert u+v\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
, the length of
P_{u}
and the length of
P_{v}
differ by at most
2
. By construction, the prefix
P_{u}^{\prime}
that is obtained by removing the last
\lvert P_{u}\rvert-\lvert P_{v}\rvert
vertices and ends with a vertex
x
has
\lvert x\rvert\geqslant\lvert v\rvert
. Thus
\lvert u\cup v\setminus x\rvert\leqslant\lvert u\cup v\setminus v\rvert\leqslant\varepsilon n
. In this case, the triangle
\{x,u,v\cup u\}\in X^{\prime}(2)
can be used to decompose
C_{uv}
. Denote the monotone cycle
C_{m}=P_{u}^{\prime}\circ(x,u\cup v)\circ(u\cup v,y)\circ{P_{v}}^{-1}
. Again by
Lemma
6.24
,
C_{m}
has a
8\cdot(2/\varepsilon-1)-2
-triangle contraction.
Now we add
(x,u\cup v)
(and also
(x,u)
if it is not in
P_{u}
) to the graph
H
. Now the van-Kampen diagram
(H,Id)
has
3
faces (or
4
if
(x,u)
is not in
P_{u}
) whose face boundaries are mapped to
C_{uv},C_{m},(x,u,u\cup v,x)
(and potentially
(x,x^{\prime},u,x)
). Since the last
1
(or
2
) cycle has a
1
-triangle contraction,
C_{uv}
has a
8\cdot(2/\varepsilon-1)
-triangle contraction by
Lemma
5.7
.

∎

6.1.3
Contracting
X^{\prime}(2)
with
X
: proof of
Claim
6.10

We move back to consider the Johnson complex
X
which is a subcomplex of
X^{\prime}
.

Claim 6.25
.

Let
C=(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},v_{1}^{\prime},v_{0})
be
any
four-cycle in
X
. Then
C
has an
8
-triangle contraction.

Note that in contrast to
Claim
6.23
we do not assume monotonicity.

Proof.

Without loss of generality
v_{0}=0
. In this case
v_{2}
has
\lvert v_{2}\rvert=t
for some even
2\leqslant t\leqslant 2\varepsilon n
.
We intend to find a path between
v_{1}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}
and then apply the middle path contraction from
Claim
6.14
. For this we define the following graph
G=(V,E)
.

V=N(0)\cap N(v_{2})

E=\left\{\{u,w\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{u,w\}\{u,w,0\},\{u,w,v_{2}\}\in X(2)}\right.\{u,w,0\},\{u,w,v_{2}\}\in X(2)\right\}.

We note that
v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}\in V
, and that if there is a length-
\ell
path from
v_{1}
to
v_{1}^{\prime}
in this graph, then the four-cycle has a
2\ell
-triangle contraction by
Claim
6.14
.

To find such paths, we define two functions mapping tensor products of Johnson graphs to
G
.

\psi_{c}:G_{c}:=J(t,\frac{t}{2},\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil)\otimes J(n-t,\varepsilon n-\frac{t}{2},\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil)\to G,

\psi_{f}:G_{f}:=J(t,\frac{t}{2},\lfloor\frac{t}{4}\rfloor)\otimes J(n-t,\varepsilon n-\frac{t}{2},\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-\lfloor\frac{t}{4}\rfloor)\to G,

where every vertex
(u,w)
in the two tensor graphs where
u\in\binom{v_{2}}{t/2}
and
w\in\binom{[n]\setminus v_{2}}{\varepsilon n-t/2}
is mapped to
\psi_{c}((u,w))=\psi_{f}((u,w))=u\cup w
. Here we implicitly identify the
t
elements in
J(t,\frac{t}{2},*)
with the set
v_{2}
, and the
n-t
elements in
J(n-t,\varepsilon n-\frac{t}{2},*)
with
[n]\setminus v_{2}
.
Note that the two tensor product graphs have the same vertex space.
Furthermore when
\frac{t}{4}
is an integer, the two functions are identical.

We first show that the functions give two bijections between the vertex sets: for any distinct vertices
(u,w),(u^{\prime},w^{\prime})
in the tensor product graphs, the unions
u\cup w
and
u^{\prime}\cup w^{\prime}
are also distinct in
V
. Furthermore, for any
v\in V
,
v
and
v_{2}-v
both have weight
\varepsilon n
. This implies that
\lvert v\cap v_{2}\rvert=\frac{t}{2}
and
\lvert v\setminus v_{2}\rvert=\varepsilon n-\frac{t}{2}
. Therefore
v
has the preimage
(v\cap v_{2},v\setminus v_{2})
under
\psi_{c}
.
Vertex bijectivity follows.

We next show that
\psi_{c}
is actually a graph homomorphism. That is to say every edge in
G_{c}
is mapped to an edge in
G
.
For every edge
\{(u_{1},w_{1}),(u_{2},w_{2})\}
in
G_{c}
, we have
\lvert u_{1}\cap u_{2}\rvert=\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil
and
\lvert w_{1}\cap w_{2}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil
. Subsequently

\psi_{c}((u_{1},w_{1}))+\psi_{c}((u_{2},w_{2}))=(u_{1}\cup w_{1})+(u_{2}\cup w_{2})=(u_{1}+u_{2})\cup(w_{1}+w_{2})

has weight
2(\frac{t}{2}-\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil)+2(\varepsilon n-\frac{t}{2}-(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-\lceil\frac{t}{4}\rceil))\varepsilon n=\varepsilon n
. Therefore
\{\psi_{c}((u_{1},w_{1})),\psi_{c}((u_{2},w_{2})\}
is an edge in
G
. Thus
\psi_{c}
is a vertex bijective graph homomorphism. A similar argument shows that the same is true for
\psi_{f}
.

We next use the following claim to find length-
4
paths between any pair of vertices in
G_{c}\cup G_{f}
and then transfer this property to
G
via
\psi_{c},\psi_{f}
.

Claim 6.26
.

Let
G_{c}=(V_{c},E_{c})
and
G_{f}=(V_{f}=V_{c},E_{f})
be defined as above.
For any two vertices
v_{1}=(u,w)
and
\beta=(u^{\prime},w^{\prime})
in
V
such that
u\neq u^{\prime}
and
w\neq w^{\prime}
, there is a length two path
(v_{1},(x,y),\beta)
in
G_{cf}=(V_{c},E_{c}\cup E_{f})
such that
\{v_{1},(x,y)\}\in E_{c}
and
\{(x,y),\beta\}\in E_{f}
.

The claim immediately implies that for any pair of vertices
v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}
in the union graph
G_{cf}
, we can find an intermediate vertex
\beta
such that the two components in
\beta
are distinct from the components in
v_{1}
and
v_{1}^{\prime}
, and then find a length-
4
path of the form
(v_{1},\alpha,\beta,\alpha^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime})
in
G_{cf}
.

We postpone the proof of the claim and conclude that using the vertex bijective graph homomorphisms
\psi_{c}
and
\psi_{f}
, such paths also exist in
G
. Therefore there exists a length-
4
path between any two vertices
v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime}
in
G
. So the four-cycle has an
8
-triangle contraction.
∎

Proof of

.

When
\frac{t}{4}

. The existence of the intermediate vertex

. Then set

.

\displaystyle v\setminus w

\displaystyle=l_{a}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}l_{c}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s_{l}

\displaystyle=m_{a}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}m_{c}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}s_{m}

\displaystyle w\setminus v

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }\begin{cases}\lvert r_{a}\rvert=\lfloor\lvert w\setminus v\rvert/2\rfloor\\

.

is well defined. Define

. So

and

are all connected to

therefore the cycles

6.2
Cone diameter of the vertex links: proof of
Lemma
6.6

Recall that the links of every vertex in the complex are isomorphic to one another. Therefore it is enough to prove the lemma for the link of
0
. By
Lemma
4.5
, the underlying graph of the link complex is
J(n,\varepsilon n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n)
, and the
2
-faces are

X_{0}(2)=\left\{\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\}\{v_{0},v_{1}\},\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{0},v_{2}\}\in X_{0}(1)\text{ and }\lvert v_{0}\cap v_{1}\cap v_{2}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon}{4}n}\right.\{v_{0},v_{1}\},\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{0},v_{2}\}\in X_{0}(1)\text{ and }\lvert v_{0}\cap v_{1}\cap v_{2}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon}{4}n\right\}.

Let us begin with the following claim.

Claim 6.27
.

Let
C=(v,u,w,u^{\prime},v)
be a
4
-cycle in
J(n,\varepsilon n,\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n)

such that
v\cap w=\emptyset
, then

has a contraction using

triangles.

Proof.

We intend to use the middle path pattern from
Claim
6.14
. So let us show that there exists a path
(u,z,u^{\prime})
such that both edges in the path form triangles with
v
and with
w
.
To find
z
, we find separately
z_{1}=z\cap v
and
z_{2}=z\cap w
and set
z=z_{1}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}z_{2}
. To find
z_{1}
we set
x_{1}=u\cap v,x_{2}=u^{\prime}\cap v
and note that both of these have size
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n
while
v
has size
\varepsilon n
. We note that
\lvert x_{1}\setminus x_{2}\rvert=\lvert x_{2}\setminus x_{1}\rvert
and we take
z_{1}
by taking
t=\min\{\lvert x_{1}\setminus x_{2}\rvert,\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\}
coordinates from each of
x_{1}\setminus x_{2},x_{2}\setminus x_{1}
, an additional
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}n-t
coordinates from each of
x_{1}\cap x_{2},v\setminus(x_{1}\cup x_{2})
. This is possible since:

1.

\lvert x_{1}\cap x_{2}\rvert=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-t
and

2.

\lvert v\setminus(x_{1}\cup x_{2})\rvert\geqslant\varepsilon n-\lvert x_{1}\cup x_{2}\rvert=\varepsilon n-\lvert x_{2}\rvert-\lvert x_{1}\setminus x_{2}\rvert\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{2}n-t
.

Find
z_{2}
analogously in
w
. Then by construction
\{u,v,z\},\{u^{\prime},v,z\},\{u,w,z\},\{u^{\prime},w,z\}
are all in

. So

implies that

has a
4
-triangle contraction.
∎

6.3
Coboundary expansion of links in the complexes of
[
Gol23
]

Even though most of this
Section
6
is devoted to the Johnson complex, our techniques also extend to the complexes constructed by
[
Gol23
]
that were described in
Section
3
. In this section we prove that the link of every vertex in these complexes are coboundary expanders.

Lemma 6.29
.

Let
Y=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})^{\leqslant d})
for
n>2d
. Let
X=\beta(Y)
be the basification of
Y
. Then
X
has a cone with area
459
and is therefore a
1
-dimensional
\frac{1}{459}
-coboundary expander.

The proof of this lemma is just a more complicated version of the proof of
Lemma
6.6
.
Before proving the lemma, we give an explicit description of the link structure. The vertices are
X(0)=\mathcal{M}(\frac{d}{2})
, i.e. all matrices of a certain rank
\frac{d}{2}
. The edges are

X(1)=\left\{\{A_{1},A_{2}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{A_{1},A_{2}\}\exists B\in\mathcal{M}(\frac{d}{4}),\;B\oplus(A_{1}-B)\oplus(A_{2}-B)}\right.\exists B\in\mathcal{M}(\frac{d}{4}),\;B\oplus(A_{1}-B)\oplus(A_{2}-B)\right\}.

Finally, the triangles
X(2)
are all
\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\}
such that there exist
B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{7}\in\mathcal{M}(\frac{d}{8})
such that the sum
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{7}B_{j}
is direct, and

A_{1}=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{3}\oplus B_{4},

A_{2}=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{5}\oplus B_{6},

A_{3}=B_{1}\oplus B_{3}\oplus B_{5}\oplus B_{7}.

This description is a direct consequence of the definition of admissible functions in
Section
3
.

We follow the footsteps of the proof of
Lemma
6.6
. Towards this we need two claims. The first proves that there exist length
4
paths between any two vertices in
X
, so that we can construct a cone where the cycles have constant length. This was immediate in the Johnson case but needs to be argued here. This claim also constructs length-
2
paths between any two matrices that form a direct sum. we will need this later on.

Claim 6.30
.

1.

For any two matrices
A,B\in X(0)
there is a path of length
4
between
A
and
B
.

2.

If
A\oplus B
then there is a path
(A,C,B)
. Moreover, there exists such a path where the middle vertex
C=A_{1}\oplus B_{1}
such that the components of this sum have equal rank and satisfy
A_{1}\leqslant A
and
B_{1}\leqslant B
18
18

18

Not all paths from
A
to
B
have this property. For instance, let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}
be four independent vectors. Let
A=e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{2}
,
C=e_{2}\otimes e_{2}+e_{3}\otimes e_{3}
and
B=(e_{2}+e_{3})\otimes e_{3}+e_{4}\otimes e_{4}
. One can verify that because
C=(e_{2}+e_{3})\otimes e_{3}+e_{2}\otimes(e_{2}+e_{3})
then
(A,C,D)
is indeed a path. However, we can directly check that there are no
A_{1}\leqslant A,B_{1}\leqslant B
such that
C=A_{1}\oplus B_{1}
.
.

The second claim is a a variant of
Claim
6.27
.
Claim
6.27
bounds
4
-cycles in the Johnson complex provided that two non-neighbor vertices in the cycle are disjoint. This claim is the analog for
X
:

Claim 6.31
.

Let
(Z,C,A,D,Z)
be a
4
-cycle with the following properties.

1.

The matrices
Z\oplus A
are a direct sum.

2.

We can decompose
C=C_{1}\oplus C_{2}
and
D=D_{1}\oplus D_{2}
such that
C_{1},D_{1}\leqslant Z
and
C_{2},D_{2}\leqslant A
.
19
19

19

We note that there is no analogue to second property of the cycle in the statement
Claim
6.27
. The analogue is that
C=(C_{1}\cap A)\cup(C\cap Z)
(and similarly for
D
). This follows from the first property in the Johnson case but we require it explicitly in this case.

Then the cycle has a tiling with
O(1)
-triangles.

Both of these claims are proven after the lemma itself.

Proof of
Lemma
6.29
.

It is easy to see that the complex
X
has a transitive action on the triangles. Therefore by
Theorem
5.6
, it suffices to find a cone whose area is
459
to prove coboundary expansion.

Let
M_{0}
be an arbitrary matrix we take as the root of our cone. We take arbitrary length
4
paths
P_{A}
from
M_{0}
to every matrix
A\in X(0)
. This is possible by
Claim
6.30
.

For every edge
\{A,A^{\prime}\}
, we need to find a contraction for the cycle
P_{A}\circ(A,A^{\prime})\circ P_{A^{\prime}}^{-1}
. We note that this cycle has length
9
. Therefore it suffices to show that every
9
cycle
(M_{0},A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{8},M_{0})
has a tiling with
459
triangles. We begin by reducing the problem into tilings of
5
cycles instead. Indeed, observe that for every edge
\{A_{i},A_{i+1}\}
in the cycle,
\mathrm{dim}(\row(A_{i})+\row(A_{i+1}))=\mathrm{dim}(\col(A_{i})+\col(A_{i+1}))=\frac{3d}{4}
. Therefore there exists some
Z\in X(0)
such that
\row(Z)
and
\col(Z)
are a direct sum with
\row(A_{i})+\row(A_{i+1})
and
\col(A_{i})+\col(A_{i+1})
respectively (In fact, a random matrix
Z
will satisfy this with high probability for all edges in the cycle because
n\geqslant 2d
).

(a)

From a
9
-cycle to
5
-cycles

(b)

Middle vertex
D

Figure 11
:
Decomposition of the cycles

By
Claim
6.30
there exists a length two path from
Z
to every vertex
A_{i}
in the cycle as in
Figure
11(a)
. Therefore, by drawing these paths we can decompose our cycle into
9
cycles of length
5
of the form
(Z,C_{i},A_{i},A_{i+1},C_{i+1},Z)
such that the row/column spaces of
Z
are a direct sum with
\row(A_{1})+\row(A_{2})
and
\col(A_{1})+\col(A_{2})
. This is a similar step as the one we do in
Figure
9(a)
in the Johnson case.

Fix a cycle as above let us denote by
B
the matrix such that
B\oplus(A_{1}-B)\oplus(A_{2}-B)
. Let
Z_{1},Z_{2}
be of equal rank such that
Z=Z_{1}\oplus Z_{2}
. Denote by
D=Z_{1}\oplus B
. We observe that
\{Z,D\},\{A_{1},D\}
and
\{A_{2},D\}
are all edges and therefore we can decompose this cycle into a
3
-cycle
(D,A_{1},A_{2},D)
and two
4
-cycles
(Z,C_{i},A_{i},D,Z)
that we need to tile (see
Figure
11(b)
).

The tiling of
(D,A_{1},A_{2},D)
is simple. We decompose
Z_{1},B,A_{1}-B
and
A_{2}-B
into a direct sum of matrices of equal rank
Z_{1}=K_{1}\oplus K_{2},B=L_{1}\oplus L_{2},A_{1}-B=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
and
A_{2}-B=N_{1}\oplus N_{2}
. Then the matrix
E=K_{1}\oplus L_{1}\oplus M_{1}\oplus N_{1}
is connected in a triangle with all three edges. so we can triangulate this three cycle using three triangles.

As for the cycles
(Z,C_{i},A_{i},D,Z)
, we tile them with the claim
Claim
6.31
using
24
-triangles. Thus we can triangulate every
5
-cycle using of
51
triangles. As every
9
-cycle is decomposed into
9
such
5
-cycles we get a total of
459
triangles, so the cone area is
459
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
6.30
.

First note that the first statement follows from the second, because for any
A,B
there exists some
D\in X(0)
such that
A\oplus D
and
B\oplus D
(this is because of the assumption that
n>2d
). Thus we can take a
2
-path from
A
to
D
and then another
2
-path from
D
to
B
.

Let us thus show that assuming that
A\oplus B
there is a
2
-path from
A
to
B
. Decompose
A=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
of equal rank, and similarly for
B=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
. Let
C=A_{1}\oplus B_{1}
and take the path
(A,C,B)
. The claim follows.
∎

Up until now, this was largely similar to
Lemma
6.6
. Surprisingly, the proof of
Claim
6.31
is more complicated then its Johnson analogue
Claim
6.27
. This stems from the fact the matrix domination poset is different from the matrix Johnson Grassmann poset, and in particular there isn’t a perfect analogue to set intersection in the matrix poset (this is called a meet in poset theory).

Proof of
Claim
6.31
.

We will use the middle path pattern
Claim
6.14
. We observe that
C_{1},D_{1}\leqslant Z
and
D_{2},D_{2}\leqslant A
, and we can view them as vertices in
\mathcal{T}^{Z}
and
\mathcal{T}^{A}
respectively. We will use a similar strategy as in
Claim
6.27
, and use the fact that the graph
\mathcal{T}^{Z}
has short paths between every two matrices. That is,

Claim 6.32
.

For every
m
and two matrices
C,D\in\mathcal{T}^{m}
there is a path of length
12
between
C
and
D
.

This claim is proven in the end of
Appendix
D.4
.

Using this claim, we find a pair of
12
-paths from
C_{i}
to
D_{i}
in
\mathcal{T}^{Z}
and
\mathcal{T}^{A}
respectively:
(C_{1},M^{1},M^{2},\dots,M^{11},D_{1})
and
(C_{2},N^{1},N^{2},\dots,N^{11},D_{2})
. Then we observe that the matrices
K^{i}=M^{i}\oplus N^{i}
form a path
(C,K^{1},K^{2},\dots,K^{11},D)
from
C
to
D
in
X
. Moreover, every edge in this path is in a triangle with
A
and with
Z
. The claim is proven.
∎

6.3.1
Coboundary expansion in the links and cosystolic expansion

In
[
Gol23
]
it is proven that the Cayley complex whose link is
X
is not a coboundary expander for any constant
\beta>0
. It is done by proving that
Z^{1}(X,\Gamma)\neq B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
.

However, we can still prove that these complexes are
cosystolic expanders
. This notion is a weakening of coboundary expansion that allows the existence of cohomology, i.e. for
Z^{1}(X,\Gamma)\neq B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
:

Definition 6.33

(
1
-dimensional cosystolic expander)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional simplicial complex for
d\geqslant 2
. Let
\beta>0
. We say that
X
is a
1
-dimensional
\beta
-cosystolic expander
if for every group
\Gamma
, and every
f\in C^{1}(X,\Gamma)
there exists some
h\in Z^{1}(X,\Gamma)
such that

\beta\dist(f,h)\leqslant{\mathrm{wt}}({\delta}f).

(6.1)

The difference between this definition and the definition of coboundary expansion, is that in the definition of coboundary expansion we require
h={\delta}g
, or equivalently
h\in B^{1}(X,\Gamma)
whereas here we only require
h\in Z^{1}(X,\Gamma)
which is less restrictive.

We note that cosystolic expansion is still an important property and can sometimes be used in applications such as topological overlapping property
[
DKW18
]
or to get some form of agreement testing guarantee
[
DD23
]
.

Kaufman, Kazhdan and Lubotzky were the first to prove that in local spectral expanders, cosystolic expansion follows from coboundary expansion of the links
[
KKL16
]
(together with local spectral expansion). Later on their seminal result was extended to all groups and higher dimensions
[
EK16
,
KM18
,
KM21
,
KM22
,
DD23a
]
. In particular, we can use the quantitatively stronger theorem in
[
DD23a
, Theorem 1.2]
, to get cosystolic expansion from
Lemma
6.29
and the local spectral expansion.

Corollary 6.34
.

Let
Y=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})^{\leqslant d})
for
n>2d
. Let
X=\beta(Y)
be the basification of
Y
. Let
Cay(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},S)
be the complexes whose links are isomorphic to
X
, as constructed in
Section
3
. Assume that
n
is sufficiently large such that
Cay(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},S)
is a
10^{-4}
-local spectral expander. Then
Cay(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},S)
is also a
1
-dimensional
10^{-4}
-cosystolic expander.

We omit the details.

7
Induced Grassmann posets

In this subsection we will present a generalization of our Johnson and Matrix Grassmann posets. After a few preliminaries, we will present a way to construct Grassmann posets based on the Hadamard encoding. We will see that such complexes have a simple description of their links. Afterwards, we will show that on some of these complexes we can decompose the links into a tensor product of simple graphs, generalizing some of the previous results in this paper. We start with some classical facts about quotient spaces and bilinear forms.

7.1
Preliminaries on quotient spaces and bilinear forms

Quotient spaces

Let
U\subseteq V
be a subspace. The quotient space is a vector space
\nicefrac{{V}}{{U}}
whose vectors are the sets
v+U=\left\{v+y\left|\;\vphantom{v+yy\in U}\right.y\in U\right\}
for every vector
v\in V
. It is standard that addition (and scalar multiplication) is well defined on these sets. Addition is defined as
(v+U)+(x+U)\coloneqq(v+x)+U
and the sum of the two sets does not depend on the representatives
v\in v+U
and
x\in x+U
.

Bilinear forms

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be two vector spaces. A bilinear form is a function
\langle\rangle:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
such that

\langle x_{1}+x_{2},y_{1}+y_{2}\rangle=\langle x_{1},y_{1}\rangle+\langle x_{1},y_{2}\rangle+\langle x_{2},y_{1}\rangle+\langle x_{2},y_{2}\rangle

for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in V_{1}
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in V_{2}
. A bilinear form is
non-degenerate
if for every
0\neq x\in V_{1}
there exists
y\in V_{2}
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=1
, and for every
0\neq y\in V_{2}
there exists
x\in V_{1}
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=1
.

When possible, we will work with the standard bilinear form
\langle\rangle_{std}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
given by
\langle x,y\rangle_{std}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}y_{j}
instead of arbitrary bilinear forms. The next claim allows us to move to this standard bilinear form.

Claim 7.1
.

Let
\langle\rangle:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be a non-degenerate bilinear form. Then
\mathrm{dim}(V_{1})=\mathrm{dim}(V_{2})=:m
and there exists isomorphisms
\psi_{i}:V_{i}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
such that
\langle x,y\rangle=\langle\psi_{1}(x),\psi_{2}(y)\rangle_{std}
.

From now on, we write
\langle\rangle
instead of
\langle\rangle_{std}
when it is clear from context;
Claim
7.1
justifies the abuse of notation.

Proof sketch of
Claim
7.1
.

To see that
\mathrm{dim}(V_{1})\leqslant\mathrm{dim}(V_{2})
, note that from non-degeneracy the function
\pi:V_{1}\to V_{2}^{*}
that sends
x\overset{\pi}{\mapsto}\langle x,\cdot\rangle
is an injective linear function. Swapping
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
shows that the dimensions are equal.

We observe that for every basis
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
of
V_{1}
there exists a
dual basis

h_{1},\dots,h_{m}
of
V_{2}
such that
\langle f_{i},h_{j}\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}
.
As for the isomorphisms, we fix some basis
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
of
V_{1}
and its dual basis
h_{1},\dots,h_{m}\in V_{2}
. Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{m}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
be the standard basis. It is easy to verify that the pair of isomorphisms
\psi_{1}
that sends
f_{i}
to
e_{i}
, and
\psi_{2}
that sends
h_{i}
to
e_{i}
satisfy
\langle x,y\rangle=\langle\psi_{1}(x),\psi_{2}(y)\rangle
.
∎

For a fixed bilinear form and a subspace
U\subseteq V_{1}
the
orthogonal subspace
is the subspace

U^{\bot}=\left\{y\in V_{2}\left|\;\vphantom{y\in V_{2}\forall x\in U,\langle x,y\rangle=0}\right.\forall x\in U,\langle x,y\rangle=0\right\}\subseteq V_{2},

and for a subspace
U\subseteq V_{2}
, one can analogously define
U^{\bot}\subseteq V_{1}
.

Definition 7.2

(Derived bilinear form)
.

Let
\langle\rangle:V_{1}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
be a non-degenerate bilinear form and let
U\subseteq V_{1}
be a subspace. The
derived bilinear form

\langle\rangle_{U}:\nicefrac{{V_{1}}}{{U}}\times U^{\bot}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a bilinear form given by

\langle x+U,v\rangle_{U}=\langle x,v\rangle

for any
x+U\in\nicefrac{{V_{1}}}{{U}}
and
v\in U^{\bot}
.

It is easy to verify that
\langle x+U,v\rangle_{U}
is well defined. That is, it does not depend on the representative
x\in x+U
. It is also straightforward to verify that this derived bilinear form is non-degenerate.

Most of the time we will abuse notation and write
\langle x+U,v\rangle
instead of
\langle x+U,v\rangle_{U}
when
U
is clear from the context.

7.2
The induced Grassmann poset - definition and running examples

In this subsection we construct a type of Grassmann posets called
induced Grassmann posets
. This construction generalizes the matrix Grassmann poset
[
Gol23
]
, its sparsified version in
Definition
3.8
and the Johnson Grassmann poset in
Definition
4.3
.

Let
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a function, not necessarily linear. The
induced Hadamard encoding
of
s
is the
linear
map
\widehat{s}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
where

\widehat{s}(x)\coloneqq\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}:\langle x,v\rangle=1}s(v).

We note that the inner product between any
x
and the
0
vector is always
0
. Thus we just need to define these functions over
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
.

We call
\widehat{s}
the induced Hadamard encoding, because if
n=2^{d}-1
, we index the coordinates of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
by the non-zero vectors of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
, and set
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
to be the function that sends
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
to the standard basis vector that is
1
on the
v
-th coordinate and zero elsewhere. Then
\widehat{s}
is the (usual) Hadamard encoding and its image is the Hadamard code.

Definition 7.3

(Induced Grassmann poset)
.

Let
d,n\geqslant 0
be two integers such that
n\geqslant 2^{d}-1
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of (non-linear) functions such that every
s\in\mathcal{S}
is injective and the set
Im(s)\coloneqq\left\{s(v)\left|\;\vphantom{s(v)v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\right.v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\right\}
is independent. The
\mathcal{S}
-
induced Grassmann poset
is the
d
-dimensional Grassmann poset
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
such that
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)=\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}\right\}
.

To be explicit, for
i<d
, an
i
-dimensional subspace
W
is in
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
if there exists some
W^{\prime}\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
containing
W
. Equivalently, this is if and only if there exist a
s\in\mathcal{S}
and a subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
such that
\widehat{s}|_{U}=W
. For
i=1
this says that
v\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(1)
if and only if
v=\widehat{s}(x)
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}
and non-zero
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
.

We note that if the vectors in the image of
s
are independent, then
\mathrm{dim}(Im(\widehat{s}))=d
. We also note that different
s,s^{\prime}
may still result in the same spaces
Im(\widehat{s})=Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}})
.

Although this definition seems abstract, hopefully, the following examples, which were already introduced in the previous sections, will convince the reader that this definition is natural.

Example 7.4

(The complete Grassmann)
.

Let
d
and
n\geqslant 2^{d}-1
be positive integers. Let
\mathcal{S}
be
all
injective functions whose image is independent. Then
Y^{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{G}(F_{2}^{n})^{\leqslant d}
is the
d
-skeleton of the complete Grassmann of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. To see this let us show that every
d
-dimensional subspace
W
has some
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that
Im(\widehat{s})=W
. Let
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
be an arbitrary function and let
W^{\prime}=Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}})
. Find some
A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
that maps
W^{\prime}
to
W
and set
s(x)\coloneqq A(s^{\prime}(x))
. It is easy to verify that indeed
s\in\mathcal{S}
and that
Im(\widehat{s})=W
.

Example 7.5

(Johnson Grassmann poset)
.

Let
d
and
n\geqslant 2^{d}-1
be positive integers. Let
E=\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be the standard basis for
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{J}
be the set of injective functions from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
to
E
. For this
\mathcal{S}_{J}
, the poset
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{J}}
is the Johnson Grassmann poset.

First note that a vector from
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
is in
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{J}}(1)
if and only if its Hamming weight is
2^{d-1}
: every such vector
w
is equal to
w=\widehat{s}(x)=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}_{J}
and
x\neq 0
. The
s(v)
’s are distinct standard basis vectors, and there are exactly
2^{d-1}
non-zero
\langle x,v\rangle
in the sum. Therefore the weight of
w
is
2^{d-1}
.

The
d
-dimensional subspaces are the Hadamard encoding on
2^{d}-1
coordinates inside
n
. Let us be more precise. Fix a map
s
and for simplicity assume that
Im(s)=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{2^{d}-1}\}
. Let
W=\mathrm{span}(Im(s))
. By indexing
W
with the coordinates of
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, then
\widehat{s}(x)=\left(\langle x,v\rangle\right)_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\in W
, which is the Hadamard encoding of
x
.

The basification of this poset is an alternative description of the links of Johnson complexes.

Example 7.6

(Matrix poset
[
Gol23
]
)
.

Let
d
and
n\geqslant 2^{d}-1
. Recall that
\mathcal{M}(1)
is the set of all rank one
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
be the set of functions
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\to\mathcal{M}(1)
such that the matrix
M_{s}=\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\right)
is a direct sum, or in other words
\rank(M_{s})=2^{d}-1
. One can see directly that such
s
’s are the admissible functions of
Definition
3.7
for the special case where the ambient poset is complete Grassmann
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})
. In this case,
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}=\mathcal{SP}(\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}))
.
20
20

20

There is a slight technical difference between this complex and the one in
[
Gol23
]
, that follows from Golowich’s use of larger fields. See
[
Gol23
]
for more details.

To draw the analogy to the Johnson, let us illustrate that indeed a matrix
A
is a
1
-dimensional subspace in the
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
-induced Grassmann poset
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}
if and only if
\rank(A)=2^{d-1}
. This follows the same logic as in the Johnson case. If
A\in Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1)
then
A=\widehat{s}(x)
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
x\neq 0
. This means that
A
is the sum of
2^{d-1}
distinct
s(v)
’s. As the matrix
M_{s}
is a direct sum, the sum of the
s(v)
’s is also a direct sum. This implies that
\rank(A)=\sum_{x:\langle v,x\rangle=1}\rank(s(v))=2^{d-1}
. On the other hand, suppose
A
has rank
2^{d-1}
and decomposition
A=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{d-1}}e_{j}\otimes f_{j}
. Take an arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and let
R=\left\{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\left|\;\vphantom{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\langle v,x\rangle=1}\right.\langle v,x\rangle=1\right\}
. As
R
also has size
2^{d-1}
, we can find some
s\in\mathcal{S}
that maps the elements in
R
to the
e_{j}\otimes f_{j}
in the decomposition of
A
. For such an
s
, we have
\widehat{s}(x)=\sum_{v\in R}s(v)=A
, thus concluding
A\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(1)
.

The construction in
[
Gol23
]
used matrices over a larger field of characteristic
2
instead of
\mathbb{F}_{2}
itself. See
[
Gol23
]
for the details.

The next example is the one given in
Definition
3.8
.

Example 7.7

(Sparsified matrix poset)
.

Let
d>0
and let
Y_{0}
be any
2^{d}-1
-dimensional Grassmann poset over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. We define a sparsification
\mathcal{S}_{Y_{0}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
.

\mathcal{S}_{Y_{0}}=\left\{s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\left|\;\vphantom{s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}\row(M_{s}),\col(M_{s})\in Y_{0}(2^{d}-1)}\right.\row(M_{s}),\col(M_{s})\in Y_{0}(2^{d}-1)\right\}

where
M_{s}=\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\right)
. The one dimensional spaces
M\in Y^{\mathcal{S}_{Y_{0}}}(1)
are
2^{d-1}
-rank matrices such that
\row(M),\col(M)\in Y_{0}(2^{d-1})
. The top-level spaces are
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}(d)
, such that the sum of row spaces (resp. column spaces) is in
Y_{0}(2^{d}-1)
.

Finally, we end this subsection with the comment that if one has a distribution
\mu
over
\mathcal{S}
, this also defines a distribution over
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
in a natural way. That is, sampling
s\sim\mu
and then outputting
Im(\widehat{s})\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
. Unless otherwise stated, we will always take the uniform distribution over
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
.

7.2.1
Subspaces of an induced Grassmann poset

Fix
i<d
. The subspaces
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
are defined by downward closure, but we can give an alternative description of
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
via an induced Grassmann poset with some
\mathcal{S}_{i}
that is derived from
\mathcal{S}
. We begin by looking at our examples for intuition and then generalize.

In the examples above, the lower dimensional subspaces have some structure to them. For instance, one can verify that the
i
-dimensional subspaces in the Johnson Grassmann poset could be described as images of Hadamard encodings
\widehat{s}
where the image of
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
consists of vectors of Hamming weight
2^{d-i}
such that the supports of every pair
s(v)
and
s(v^{\prime})
are disjoint. This generalizes the case where
i=d
, and the image of
s\in\mathcal{S}_{J}
are distinct vectors of weight
1
.

In a similar manner, we saw in
Claim
3.9
that
i
-dimensional subspaces of the Matrix Grassmann poset are images of induced Hadamard encodings

Im(\widehat{s})=\left\{\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)\left|\;\vphantom{\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}\right.x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\right\},

where every
s(v)
has rank
2^{d-i}
and
M_{s}=\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)
has rank
2^{d}-2^{d-i}
. Again, this is a natural generalization of the case where
i=d
.

Back to the more general setup. Recall that every
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
is the image of
\widehat{s}
for some
s\in\mathcal{S}
. The map
\widehat{s}
is always injective, therefore every
i
-dimensional subspace
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
is also the image
W^{\prime}=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
of some
i
-dimensional subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
.

Let
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
be the
(d-i)
-dimensional space such that
U=V^{\perp}
, that is,
U=\left\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\left|\;\vphantom{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\forall y\in V,\;\langle x,y\rangle=0}\right.\forall y\in V,\;\langle x,y\rangle=0\right\}
. Let
s_{V}:(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}})\setminus\{0+V\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be
s_{V}(v+V)=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}s(v^{\prime})
. We use the bilinear form
\langle\rangle_{V}
to define the function
\widehat{s_{V}}:U\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
as the induced Hadamard encoding, i.e.

\widehat{s_{V}}(x)=\sum_{v+V\in\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}}}\langle v+V,x\rangle_{V}\cdot s_{V}(v+V).

Here we recall that
\langle v+V,x\rangle_{V}\coloneqq\langle x,v\rangle
for
x\in U
(and we recall that this is independent of the choice of representative
v
).

Let

\mathcal{S}_{i}=\left\{s_{V}\left|\;\vphantom{s_{V}s\in\mathcal{S},V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d},\mathrm{dim}(V)=d-i}\right.s\in\mathcal{S},V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d},\mathrm{dim}(V)=d-i\right\}.

We will show that for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})=Im(\widehat{s_{V}})
and hence
\mathcal{S}_{i}
induces
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
.

Claim 7.8
.

For every subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
,
V=U^{\bot}
, function
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, and
x\in U
,
\widehat{s_{V}}(x)=\widehat{s}(x)
. As a result,
(Y^{\mathcal{S}})^{\leqslant i}=Y^{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
.

Here there is some abuse of notation since the domain of
\widehat{s_{V}}
is not
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}
, but some arbitrary
i
-dimensional space
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
. However, this does not matter since we are taking the
image space
of
\widehat{s_{V}}
.

Before proving this claim, let us review our previous examples in light of this.

Example 7.9

(Johnson Grassmann poset)
.

For the Johnson Grassmann poset, fix a
(d-i)
-dimensional subspace
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
. Then a function
s_{V}\in\mathcal{S}_{J,i}
if and only if every value
s_{V}(v+V)
has Hamming weight
2^{d-i}
and for every
v+V\neq u+V
the supports of
s_{V}(v+V)
and
s_{V}(u+V)
are mutually disjoint.

By
Claim
7.1
we replace the functions in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
, with similar functions whose domain is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}
, without changing the resulting poset. Thus another set of functions
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
such that the induced Grassmann on
\mathcal{S}_{i}^{\prime}
is also equal to
(Y^{\mathcal{S}_{J}})^{\leqslant i}
is the set of all functions
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that the Hamming weight of every
s(v)
is
2^{d-i}
, and such that if
v\neq u
then the supports of
s(v)
and
s(u)
are disjoint. These are exactly the admissible functions in
Definition
4.14
.

Example 7.10

(Matrix poset and sparsified matrix poset)
.

For the matrix example above let us fix some
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}
and
(d-i)
-dimensional subspace
V\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
. The matrix
s_{V}(v+V)
is a direct sum of
2^{d-i}
rank
1
matrices. Thus
\rank(s_{V}(v+V))=2^{d-i}
. The matrices
\{s_{V}(v+V)\}_{v+V}
have the property that their sum
M_{s_{V}}=\bigoplus_{v+V\neq 0+V}s_{V}(v+V)
is a direct sum, or equivalently that
\rank(M_{s_{V}})=2^{d}-2^{d-i}
.
22
22

22

Note that this is not the matrix
M_{s}
in
Example
7.6
, since the matrices
s(v)
do not participate in this sum when
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
.

Again by
Claim
7.1
, we can give an equivalent set of functions
\mathcal{S}_{i}
— the level
i

admissible
functions in
Section
3
. These are all functions
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that
\rank(s(v))=2^{d-i}
and
\rank(M_{s})=2^{d}-2^{d-i}
where
M_{s}=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)
.

We remark that the sparsified matrix induced Grassmann poset
\mathcal{S}_{Y_{0}}
also admit a similar description of the
i
-spaces which was explained in
Section
3
.

Proof of
Claim
7.8
.

Fix
s
and an
i
dimensional subspace
U
. Let
V=U^{\bot}
be some
d-i
-dimensional subspace in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
. Let
x\in U
. Note that while
\widehat{s}(x)=\sum_{v\neq 0}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)
, we can remove all elements
v\in V
from the sum since
x
is orthogonal to them. We also recall that for every
v_{1},v_{2}\in v+V
,
\langle x,v_{1}\rangle=\langle x,v_{2}\rangle\coloneqq\langle x,v+V\rangle
so

\displaystyle\widehat{s}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\notin V}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v+V\neq 0+V}\langle x,v+V\rangle\left(\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}s(v^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v+V\neq 0+V}\langle x,v+V\rangle s_{V}(v+V)=\widehat{s_{V}}(x).

Thus in particular for every
s
,
Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})=Im(\widehat{s}_{V})
. As
\widehat{s}
is injective, every subspace
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(i)
is the image
Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
of some
i
-dimensional subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
. The claim follows.
∎

7.3
A decomposition for the links

Our goal is to prove that links of some induced Grassmann posets decompose into tensor products of simple graphs, reproving and extending both
Proposition
3.19
for the matrix Grassmann poset,
Lemma
3.14
for the sparsified matrix poset, and
Proposition
4.7
for the Johnson Grassmann poset.

For this we need to zoom in to a less general class of induced Grassmann posets, which we call
rank-based
induced Grassmann posets with the
direct-meet property
. Let us define these.

7.3.1
Rank-based posets

Let
G\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a spanning set that does not contain
0
. The rank of a vector
v
with respect to
G
,
\rank_{G}(v)
, is the minimum number of elements in
G
necessary to write
v
as their sum.

Definition 7.11

(
k
-admissible spanning set)
.

We say
G
is
k
-admissible if for every
v
with
\rank_{G}(v)\leqslant k
there exists some
u
such that
\rank_{G}(v+u)=\rank_{G}(v)+\rank_{G}(u)=k
.

Example 7.12
.

1.

For example in Johnson Grassmann posets, take
G_{\mathcal{J}}\coloneqq\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
to be the standard basis. Then
\rank_{G_{\mathcal{J}}}(v)=wt(v)
is just its Hamming weight. This set is
n
-admissible.

2.

For matrix Grassmann posets, taking
G_{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}(1)
to be the set of rank one matrices inside
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
, then
\rank_{G_{\mathcal{M}}}(M)=\rank(M)
is its usual matrix rank. This set is also
n
-admissible. This second example can be extended to
G
being the rank one
k
-tensors for
k>2
in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n\dots\times n}
as well.

The following notation of a direct sum generalizes the matrix poset direct sum.

Definition 7.13

(Abstract direct sum)
.

We say that
w_{1}+w_{2}+\dots+w_{j}
is a direct sum and write
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus w_{j}
, if
\rank_{G}(w_{1}+w_{2}+\dots+w_{j})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}\rank_{G}(w_{\ell})
. We also write
w_{1}\leqslant w_{2}
if
w_{1}\oplus(w_{2}-w_{1})
.

For
G_{\mathcal{J}}
we have
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}
if and only if they are disjoint (as sets) and
w_{1}\leqslant w_{2}
if and only if
w_{1}\subseteq w_{2}
. For
G_{\mathcal{M}}
the definitions of
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
and
M_{1}\leqslant M_{2}
coincide with the direct sum and matrix domination order defined in
Section
2.4
. It is easy to show that for any
G
the relation
\leqslant
is always a partial order.

Definition 7.14

(Rank
r
-admissible functions)
.

Let
d
be an integer. Let
G
be a
(r(2^{d}-1))
-admissible set. A
rank
r
admissible
function is
s:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that every
s(v)
has
\rank_{G}(s(v))=r
, and such that
\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)
is a direct sum. The rank
r
admissible set of functions
\mathcal{S}_{G}
is the set containing all rank
r
admissible functions.

We comment that this set
\mathcal{S}
indeed gives rise to an induced Grassmann poset, because if the sum of the vectors in
\{s(v)\}_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
is a direct sum, then this set is independent. The reason is that if there is a nonempty subset
A\subseteq\{s(v)\}_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}
such that
\sum_{v\in A}s(v)=0
, then

\rank_{G}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\right)=\rank_{G}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus(A\cup\{0\})}s(v)\right)\leqslant\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus(A\cup\{0\})}\rank_{G}(s(v))<\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}\rank_{G}(s(v)),

which shows that in such a case the sum was not a direct sum in the first place.

For the rest of this section we will always assume there is some ambient
(r(2^{d}-1))
-admissible set
G
, and that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{G}
with respect to this set.

Definition 7.15

(Rank-based induced Grassmann poset)
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is the rank
r
admissible set with respect to some
G
, then
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
is called a
rank-based
induced Grassmann poset.

Example 7.16
.

Both the Johnson Grassmann poset and the matrix Grassmann posets are rank-based induced Grassmann posets for
G_{\mathcal{J}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
respectively.

Example 7.17

(Non example)
.

The
d
-skeleton of the complete Grassmann is
not
a rank-based induced Grassmann for any
d\geqslant 2
. To see this we observe that in a rank-based induced Grassmann poset
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
, every
w\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(1)
has
\rank_{G}(w)=r2^{d-1}\geqslant 2
. This is because
\widehat{s}(x)
is always a direct sum of
2^{d-1}
vectors of rank
r
. However, in the complete Grassmann
Y(1)
contains all non-zero vectors, including the vectors in
G
which have rank one.

7.3.2
The direct-meet property

Unfortunately, this is still too general for a meaningful decomposition lemma. For this we also need to define another property of
G
which we will call the
direct-meet property
.

Definition 7.18

(Meet)
.

The
Meet
of a set of elements
T\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
is an element
\textup{Meet}(T)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that

1.

For every
t\in T
,
Meet(T)\leqslant t
(in the abstract direct sum sense).

2.

For every
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, if
v\leqslant t
for every
t\in T
, then
v\leqslant\textup{Meet}(T)
.

This is a standard definition in poset theory. We note that
\textup{Meet}(T)
does not necessarily exist, but if it exists it is always unique.

Definition 7.19

(Direct-meet property)
.

We say that
G
has the
direct-meet property
if for every three elements,
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}
such that
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}
, the meet of
\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}
exists and is equal to
w_{1}
.

We say
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
has the
direct-meet property
if it is a rank-based induced complex, where the generating set
G
has the direct-meet property.

We note that this definition is not satisfied by all spanning sets
G
.

Example 7.20

(Non example)
.

Let
G=\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{1}+e_{4},e_{2}+e_{4},e_{3}+e_{4}\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4}
where
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}
are the standard basis. Then
G
does not have the direct-meet property. To see this let
w_{1}=e_{1},w_{2}=e_{2},w_{3}=e_{3}
. The triple
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}
is a direct sum. However,
e_{1}+e_{4}\leqslant w_{1}+w_{2}=e_{1}+e_{2}=(e_{1}+e_{4})+(e_{2}+e_{4})
and
e_{1}+e_{4}\leqslant w_{1}+w_{3}=e_{1}+e_{3}=(e_{1}+e_{4})+(e_{3}+e_{4})
, but
e_{1}+e_{4}\not\leqslant e_{1}=w_{1}
. Hence
w_{1}
is not the meet of
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}
and
w_{1}\oplus w_{3}
.

However, both the Johnson and the matrix sets have this property.

Example 7.21
.

For the set
G_{\mathcal{J}}=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n}\}
which defines
Y^{\mathcal{J}}
, the direct-meet property holds. Indeed, the meet of any set
T
is the intersection
\textup{Meet}(T)=\bigcap_{t\in T}t
, where we treat the vectors as sets. In particular, the meet of
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}
and
w_{1}\oplus w_{3}
is
w_{1}
.

Example 7.22
.

A more interesting example is the low-rank decomposition case. Let
G
be the set of rank
1
matrices which defines
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}
. Contrary to the Johnson case, there exists a set
\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
with no meet. For example let
e_{1},e_{2}
be independent, let
M_{1}=e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes e_{2}
and let
M_{2}=e_{1}\otimes e_{1}+e_{2}\otimes(e_{1}+e_{2})
. Both
A=e_{1}\otimes e_{1}
and
B=e_{2}\otimes(e_{1}+e_{2})
are under
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
in the matrix domination order. However,
A\not\leqslant B
and
B\not\leqslant A
and therefore there is no meet for
\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
. Nevertheless, the rank
1
matrices have the direct-meet property.

Claim 7.23
.

The set
G_{\mathcal{M}}
has the direct-meet property.

This claim essentially abstracts the observation in
[
Gol23
, Lemma 57]
. We prove it in
Appendix
C.3
.

Finally we state an elementary claim about the meet which we will use later on, extending the property from three elements to any finite number of elements.

Claim 7.24
.

Let
G
be a set with the direct-meet property. Let
T=\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{m}\}
be such that
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}w_{j}
(with respect to
G
). Let
\widehat{T}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{r}\}
be linear combinations of
T
, i.e.
v_{j}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in I_{j}}w_{\ell}
for some subsets
I_{j}\subseteq[m]
. Then

\textup{Meet}(\widehat{T})=\bigoplus_{\ell\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{r}I_{j}}w_{\ell}.

The proof is just an induction on the size of
\widehat{T}
. We omit it.

7.3.3
The general decomposition lemma

We move towards our decomposition lemma for links of a rank-based
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
with the direct-meet property. Our components for the decomposition lemma are the following.

Definition 7.25

(
z
-domination graph)
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be of rank
4m
. The graph
\mathcal{T}^{z}
has vertices

V=\left\{y\leqslant z\left|\;\vphantom{y\leqslant z\rank(y)=2m}\right.\rank(y)=2m\right\},

E=\left\{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\rank(w_{i})=m,w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}\leqslant z}\right.\rank(w_{i})=m,w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}\leqslant z\right\}.

To sample an edge we sample a uniformly random decomposition
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}\oplus w_{4}=z
, such that
\rank(w_{i})=m
and output
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
such that
y=w_{1}\oplus w_{2},y^{\prime}=w_{1}\oplus w_{3}
. The set of edges is the support of the the above distribution.

For
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
, this graph coincides with the subposet graphs introduced in
Definition
3.11
. For the Johnson Grassmann poset, this graph turns out to be the Johnson graph
\mathcal{T}^{z}\cong J(4m,2m,m)
. This is because every vector in the graph has weight
2m
and is contained in a
z
of weight
4m
. Having
y=w_{1}\oplus w_{2}
and
y^{\prime}=w_{1}\oplus w_{3}
in this definition is equivalent to
y
and
y^{\prime}
intersecting on half their coordinates.

We continue with our second component.

Definition 7.26

(
z
-link graph)
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a vector whose rank is
r(2^{d}-2^{i})
and let
m\leqslant r2^{i-2}
. The graph
\mathcal{H}_{z,m}
has vertices

V=\left\{y\in\mathbb{F}^{n}_{2}\left|\;\vphantom{y\in\mathbb{F}^{n}_{2}\rank(y)=2m,y\oplus z}\right.\rank(y)=2m,y\oplus z\right\},

E=\left\{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\left|\;\vphantom{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\rank(w_{i})=m,z\oplus w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}}\right.\rank(w_{i})=m,z\oplus w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}\right\}.

We define the (weighted) edges according to the following sampling process. To sample an edge we sample a random
s\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}s(v)\coloneqq t\geqslant z
. Then we uniformly sample
z^{\prime}\leqslant t-z
such that
\rank(z^{\prime})=4m
and output a random edge in
\mathcal{T}^{z^{\prime}}
.

For
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{M}}
, this graph coincides with the disjoint graph introduced in
Definition
3.12
. For the Johnson Grassmann poset, this graph is also a Johnson graph,
\mathcal{H}_{z,m}\cong J(n-|z|,2m,m)
. This is because every vector in the graph has weight
2m
whose support is disjoint from
z
. The sampling process in this graph amounts to uniformly sampling a
4m
-weighted set
z^{\prime}
that is disjoint from
z
, and then taking a step inside its
\mathcal{T}^{z^{\prime}}
. This is the same as sampling (uniformly) two subsets
y,y^{\prime}
disjoint from
z
that intersect on half their coordinates.

The final component we need to state the decomposition lemma is this following claim.

Claim 7.27
.

Let
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
be a rank-based induced poset with the direct-meet property. Then for every
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
, and
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
such that
Im(\widehat{s_{1}})=Im(\widehat{s_{2}})=W
, it holds that
Im(s_{1})=Im(s_{2})
.

We will hence denote by
Q_{W}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{2^{i}-1}\}
the image set of a given
W
. We also denote by
z_{W}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}z_{j}
the direct sum.

This claim is a consequence of the direct-meet property. We prove it later and for now, let us proceed to the decomposition lemma.

Lemma 7.28

(The decomposition lemma)
.

Let
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
with
Q_{W}=\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}
and
z_{w}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{i}}z_{j}
as above. Then
Y_{W}\cong\mathcal{T}^{z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2^{i}-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{z_{W},2^{i-2}}
.

We observe that this lemma applied to
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{J}}}
implies
Proposition
4.7
. Applied to
Y^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{M}}}
this lemma proves
Proposition
3.19
. We can even generalize this lemma slightly more to capture the sparsified matrix case in
Lemma
3.14
. See
Section
7.4
for details.

In order to prove
Lemma
7.28
, we need to define the isomorphism. To do so, we give a combinatorial criterion for a vector
y
being in
Y_{W}^{\mathcal{S}}(1)
in terms of its meets with the elements in
Q_{W}
.

Claim 7.29
.

Let
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
with
Q_{W}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{2^{i}-1}\}
as above. Let
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. Then
y
is in the link of
W
if and only if there exists unique
y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{2^{i}}
with the following properties:

1.

y=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{i}}y_{j}
(that is,
y
is equal to the sum of all
y_{j}
’s and this sum is direct).

2.

All
y_{j}
’s have equal rank.

3.

y_{i}\leqslant z_{i}
.

In this case the subspace
W^{\prime}=W+\mathrm{span}(y)
, has
Q_{W^{\prime}}=\{z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}\cup\{z_{j}-y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}}
.

Note that items
1
and
3
imply that the rank of
y_{j}
is half the rank of
z_{j}
.

Given
Claim
7.29
, we can define the isomorphism
\psi:Y_{W}\to\mathcal{T}^{z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2^{i}-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{z_{W},2^{i-2}}
by

\psi(y)=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{2^{i}}),

(7.1)

where the image consists of the unique
y_{j}
’s promised by the claim. This function is also a bijection by
Claim
7.29
.

To conclude the proof, we need to argue that an edge
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
is sampled by sampling the different components
y_{j},y_{j}^{\prime}
independently from one another. For this part, we basically need the following independence claim for the distribution we used when sampling
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Let
P
be the random variable obtained by sampling a function from
\mathcal{S}
. For a set
R\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
, let
P|_{R}
be the random variable obtained by restricting the function only to the vectors of
R
. Let
z_{0}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. Then
P|_{R}
and
P|_{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus R}
are independent, if we condition on the event
\sum_{v\in R}P|_{R}(v)=z_{0}
.

Claim 7.30
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rank-based poset. Let
R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{m}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
be a partition. Let
z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{m-1}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be such that
\rank(z_{i})=|R_{i}|\cdot r
and
z_{1}\oplus z_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus z_{m-1}
.

Consider the joint random variables
\{P_{i}=s|_{R_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. Then conditioned on the event that for every
i=1,2,\dots,m-1

\sum_{v\in R_{i}}P_{i}(v)=z_{i},

these random variables are independent.

We prove the lemma first, then go back to show the sub-claims.

Proof of
Lemma
7.28
.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{T}^{z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2^{i}-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{z_{W},r2^{i-2}}
. We define a map
\psi:Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}(1)\to\mathcal{G}
mapping
y\mapsto(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{2^{i}})
as in (
7.1
). That is, the
y_{j}
’s are as in
Claim
7.29
. By
Claim
7.29
, this map is well defined for every
y
and sends link vertices into the vertices of
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, by
Claim
7.29
this is also a bijection, since the map

\psi^{-1}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{2^{i}})\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j=1}^{2^{i}}y_{j}

is indeed onto
\bar{Y}^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}(1)
according to the claim.

Next we prove that a pair
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
is an edge in
Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
if and only if
\{\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime})\}
is an edge in
\mathcal{G}
(ignoring any edge weights for now). Recall that
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
is an edge in
Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
if and only if
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\mathcal{S}_{J}}_{W^{\prime}}(1)
where
W^{\prime}=W+\mathrm{span}(y)
.
By
Claim
7.29
,
Q_{W^{\prime}}=\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}}\cup\{z_{j}-y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}
.
Let us re-index
t_{j}\in Q_{W^{\prime}}
’s with

t_{j,b}=\begin{cases}y_{j}&b=0\\
z_{j}-y_{j}&b=1\end{cases}.

for
(j,b)\in\left([2^{i}-1]\times\{0,1\}\right)\cup\{(2^{i},0)\}
.
Moreover, by applying
Claim
7.29
to
W^{\prime}
we have that
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W^{\prime}}(1)
if and only if:

1.

We can decompose
y^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{(j,b)\in[2^{i}]\times\{0,1\}}y^{\prime}_{j,b}
such that the rank of every summand is equal.

2.

For every
(j,b)\neq(2^{i},1)
,
y^{\prime}_{j,b}\leqslant t_{j,b}
.

3.

The final
y^{\prime}_{2^{i},1}
is a direct sum with
z_{W^{\prime}}
(the sum of all elements in
Q_{W^{\prime}}
).

So on the one hand, if
\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime})
are neighbors in the tensor product
\mathcal{G}
, we can take
y_{j,0}^{\prime}=\textup{Meet}(y_{j}^{\prime},y_{j})
and
y_{j,1}^{\prime}=\textup{Meet}(y_{j}^{\prime},z_{j}-y_{j})
which will satisfy the above property (and is well defined by the properties of
\mathcal{T}^{z_{j}}
and the direct-meet property). This shows that being an edge in
\mathcal{G}
implies being an edge in
Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}
.

For the reverse direction, suppose there exists such a decomposition of
y^{\prime}
. Then for every
z_{j}
the vectors
w_{1}=y_{j,0}^{\prime},w_{2}=y_{j,1}^{\prime},w_{3}=t_{j,0}-y_{j,0}^{\prime}
and
w_{4}=t_{j,1}-y_{j,1}^{\prime}
have that
w_{1}\oplus w_{2}=y_{j}^{\prime}
and
w_{1}\oplus w_{3}=y_{j}
. In addition they have equal rank and their direct sum is equal to
z_{j}
. Thus
\{y_{j},y_{j}^{\prime}\}
is an edge in
\mathcal{T}^{z_{j}}
. For the final component, a similar argument applies: We decompose
w_{1}=y^{\prime}_{2^{i},0}
,
w_{2}=y_{2^{i}}-y^{\prime}_{2^{i},0}
and
w_{3}=y^{\prime}_{2^{i},1}
. All three vectors have rank
2^{i-2}
and have a direct sum. Furthermore,
y_{2^{i}}=w_{1}\oplus w_{2}
and
y_{2^{i}}^{\prime}=w_{1}\oplus w_{3}
. As a result
\{y_{2^{i}},y_{2^{i}}^{\prime}\}
is an edge in
\mathcal{H}_{z_{W},r2^{i-2}}
. Thus
\{\psi(y),\psi(y^{\prime})\}
is an edge in
\mathcal{G}
.

Finally, we note that we can sample
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
by sampling every pair
\{y_{j},y_{j}^{\prime}\}
independently. Indeed, when sampling an edge in the link of
W
, we are conditioning on sampling some
s\in\mathcal{S}
so that for a fixed
(d-i)
-dimensional subspace
V_{0}
, the image of
s_{V_{0}}:\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V_{0}}}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
is fixed. For every
j\leqslant 2^{i}-1
, there exists some
v+V_{0}
such that
\{y_{j},y_{j}^{\prime}\}
depend only on the values of
v^{\prime}\in V_{0}
(this is the
v+V_{0}
such that
y_{j},y_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq s_{V_{0}}(v+V_{0})
). For the final component
\{y_{2^{i}},y_{2^{i}}^{\prime}\}
, this edge only depends on
s
restricted to
(0+V_{0})\setminus\{0\}
. By
Claim
7.30
, the values of
s
on the respective parts in each coset
v+V_{0}
are independent once all values of
s_{V_{0}}
have been fixed. We conclude that indeed
\psi
is an isomorphism.
∎

We conclude this subsection with the proofs of
Claim
7.27
,
Claim
7.29
and
Claim
7.30
.

Proof of
Claim
7.27
.

We will prove something even stronger. With the notation in the claim, we show that there exists some
A\in GL_{i}(\mathbb{F}_{2})
such that
s_{2}(x)=s_{1}(Ax)
23
23

23

Note that a priori
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
could have different domains, so technically
A
is an isomorphism between the domain of
s_{1}
and that of
s_{2}
.
. Let
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s_{1}})=Im(\widehat{s_{2}})=W
and such that without loss of generality the domain of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
is
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}
. Observe that by
Claim
7.24
,
\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s}(y)\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s}(y)y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1}\right.y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1\right\})=s(x)
, since the subset on the left consists of direct sums of a bunch of
s(x^{\prime})
, and
s(x)
is the only common component in these sums of
\widehat{s}(y)
.

Now assume that
Im(\widehat{s_{2}})=Im(\widehat{s_{1}})
. This implies that there is an isomorphism
B
such that
\widehat{s_{1}}(x)=\widehat{s_{2}}(Bx)
. On the other hand, by the above

\displaystyle s_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s_{1}}(y)\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s_{1}}(y)y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1}\right.y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1\right\})

\displaystyle=\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s_{2}}(By)\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s_{2}}(By)y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1}\right.y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,y\rangle=1\right\})

\displaystyle=\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s_{2}}(By)\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s_{2}}(By)By\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,B^{-1}By\rangle=1}\right.By\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,B^{-1}By\rangle=1\right\})

\displaystyle\overset{y^{\prime}=By}{=}\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s_{2}}(y^{\prime})\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s_{2}}(y^{\prime})y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,B^{-1}y^{\prime}\rangle=1}\right.y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle x,B^{-1}y^{\prime}\rangle=1\right\})

\displaystyle=\textup{Meet}(\left\{\widehat{s_{2}}(y^{\prime})\left|\;\vphantom{\widehat{s_{2}}(y^{\prime})y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle(B^{-1})^{\top}x,y^{\prime}\rangle=1}\right.y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i},\langle(B^{-1})^{\top}x,y^{\prime}\rangle=1\right\})

\displaystyle=s_{2}((B^{-1})^{\top}x).

Set
A=(B^{-1})^{\top}
and we got
s_{1}(x)=s_{2}(Ax)
.
∎

We note that the above proof used the intersection patterns of the Hadamard code.

Proof of
Claim
7.29
.

Let
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
U^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s}|_{U^{\prime}})=W^{\prime}
and let
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})=W
. Write
V=V^{\prime}\oplus\mathrm{span}\{b\}
such that
U^{\prime}=V^{\prime\bot}
and
U=V^{\bot}
. We recall that every
v+V\in\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}}
can be partitioned into two cosets of
V^{\prime}
,
v+V=v+V^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}(v+b)+V^{\prime}
and therefore
s_{V}(v+V)=s_{V^{\prime}}(v+V^{\prime})+s_{V^{\prime}}((v+b)+V^{\prime})
. By the fact that
s_{V^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
this sum is also direct.

We first prove the only if direction. Let
x\in U^{\prime}\setminus U
be such that
\widehat{s}(x)=y
. By
Claim
7.8
, it also holds that
\widehat{s_{V^{\prime}}}(x)=y
. By the fact that
x\in U^{\prime}\setminus U
, the inner product
\langle x,b\rangle=1
and therefore,
\langle x,v+V^{\prime}\rangle\neq\langle x,(v+b)+V^{\prime}\rangle
. This implies that for every
v_{0}+V\neq 0+V
, exactly one of
y_{j}\in\{s_{V^{\prime}}(v_{0}+V^{\prime}),s_{V^{\prime}}((v_{0}+b)+V^{\prime})\}
satisfies
y_{j}\leqslant y
. Indeed:

\displaystyle y=\widehat{s_{V^{\prime}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v+V^{\prime}\not\subseteq 0+V}\langle x,v+V^{\prime}\rangle s_{V^{\prime}}(v+V^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v_{0}\mid v_{0}+V\neq 0+V}\left(\langle x,v_{0}+V^{\prime}\rangle s_{V^{\prime}}(v_{0}+V^{\prime})+\langle x,(v_{0}+b)+V^{\prime}\rangle s_{V^{\prime}}(v_{0}+b+V^{\prime})\right).

Assume that
\langle x,v_{0}+V^{\prime}\rangle=1
and thus
\langle x,(v_{0}+b)+V^{\prime}\rangle=0
(the other case is similar). In this case, because this whole sum is a direct sum every non-zero component in the sum is dominated by
y
. In particular
s_{V^{\prime}}(v_{0}+V^{\prime})\leqslant y
. Moreover, any component that does not appear in this sum is a direct sum with
y
, and in particular
s_{V^{\prime}}(v_{0}+b+V^{\prime})\not\leqslant y
.

Thus we re-index
z_{j}=s_{V}(v+V)
(arbitrarily) and set
y_{j}
to be the component where
y_{j}\leqslant y
and
y_{j}\leqslant s_{V}(v+V)=z_{j}
. We also set
y_{2^{i}}=s_{V^{\prime}}(b)
and note that
y_{2^{i}}
also participates in the linear combination of
\widehat{s_{V^{\prime}}}(x)
because
\langle x,b\rangle=1
. All
y_{j}
’s have rank
2^{d-i-1}
and by the discussion above, we have that
y=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{i}}y_{j}
. Moreover,
y_{j}\leqslant z_{j}
for every
j\leqslant 2^{i}-1
and
y_{2^{i}}
is a direct sum with the sum of
z_{j}
’s. Thus,
Q_{W^{\prime}}=\left\{s_{V^{\prime}}(v+V^{\prime})\left|\;\vphantom{s_{V^{\prime}}(v+V^{\prime})v+V^{\prime}\neq V^{\prime}}\right.v+V^{\prime}\neq V^{\prime}\right\}=\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}}\cup\{z_{j}-y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}
by construction. Finally, we note that the when the admissible functions come from a rank-based construction that has the direct-meet property, then
y_{j}=\textup{Meet}(y,s_{V}(v_{0}+V))
for some
v_{0}+V
. Thus, the
y_{j}
’s are unique.

Conversely, let us assume that the three items hold and prove that
W^{\prime}\in Y(i+1)
. Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s})=W
. Recall that
\mathcal{S}_{i+1}
consists of all functions
s^{\prime}
whose image contains vectors of rank
2^{d-i}
whose sum is a direct sum. Therefore, let us construct such a function
s^{\prime}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i+1}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
satisfying
Im(\widehat{s})=W^{\prime}
. First, we re-index the elements
y_{j}
as
y_{v}
’s for every
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}
(but keep the notation
y_{2^{i}}
as before) so that
y_{v}\leqslant s(v)
. For an input
(b,v)\in(\mathbb{F}_{2}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i})\setminus\{0\}
,

s^{\prime}(b,v)=\begin{cases}s(v)-y_{v}&b=0,v\neq 0\\
y_{v}&b=1,v\neq 0\\
y_{2^{i}}&b=1,v=0\end{cases}.

It is easy to see that the image of
s^{\prime}
is
\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}}\cup\{z_{j}-y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}
, and these vectors form a direct sum and they all have rank
2^{d-i-1}
. Thus we successfully construct a
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{i+1}
. Moreover, one readily verifies that
\widehat{s^{\prime}}(1,\vec{0})=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}s^{\prime}(1,v)=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{i}}y_{j}=y
and that for
U^{\prime\prime}=\left\{(0,v)\left|\;\vphantom{(0,v)v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}\right.v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\right\}
we have that
Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}}|_{U^{\prime\prime}})=W
. Thus
Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}})=W^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
7.30
.

It suffices to prove that
P_{i}
is independent of
P_{[m]\setminus\{i\}}
where
P_{[m]\setminus\{i\}}
denotes the rest of the random variables. For any
i=1,2,\dots,m-1
, the distribution for
P_{i}
is uniform over all partial functions
s_{0}:R_{i}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}
satisfying (1)
\forall v\in R_{i}

\rank(s_{0}(v))=r
, and (2)
\bigoplus_{v\in R_{i}}s_{0}(v_{i})=z_{i}
. This follows from the fact that for
any fixed conditioning
of the form
P_{j}=s|_{(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\})\setminus R_{i}}
where the image of
P_{j}
sum up to
z_{j}
for all
j\neq i
, all such functions
s_{0}
have equal probability density.

For
i=m
, a similar argument applies. Conditioned on
P_{1}=s|_{R_{1}},P_{2}=s|_{R_{2}},\dots,P_{m-1}=s|_{R_{m-1}}
(where the sum of each
s|_{R_{i}}
’s values equals
z_{i}
), the last partial function
s|_{R_{m}}
is sampled by picking a uniformly random (ordered) set of rank
r
elements
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{|R_{m}|}
satisfying that
\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m-1}z_{j}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{|R_{m}|}t_{j}\right)
. It is obvious that the distribution of
P_{m}
is independent of the specific functions assigned to
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{m-1}
.
∎

7.4
The distribution over admissible functions

We can extend the decomposition lemma above to a slightly more general setup. In this setup we sample
s\in\mathcal{S}
according to a specified distribution instead of sampling it uniformly. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a set of admissible functions. Let
\mu
be a probability density function of
\mathcal{S}
. We say that
\mu
is permutation invariant if for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
and every permutation
\pi
of
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mu(s)=\mu(s\circ\pi)
. Such a
\mu
induces the following measure over the
d
-dimensional subspaces
Y^{\mathcal{S}}(d)
in the rank-based induced poset: first sample
s\sim\mu
, and then outputs
Im(\widehat{s})
. We call posets obtained by this process
measured rank-based posets
.

The sparsified matrix poset is an example of a measured rank-based posets, where the distribution
\mu
of admissible functions is given by the following:

1.

Sample
W_{1},W_{2}\in Y_{0}(2^{d}-1)
independently.

2.

Sample
s
uniformly at random such that
\row(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v))=W_{1}
and
\col(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v))=W_{2}
.

For general
Y_{0}
, this distribution
\mu
is nonuniform, and the resulting poset is a measured rank-based poset.
Hence this more generalized definition captures the sparsified matrix poset in
Definition
3.8
.

For such posets we can prove a version of the decomposition lemma using the following variants of the graphs from
Definition
7.26
.

Definition 7.31

(
(z,\mu)
-link graph)
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be of rank
2^{d}-2^{i}
and let
m\leqslant 2^{i-2}
. The graph
\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{z}
has vertices

V=\left\{y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\left|\;\vphantom{y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\rank(y)=2m,y\oplus z}\right.\rank(y)=2m,y\oplus z\right\},

E=\left\{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\left|\;\vphantom{\{w_{1}\oplus w_{2},w_{1}\oplus w_{3}\}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\rank(w_{i})=m,z\oplus w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}}\right.\rank(w_{i})=m,z\oplus w_{1}\oplus w_{2}\oplus w_{3}\right\}.

The edge distribution is given by: first sample a random vector
s\sim\mu
conditioned on
t:=\bigoplus_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)\geqslant z
. Then uniformly sample a
z^{\prime}\leqslant t
of rank
4m
and finally output an edge in
\mathcal{T}^{z^{\prime}}
according to the distribution in that graph.

Lemma 7.32
.

Let
Y^{\mathcal{S}}
be a measured rank-based poset with the direct-meet property. Then for every
W\in Y^{\mathcal{S}}(i)
with
Q_{W}=\{z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{2^{i}-1}\}
and
z_{W}=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{i}-1}z_{j}
defined as before,

Y^{\mathcal{S}}_{W}\cong\mathcal{T}^{z_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}^{z_{2^{i}-1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mu}_{z_{W},r2^{i-2}}.

The proof of this lemma follows the exact same argument in the proof of the decomposition lemma
Lemma
7.28
. Therefore we omit the proof for brevity.

Appendix A
The decomposition lemma

Proof of
Lemma
2.8
.

Let us denote by
A_{G},A_{t}
the adjacency operators of
G,G_{t}
respectively. We also denote by
A_{\mathcal{H}}
the
bipartite
adjacency operator of
B_{\tau}
. That is, for a function
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
,
A_{\mathcal{H}}f:T\to\mathbb{R}
is the function
A_{\mathcal{H}}f(t)=\E_{{v\sim\mu_{t}}}\left[f(v)\right]
. We note that by assumption, for every
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\E_{{v}}\left[f(v)\right]=0
,
\lVert A_{\mathcal{H}}f\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lambda_{2}^{2}\lVert f\rVert^{2}
.

Let
f:V\to\mathbb{R}
be an eigenvector of
A_{G}
of norm
1
and denote its eigenvalue by
\mu
. Then

\displaystyle\lvert\mu\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\langle f,A_{G}f\rangle\rvert=\E_{{\{u,v\}\sim\mu_{G}}}\left[f(u)f(v)\right]

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[\E_{{\{u,v\}\sim\mu_{t}}}\left[f(u)f(v)\right]\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[\langle f_{t},A_{t}f_{t}\rangle\right]\right\rvert

where
f_{t}
is the restriction of
f
to the vertices of
V_{t}
. Let us denote
f_{t}=f_{t}^{0}\cdot{\mathbf{1}}+f_{t}^{\perp}
such that
f_{t}^{0}\cdot{\mathbf{1}}
is a constant and
f_{t}^{\perp}\perp f_{t}^{0}\cdot{\mathbf{1}}
. Then by expansion,

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[(f_{t}^{0})^{2}+\gamma\langle f_{t}^{\perp},f_{t}^{\perp}\rangle\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[(1-\gamma)(f_{t}^{0})^{2}+\gamma\langle f_{t},f_{t}\rangle\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[(f_{t}^{0})^{2}\right]\right\rvert+\gamma\left\lvert\E_{{v\sim V}}\left[f(v)^{2}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\left\lvert\E_{{t\in\mu_{T}}}\left[(f_{t}^{0})^{2}\right]\right\rvert+\gamma.

Here first equality is algebra, the second is because
\mu_{G}(v)f(v)^{2}=\sum_{v\in t}\mu_{T}(t)\mu_{t}(v)f(v)^{2}
and the last equality is due to the fact that
f
has norm
1
. Finally, we note that the constant
f_{t}^{0}=\E_{{v\sim\mu_{t}}}\left[f(v)\right]=A_{\mathcal{H}}f(t)
. That is

=(1-\gamma)\lVert A_{\mathcal{H}}f\rVert^{2}+\gamma\overset{(\lVert A_{\mathcal{H}}f\rVert^{2}\leqslant\lambda_{2}^{2})}{\leqslant}\gamma+(1-\gamma)\lambda_{2}^{2}.

∎

Appendix B
Intermediate subspaces of the expanding matrix Grassmann poset

In this appendix we prove that the intermediate subspaces in the Grassmann matrix poset come from level
i
-admissible functions.

Claim

(Restatement of
Claim
3.9
)
.

Let
Y^{\prime}
be as in
Definition
3.8
. Then

Y^{\prime}(i)=\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}.

For this proof we recall the preliminaries in
Section
7
on quotient spaces and bilinear forms.

Proof of
Claim
3.9
.

Fix
{d}
and
{d^{\prime}}=\log{d}
. It is enough to prove that
W\in\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}
if and only if there exists
W^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}({d^{\prime}})
such that
W^{\prime}\supseteq W
(i.e. if and only if
W\in Y^{\prime}(i)
). We start with some
W\in Y^{\prime}(i)
and prove that
W\in\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}
. Let
W^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}({d^{\prime}})
be such that
w^{\prime}\supseteq w
. Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s})=W^{\prime}
. The matrices in the image of
s
are linearly independent
24
24

24
