Here we say matrices are linearly independent if they are linear indpendent as vectors in
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n^{2}}
.
since their sum has maximal rank. Hence
\widehat{s}:\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d^{\prime}}\to W^{\prime}
is an isomorphism. From this there exists some
i
-dimensional subspace
U\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}}
so that
W=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
. We write
U=V^{\bot}
for some
(d-i)
-dimensional subspace
V
(with respect to the standard bilinear form). Let us define
s^{\prime}:(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}})\setminus\{[0]\}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
as

s^{\prime}(v+V)=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}s(v^{\prime}).

It is direct to check that
s^{\prime}
is admissible, since every
\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}s(v^{\prime})
is a disjoint sum of
2^{{d^{\prime}}-i}
rank one matrices, and the sum
M_{s^{\prime}}=\sum_{v+V\neq 0+V}s^{\prime}(v+V)
is also equal to

M_{s^{\prime}}=\sum_{v\notin V}s(v)

which has rank
2^{d}-2^{d-i}
and whose row and column spaces are in
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
respectively, since it is contained in the row and column space of
M_{s}=\sum_{v\neq 0}s(v)
. We can also define
\widehat{s^{\prime}}:U\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n\times n}
as

\widehat{s^{\prime}}(x)=\sum_{v+V\in\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d^{\prime}}}}{{V}}\setminus\{[0]\}}\langle x,v+V\rangle s^{\prime}(v+V).

Here the inner product is the derived inner product as in
Section
2.2
. We note that

\widehat{s^{\prime}}(x)=\widehat{s}(x)
because

\displaystyle\widehat{s^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v+V\in(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}})\setminus\{[0]\}}\langle x,v+V\rangle s^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v+V\in(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}})\setminus\{[0]\}}\langle x,v+V\rangle\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}s(v)

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}\sum_{v+V\in(\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{2}^{d}}}{{V}})\setminus\{[0]\}}\sum_{v^{\prime}\in v+V}\langle x,v^{\prime}\rangle s(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}}\setminus\{0\}}\langle x,v^{\prime}\rangle s(v^{\prime})=\widehat{s}(x)

where
(*)
is because the derived inner product
\langle x,v+V\rangle
is equal to (the standard) inner product
\langle x,v^{\prime}\rangle
for every
v^{\prime}\in v+V
. Even though the function
s^{\prime}
is defined over
U
which is not equal to
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}
, and
\widehat{s}
is defined through the derived bilinear form (not the standard one), by
Claim
7.1
we can find an admissible
s^{\prime\prime}
so that
Im(\widehat{s^{\prime\prime}})=Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}})
. This proves that
w\in\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}
.

As for the converse direction, ‘reverse engineer’ the proof above. Let
W\in\left\{Im(\widehat{s})\left|\;\vphantom{Im(\widehat{s})s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}\right.s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}\right\}
, and we show that
W\in Y^{\prime}(i)
. Let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
be such that
Im(\widehat{s})=W
. Let
M_{s}=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}s(v)
and let
W_{1}\in(Y_{1})_{\row(M_{s})}({d}-1),W_{2}\in(Y_{2})_{\col(M_{s})}({d}-1)
. These spaces exist from the purity of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. We define
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{{d^{\prime}}}
as follows. For every
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}
we (arbitrarily) find a decomposition of
s(v)
into rank one matrices, and index them by
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}
. That is,
s(v)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}}e_{x}\otimes f_{x}
. We set
s^{\prime}(v,x)=e_{x}\otimes f_{x}
. For
v=0\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}
we define
s^{\prime}
using
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. We find space
W^{\prime}_{1}
and
W^{\prime}_{2}
such that
\row(S)\oplus W^{\prime}_{j}=W_{j}
and ordered bases
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{2^{{d^{\prime}}-i}-1}
to
W_{1}
and
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{2^{{d^{\prime}}-i}-1}
to
W_{2}
. We (arbitrarily) re-index these bases as
\left\{e_{x}\left|\;\vphantom{e_{x}x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}\setminus 0}\right.x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}\setminus 0\right\}
and
\left\{f_{x}\left|\;\vphantom{f_{x}x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}\setminus 0}\right.x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}\setminus 0\right\}
and set
s^{\prime}(0,x)=e_{x}\otimes f_{x}
. It is a direct definition chase to verify that
s^{\prime}
is admissible, i.e. that
M_{s^{\prime}}=\sum_{(v,x)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}}\setminus\{0\}}s^{\prime}(v,x)
has rank
2^{d^{\prime}}-1
and its row and column spaces are equal to
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
respectively. Finally, the image of
\widehat{s^{\prime}}
restricted to
U=\left\{(x,0)\left|\;\vphantom{(x,0)x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}}\right.x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\right\}
is
w
since

\displaystyle\widehat{s^{\prime}}(x,0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\times\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}\setminus\{(0,0)\}}\langle(x,0),(v,v^{\prime})\rangle s^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}\langle x,v\rangle\sum_{v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{{d^{\prime}}-i}}s^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{i}\setminus\{0\}}\langle x,v\rangle s(v)=\widehat{s}(x).

Thus
W^{\prime}=Im(\widehat{s^{\prime}})\in Y^{\prime}({d^{\prime}})
and it contains
W=Im(\widehat{s}|_{U})
, so
W\in Y^{\prime}(i)
.
∎

Appendix C
Elementary properties of matrix poset

C.1
Domination is a partial order

Let us begin by showing that this relation is a partial order. We do so using this claim.

Proof of
Claim
2.18
.

The implications of
(4)\Rightarrow(2)
and
(3)\Rightarrow(2)
are a direct consequence of the definitions. Let us see that
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
.

\displaystyle\rank(A+B)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}(\row(A+B))

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathrm{dim}(\row(A)+\row(B))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}(\row(A))+\mathrm{dim}(\row(B))-\mathrm{dim}(\row(A)\cap\row(B))

\displaystyle=\rank(A)+\rank(B)-\mathrm{dim}(\row(A)\cap\row(B)).

This shows that if
\rank(A+B)=\rank(A)+\rank(B)
then
\row(A)\cap\row(B)=\{0\}
(and similarly for the column space).

Finally, let us see that
(2)\Rightarrow(1),(3),(4)
. Assume that
\row(A)\cap\row(B),\col(A)\cap\col(B)
are trivial. Let us write
r_{1}=\rank(A),r_{2}=\rank(B)
. Then we can write
A=\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}e_{i}\otimes f_{i}
and
B=\sum_{i=1}^{r_{2}}g_{i}\otimes h_{i}
and because the row and column spaces intersect trivially, we have that
\{e_{i}\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{g_{i}\}
and
\{f_{i}\}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{h_{i}\}
are
r_{1}+r_{2}
sized sets that are independent, thus proving
(4)
(which implies
(3)
). This also shows that
\rank(A+B)=\mathrm{dim}(\col(A+B))=\rank(A)+\rank(B)
, thus proving
(1)
.

∎

Proof of
Claim
2.19
.

By
Claim
2.18
,
X\oplus Y
if and only if
\col(X)\cap\col(Y)
and
\row(X)\cap\row(Y)
are
\{0\}
. So first of all note that if
C\oplus(B\oplus A)
then
\row(C)\cap\row(B\oplus A)=\{0\}
(and the same for the column space). By
Claim
2.18

\row(B\oplus A)=\row(B)\oplus\row(A)
, so in particular
\row(C)\cap\row(B)=\{0\}
(resp.
\col(C)\cap\col(B)=\{0\}
). Thus,
C\oplus B
.

Now let us show that
A\oplus(B\oplus C)
. By
Claim
2.10
, if
\row(A)\oplus\row(B)
and
\row(C)\oplus(\row(A)\oplus\row(B))
then
\row(A)\oplus(\row(B)\oplus\row(C))
(resp. column spaces). Thus by
Claim
2.18

A\oplus(B\oplus C)
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.17
.

We need to verify reflexivity, anti-symmetry and transitivity. Let
A,B,C
be matrices.

1.

Reflexivity is clear.

2.

Anti-symmetry: assume that
A\leqslant B
and
B\leqslant A
this implies that
\rank(B-A)=\rank(A)-\rank(B)=\rank(B)-\rank(A)=0
so
A=B
.

3.

Transitivity:
C\leqslant B
and
B\leqslant A
. Then by
Claim
2.18
,
\row(A)=\row(B)\oplus\row(A-B)
. In addition this claim shows that
\row(B-C)\subseteq\row(B)
. Together this implies that
\row(B-C)
intersects trivially with
\row(A-B)
. The same holds also for the column spaces. Thus by
Claim
2.18
this implies that
\rank(A-C)=\rank(A-B)+\rank(B-C)
, i.e.
A-C\geqslant A-B
. Thus,

\displaystyle\rank(A)

\displaystyle\overset{(B\leqslant A)}{=}\rank(B)+\rank(A-B)

\displaystyle\overset{(C\leqslant B)}{=}(\rank(C)+\rank(C-B))+\rank(A-B)

\displaystyle\overset{(A-B\leqslant A-C)}{=}(\rank(C)+\rank(C-B))+(\rank(A-C)-\rank(B-C))

\displaystyle=\rank(C)+\rank(A-C).

∎

Remark C.1
.

Recall the following definition from partially ordered set theory.

Definition C.2

(Rank function)
.

Let
(A,\leqslant)
be a poset. A
rank function

\rho:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{N}
is a function with the following properties.

1.

If
A>B
then
\rho(A)>\rho(B)
.

2.

For every
A>B
there exists
A\geqslant C>B
so that
\rho(C)=\rho(B)+1
.

A poset is called a
graded poset
if there exists a rank function for that poset.

The order of matrices is also a graded poset, where the matrix
\rank
is the rank function. To see this, we note that if
A\leqslant B
, then
\rank(A)=\rank(B)+\rank(A-B)
so in particular
\rank(A)\geqslant\rank(B)
and if
A\neq B
,
\rank(A)>\rank(B)
. Moreover, if
A>B
then by the fourth item of
Claim
2.18
, we can write
A=B\oplus\left(\sum_{j=1}^{t}e_{j}\otimes f_{j}\right)
for some
t>0
. Taking
C=B\oplus(e_{j}\otimes f_{j})
concludes that the
\rank
function (of matrices) is a rank function (of the graded poset).

C.2
Isomorphism of intervals

We begin with this claim.

Claim C.3
.

1.

Let
A\leqslant B,C
. Then
B\leqslant C
if and only if
B-A\leqslant C-A
.

2.

Let
B,C
be such that
B\oplus A
and
C\oplus A
. Then
B\leqslant C
if and only if
B\oplus A\leqslant C\oplus A
.

Proof.

For the first item,

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\leqslant C

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow rank(C)=rank(B)+rank(C-B)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow rank(C)-rank(A)=rank(B)-rank(A)+rank(C-B)

\displaystyle\overset{(A\leqslant B,C)}{\Leftrightarrow}rank(C-A)=rank(B-A)+rank((C-A)-(B-A))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow B-A\leqslant C-A.

For the second item,

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\leqslant C

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow rank(C)=rank(B)+rank(C-B)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow rank(C)+rank(A)=rank(B)+rank(A)+rank(C-B)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow rank(C\oplus A)=rank(B\oplus A)+rank((C\oplus A)-(B\oplus A))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow B\oplus A\leqslant C\oplus A.

∎

Proof of
Proposition
2.21
.

By
Claim
C.3
the bijection
\psi
maps
A
such that
A\leqslant M_{2}
to
A^{\prime}=A-M_{1}
such that
A^{\prime}\leqslant M_{2}-M_{1}
. Therefore
\psi
is well defined as a function from
I(M_{1},M_{2})
to
I(0,M_{2}-M_{1})
. Obviously
\psi
is injective (since it is injective over the set of all matrices). Moreover, if
M_{1}\leqslant A_{1}\leqslant A_{2}\leqslant M_{2}
then by
Claim
C.3
the order is maintained, i.e.
A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{1}-M_{1}\leqslant A_{2}-M_{1}=A_{2}^{\prime}
. The fact that
rank(A^{\prime})=rank(A)-rank(M_{1})
follows from
M_{1}\leqslant A
. Thus to conclude we need to show that
\psi
is surjective, or in other words, if
A^{\prime}\leqslant M_{2}-M_{1}
then
A^{\prime}+M_{1}\leqslant M_{2}
. Let
A^{\prime}\leqslant M_{2}-M_{1}
. Then by
Claim
2.18

\row(A^{\prime})+\row(M_{2}-M_{1}-A)=\row(M_{2}-M_{1})
and in particular
\row(A^{\prime})\subseteq\row(M_{2}-M_{1})
and similarly
\col(A^{\prime})\subseteq\col(M_{2}-M_{1})
. By
M_{1}\leqslant M_{2}
, we have that
\rank(M_{2})=\rank(M_{1})+\rank(M_{2}-M_{1})
and by
Claim
2.18
this implies that
\row(M_{2}-M_{1})\cap\row(M_{1})=\{0\}
and similarly for the columns spaces. We conclude that
\row(A^{\prime})\cap\row(M_{1})
and
\col(A^{\prime})\cap\col(M_{1})
are trivial. By using
Claim
2.18
we have that for
A=\psi^{-1}(A^{\prime})=A^{\prime}+M_{1}
,

\rank(A)=\rank(A^{\prime})+\rank(M_{1})

or equivalently that
A\geqslant M_{1}
. Finally, we also have that
A\leqslant M_{2}
by
Claim
C.3
(as
A,M_{2}\geqslant M_{1}
and
A^{\prime}=A-M_{1}\leqslant M_{2}-M_{1}
). Thus
\psi
is surjective since
A
is a preimage of
A^{\prime}
inside
\left\{A\left|\;\vphantom{AM_{1}\leqslant A\leqslant M_{2}}\right.M_{1}\leqslant A\leqslant M_{2}\right\}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
2.23
.

Fix
A
and note that
B\oplus A
if and only if
A\leqslant A+B
. Thus
\phi
is a bijection. The order preservation follows from the second item of
Claim
C.3
.
∎

C.3
The matrices have the direct-meet property

In this subsection we prove
Claim
7.23
.

Proof of
Claim
7.23
.

The technical statement we shall use in the proof is the following: For any matrices
A,B\leqslant C
such that
\row(A)\subseteq\row(B)
and
\col(A)\subseteq\col(B)
, it holds that
A\leqslant B
.

Let us first show that this statement implies the direct meet property. Let
M_{1},M_{2},M_{3}
be three matrices whose sum is direct. We observe that
\row(M_{1})=\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\cap\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{3})
25
25

25

It is clear that
\row(M_{1})\subseteq\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\cap\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{3})
. The other direction follows from a dimension count.
and similarly
\col(M_{1})=\col(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\cap\col(M_{1}\oplus M_{3})
. Consider any
A
such that
A\leqslant M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
and
A\leqslant M_{1}\oplus M_{3}
. Observe that
\row(A)\leqslant\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\cap\row(M_{1}\oplus M_{3})
. Thus
\row(A)\subseteq\row(M_{1})
. The same argument applies to the respective column spaces, and so by the technical statement above
A\leqslant M_{1}
. This implies that the set
G_{\mathcal{M}}
has the direct meet property.

Now we prove the statement itself. Observe that
\row(B-A)\subseteq\row(B)
and
\col(B-A)\subseteq\col(B)
. Moreover,
(C-B)\oplus B
which means that
\row(C-B)
(respectively
\col(C-B)
) intersects trivially with
\row(B)
(respectively
\col(B)
). Thus
(C-B)\oplus(B-A)
. So we get

\rank(C-A)=\rank((C-B)+(B-A))=\rank(C-B)+\rank(B-A).

On the other hand, because
A,B\leqslant C
, the equality above can be rewritten as

\rank(C)-\rank(A)=\rank(C)-\rank(B)+\rank(B-A)

or equivalently
\rank(B)=\rank(A)+\rank(B-A)
i.e.
A\leqslant B
.
∎

Appendix D
Properties of the graphs induced by the matrix poset

In this appendix we prove
Lemma
3.15
and
Claim
3.23
necessary to prove the expansion of the complexes constructed in
Section
3
. We also prove
Claim
6.32
necessary for the coboundary expansion proven in
Section
6
. To do so, we need to take a technical detour and provide some further definitions that generalize the graphs in said statements.
In
Section
3
we defined some graphs that naturally arise from the relations
\leqslant
and
\oplus
. To analyze them, we need to generalize these definitions.

Definition D.1

(Relation graph)
.

Let
i<j\leqslant n
. The relation graph
\mathcal{R}(i,j)=(L,R,E)
is the bipartite graph whose vertices are
L=\mathcal{M}(i),R=\mathcal{M}(j)
and
\{A,B\}\in E
if
A\leqslant B
.

This is the analogous graph to the ‘down-up’ graph in simplicial complexes, i.e. the graph whose vertices are
X(i)
and
X(j)
(for some simplicial complex
X
) and whose edges are all pairs
\{s,t\}
such that
s\subseteq t
(see e.g.
[
DK17
]
).

Another important graph is the following ‘sum graph’.

Definition D.2

(Sum graph)
.

Let
i,j,k
be integers such that
k\leqslant i,j
and
i+j-k\leqslant n
. The Sum graph
\mathcal{S}(i,j,k)=(L,R,E)
is the bipartite graph whose vertices are
L=\mathcal{M}(i),R=\mathcal{M}(j)
and
\{A,B\}\in E
if there exists
C\in\mathcal{M}(k)
such that
C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)
are a direct sum.

When
k=0
we denote this graph by
\mathcal{DS}(i,j)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}(i,j,0)
. In this case its definition simplifies:
A\sim B
if and only if
A\oplus B
.

All the aforementioned graphs turn up when one analyzes the Grassmann graph defined in
Section
3
. However, the bulk of the analysis in
Section
3
is on
links
of the Grassmann complex. There the graphs that appear are actually subgraphs of these graphs. Hence, we also make the following definitions of the graphs where we take
\mathcal{M}^{U}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}
as our underlying posets instead of all
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition D.3

(Upper bounded relation graph)
.

Let
i<j\leqslant\ell\leqslant n
. Let
U\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The graph
\mathcal{R}^{U}(i,j)=(L,R,E)
is the bipartite graph whose vertices are
L=\mathcal{M}^{U}(i),R=\mathcal{M}^{U}(j)
and
\{A,B\}\in E
if
A\leqslant B
.

Note that for every
U_{1},U_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
, the graphs
\mathcal{R}^{U_{1}}(i,j),\mathcal{R}^{U_{2}}(i,j)
are isomorphic. So when we do not care about the matrix we write
\mathcal{R}^{\ell}(i,j)
instead of
\mathcal{R}^{U}(i,j)
.

The graphs
\mathcal{DS}^{U}(i,j)
and
\mathcal{S}^{U}(i,j,k)
are defined analogously as the subgraphs of matrices in
\mathcal{M}^{U}
:

Definition D.4

(Upper bounded sum graph)
.

Let
i,j,k,\ell
be integers such that
k\leqslant i,j
and
i+j-k\leqslant\ell
. Let
U\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The Sum graph
\mathcal{S}^{U}(i,j,k)=(L,R,E)
is the bipartite graph whose vertices are
L=\mathcal{M}^{U}(i),R=\mathcal{M}^{U}(j)
and
\{A,B\}\in E
if there exists
C\in\mathcal{M}(k)
such that
C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\leqslant U
.

In these cases we also write
\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(i,j),\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(i,j,k)
when we do not care about
U
.

The second type of localization is with respect to subspaces.

Definition D.5

(Lower bounded local relation graph)
.

Let
i,j,\ell
be integers such that
i<j\leqslant n-\ell
. Let
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The graph
\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,j)
is the subgraph of
\mathcal{R}(i,j)
whose vertices are in
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}
.

Definition D.6

(Lower bounded sum graph)
.

Let
i,j,k,\ell
be integers such that
k\leqslant i,j
and
i+j-k\leqslant n-\ell
. Let
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The Sum graph
\mathcal{S}_{\cap D}(i,j,k)=(L,R,E)
is the bipartite graph whose vertices are
L=\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i),R=\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)
and
\{A,B\}\in E
if there exists
C\in\mathcal{M}(k)
such that
D\oplus C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)
(is a direct sum).

In the next section we prove upper bounds on the expansion of some of these graphs. In all the following claims
\gamma\in(0,1)
stands for universal constants which we don’t calculate (and may change between claims). The main lemmas we need are these.

Lemma D.7
.

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(i,j,k))\leqslant\gamma^{-(\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}
.

Lemma D.8
.

Let
i,j,k,\ell
be such that
i\leqslant j
and
2i\leqslant n-\ell
. Let
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. Then
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\cap D}(i,j,k))\leqslant\gamma^{-(n-\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}
.

In order to prove these lemmas we need to analyze the other graphs as well. More specifically, we will use
Lemma
2.8
to reduce the analysis of the
\mathcal{S}
graphs to the expansion of the
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{DS}
graphs. The bounds we need for
Lemma
D.7
are these.

Claim D.9
.

Let
i,j\leqslant\ell
. Then

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(i,j))\leqslant ij\cdot\gamma^{-(\ell-i-j)}
.

Claim D.10
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The graph
\mathcal{R}^{U}(i,j)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(i,\ell-j)
. Consequently by
Claim
D.9

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{R}^{\ell}(i,j))\leqslant\gamma^{-(j-i)}
.

For
Lemma
D.8
we will also need this claim.

Claim D.11
.

Let
i,j,\ell
be such that
i\leqslant\frac{j}{2}
and
n-\ell\geqslant j
. Let
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. Then
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,j))\leqslant\gamma^{j}
.

The proofs of all these claims are all quite technical, and while most of the proof ideas repeat themselves among claims, we did not find one proof from which to deduce all claims. More details are given in the next subsections. We conclude this sub section with the short proofs of
Lemma
3.15
and
Claim
3.23
, that were necessary to prove the main theorem in
Section
3
. They follow directly from
Lemma
D.7
and
Lemma
D.8
.

Proof of
Lemma
3.15
.

The double cover of
\mathcal{T}^{m}
is
\mathcal{S}^{4m}(2m,2m,m)
. Thus by
Lemma
D.7
and
Observation
2.7

\lambda(\mathcal{T}^{m})=\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{4m}(2m,2m,m))=\gamma^{m}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
3.23
.

Careful definition chasing reveals that the double cover of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W)
is (isomorphic to)
\mathcal{S}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m,2m,m)
. Indeed, assume for simplicity that
w_{1}=w_{2}=\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
. Thus the vertices in the double cover of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W)
are two copies of
\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m)
. This is because by
Claim
2.18
,
A\oplus S_{W}
if and only if
\row(A)\oplus\row(Z_{W}),\col(A)\oplus\col(Z_{W})
are a direct sum. Hence by
Observation
2.7
it is enough to argue about the bipartite expansion of
\mathcal{S}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m,2m,m)
.

We observe that
A\sim A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m,2m,m)
if and only if
A\sim A^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(W_{1}),\mathcal{G}(W_{2}),W)
. Matrices
A
and
A^{\prime}
are connected in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),U)
when there exists
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(4m)
such that
A\sim A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{U}(2m,2m,m)
. This is equivalent to existence of
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}\in\mathcal{M}(m)
such that
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{3}\oplus B_{4}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(4m)
with
A=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
and
A^{\prime}=B_{1}\oplus B_{3}
(the sum of
B_{i}
’s is
U
). Observe that
B_{4}
is redundant in this definition, that is, it is enough to require that there exists
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}\in\mathcal{M}(m)
such that
B_{1}\oplus B_{2}\oplus B_{3}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap Z_{W}}(3m)
with
A=B_{1}\oplus B_{2}
and
A^{\prime}=B_{1}\oplus B_{3}
. This is because if we are given these
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}
as so, we can always find a suitable
B_{4}
. By writing
B_{2}=A-B_{1}
and
B_{3}=A^{\prime}-B_{1}
, we get that
A,A^{\prime}
are connected in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W)
, if and only if
Z_{W}\oplus B_{1}\oplus(A-B_{1})\oplus(A^{\prime}-B_{1})
. This is exactly the definition of the edges in
\mathcal{S}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m,2m,m)
.

Thus by
Lemma
D.8

\lambda(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G}(w_{1}),\mathcal{G}(w_{2}),W))=\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\cap Z_{W}}(2m,2m,m))\leqslant\gamma^{(d_{0}-\mathrm{dim}(W)-3m)}=\gamma^{m}
.
∎

D.1
Expansion of the upper bound local graphs

We begin by showing how
Lemma
D.7
and
Lemma
D.8
reduce to the other claims by
Lemma
2.9
.

Proof of
Lemma
D.7
.

Fix
U\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
and
i,j,k
. We intend to use
Lemma
2.9
. We index our components by
\mathcal{M}(k)
, where every component
G_{C}
will be the graph containing edges
\{A,B\}
such that
C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\leqslant U
. We claim that
G_{C}\cong\mathcal{DS}^{U-C}(i-k,j-k)
, by the map
A\mapsto A-C
. Indeed, by
Proposition
2.21
the vertices in
G_{C}
, which are
\mathcal{M}^{U}_{\cap C}(i)\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\mathcal{M}^{U}_{\cap C}(j)
map isomorphically to the vertices of
\mathcal{DS}^{U-C}(i-k,j-k)
. Moreover, by
Claim
C.3

C\oplus(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\leqslant U
if and only if
(A-C)\oplus(B-C)\leqslant U-C
.

Thus
G_{C}\cong\mathcal{DS}^{U-C}(i-k,j-k)
and by
Claim
D.9
we conclude that
\lambda_{2}(G_{C})\leqslant\gamma^{(\ell-k-(j-k)-(i-k))}=\gamma^{(\ell-j-(i-k))}
.

The corresponding bipartite decomposition graph of (say) the right-side is the graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau,R}=(V,T,E)
given by:

1.

V=\mathcal{M}^{U}(j)
.

2.

T=\mathcal{M}^{U}(k)
.

3.

E=\left\{(A,C)\left|\;\vphantom{(A,C)A\geqslant C}\right.A\geqslant C\right\}
.

This is precisely
\mathcal{R}^{U}(j,k)
. By
Claim
D.10
this graph is an
\gamma^{(\ell-j-k)}
-spectral expander. Therefore by
Lemma
2.9
,
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}^{U}(i,j,k))\leqslant\gamma^{(\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
D.8
.

We use
Lemma
2.9
. Our components will be
G^{U}=\mathcal{S}^{U}(i,j,k)
for all
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(n-\ell)
. We observe that sampling an edge by sampling
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(n-\ell)
and then sampling an edge in
\mathcal{S}^{U}(i,j,k)
, results in a uniform distribution over the edges of
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i,j,k)
. By
Lemma
D.7
every such component has
\lambda_{2}(G^{U})\leqslant\gamma^{(n-\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}
.

We note that the (say) left side of the bipartite decomposition graph is the bipartite graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau,L}=(V,T,E)
with:

1.

V=\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
.

2.

T=\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(n-\ell)
.

3.

E=\left\{(B,U)\left|\;\vphantom{(B,U)B\leqslant U}\right.B\leqslant U\right\}
.

This is precisely
\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,n-\ell)
. By
Claim
D.11
,
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,n-\ell))\leqslant\gamma^{(n-\ell-i)}\leqslant\gamma^{(n-\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}
so by
Lemma
2.9
,

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\cap D}(i,j,k))\leqslant\gamma^{(n-\ell-j-\max(k,i-k))}.

∎

Let us also give a short proof for
Claim
D.10
.

Proof of
Claim
D.10
.

Fix
i,j,\ell
and
U\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. For notational convenience let
\mathcal{R}^{U}(i,j)=(L,R,E)
and
\mathcal{DS}^{U}(i,\ell-j)=(L^{\prime},R^{\prime},E^{\prime})
. We define

\phi:L\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}R\to L^{\prime}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}R^{\prime}
to be

\forall B\in L,\;\phi(B)=B\;\;\;\hbox{and}\;\;\;\forall A\in R,\;\phi(A)=U-A.

Let us first show that indeed
\phi
bijectively maps the vertices
L,R
to
L^{\prime},R^{\prime}
respectively. For
L
this is clear. Moreover,
A\leqslant U
if and only if
U-A\leqslant U
, and in this case
\rank(A)=j
if and only if
\rank(U-A)=\rank(U)-\rank(A)=\ell-j
. Thus
\phi
is a bijection of the vertex sets in every side.

Next we verify that
\{A,B\}\in E
if and only if
\{U-A,B\}\in E^{\prime}
. Indeed,
\{A,B\}\in E
if and only if
B\leqslant A\leqslant U
. By definition of
\oplus
, this is if and only if
A=B\oplus(A-B)
and
U=A\oplus(U-A)
, or more compactly,
B\oplus(A-B)\oplus(U-A)=U
. On the other hand,
B\oplus(U-A)\leqslant U
if and only if
B\oplus(U-A)\oplus(A-B)=U
, and thus
\{A,B\}\in E
if and only if
\{U-A,B\}\in E^{\prime}
. The isomorphism is proven.
∎

In the next subsections we give proofs to
Claim
D.9
and
Claim
D.11
, are more technical.

D.2
Bounding the expansion of the direct sum graphs

The proof of
Claim
D.9
is by induction on the sum of matrix ranks,
i+j
. We begin with the base case.

Claim D.12
.

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,1))\leqslant O(2^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell})
.

The base case follows from the fact that the walk in
\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,1)
is close to the uniform random walk as in
Corollary
2.4
.

Proof of
Claim
D.12
.

Without loss of generality let us assume that
U=I
, the identity matrix whose rank is
\ell
. For vectors
x,y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\ell}

\langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}x_{j}y_{j}
the standard bilinear form over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, which we sometimes abuse notation and call an ‘inner product’. Golowich observes the following

Claim D.13

(
[
Gol23
, Lemma 66]
)
.

Let
I
the identity matrix of rank
\ell
. A matrix
A\leqslant I
if and only if
A=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}e_{i}\otimes f_{i}
for some
\ell^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\ell
and
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}},\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
such that

\langle e_{i},f_{j}\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}.

Thus
\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,1)
is (isomorphic to) the bipartite graph where
L=\left\{(e,f)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\ell}\left|\;\vphantom{(e,f)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2\ell}\langle e,f\rangle=1}\right.\langle e,f\rangle=1\right\}
,
R
is a disjoint copy of
L
, and we connect
(e,f)\sim(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if and only if
\langle e,f^{\prime}\rangle=\langle e^{\prime},f\rangle=0
(i.e. we move from
e\otimes f
to
(e,f)
). We will use
Corollary
2.4
and show that the two step walk in this graph is
O(2^{-\ell})
-close to the uniform graph, thus proving that
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,1))\leqslant O(2^{-\frac{1}{2}\ell})
. For concreteness we will use the two step walk from
L
to
L
.

Let us fix an initial vertex
(e_{0},f_{0})
and let
\mu
be the distribution over vertices in the two step walk. Let
J
be the uniform distribution over vertices.
We want to show that
\lVert\mu-J\rVert=\sum_{v\in V}\lvert\mu(v)-\frac{1}{|V|}\rvert\leqslant O(2^{-\ell})
.

The basic idea is this: we prove below that the probability that we traverse from
(e_{0},f_{0})
to
(e_{2},f_{2})
essentially only depends on whether
e_{0}=e_{2}
, and whether
f_{0}=f_{2}
(and not on
e_{2}
or
f_{2}
themselves). While this is not true
per se
, it is true up to some negligible probability. Then we use the large regularity to bound the probability that
(e_{2},f_{2})
has
e_{0}=e_{2}
or
f_{0}=f_{2}
by
O(2^{-\ell})
. This asserts that
\mu
is (almost) uniform on a set that contains all but a negligible fraction of the vertices, which is enough to show closeness to
J
. Details follow.

We record for later the following observations.

Observation D.14
.

1.

If
(e,f)\in L
then
e,f\neq 0
.

2.

|L|=(2^{\ell}-1)2^{\ell-1}
.

3.

The graph is
D=(2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2}
-regular.

The first two observations are immediate, but we explain the third: fixing
(e,f)
, we count its neighbors
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
. There are
2^{\ell-1}-1
choices for
e^{\prime}
since it is perpendicular to
f
and non-zero. Given
e^{\prime}
, the vector
f^{\prime}
satisfies

\langle e^{\prime},f^{\prime}\rangle=1\;\;\;\hbox{and}\;\;\;\langle e,f^{\prime}\rangle=0.

Note that
e,e^{\prime}
are linearly independent since both are non-zero and
\langle e,f\rangle\neq\langle e^{\prime},f\rangle
. There are
2^{\ell-2}
solutions to these linear equations hence the graph is
(2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2}
-regular.

Let
(e_{2},f_{2})
be any vertex. The first crude bound we give on the is just by regularity. For any
(x,y)\in V
,
\mu(x,y)\leqslant\frac{1}{D}=\frac{1}{(2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2}}
. This bound is tight when
(e_{0},f_{0})=(e_{2},f_{2})
. Similarly, if either
e_{0}=e_{2}
or
f_{0}=f_{2}
then we also just bound
\mu(e_{0},f_{0})\leqslant\frac{1}{(2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2}}
; there are at most
2^{\ell}
such pairs.

Now let us assume
e_{2}\neq e_{0},f_{2}\neq f_{0}
and calculate more accurate upper and lower bounds on
\mu(e_{2},f_{2})
by counting how many two paths there are from
(e_{0},f_{0})
to
(e_{2},f_{2})
, or equivalently, how many
(e_{1},f_{1})\in R
are common neighbors of
(e_{0},f_{0}),(e_{2},f_{2})
.

Given
e_{0},e_{2}
, the vector
e_{1}
needs to satisfy
\langle f_{0},e_{1}\rangle=\langle f_{2},e_{1}\rangle=0
. The two vectors
f_{0},f_{2}
are linearly independent by assumption that they are both distinct and non-zero. Thus there are
2^{\ell-2}-1
non-zero
e_{1}
-s that satisfy
\langle f_{0},e_{1}\rangle=\langle f_{2},e_{1}\rangle=0
.

Fixing
e_{1}
the vector
f_{1}
needs to satisfy
\langle e_{0},f_{1}\rangle=\langle e_{2},f_{1}\rangle=0
and
\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle=1
. If
e_{0},e_{1},e_{2}
are linearly independent then there are exactly
2^{m-3}
solutions to these equations, all of them are non-zero, and every such solution corresponds to a common neighbor
(e_{1},f_{1})
. If they are not linearly independent we claim that there is no solution. We note that every pair must be linearly independent: All three are non-zero vectors. Moreover,
e_{0},e_{2}
are distinct vectors by assumption, and similarly
e_{1}\neq e_{0}
(resp.
e_{1}\neq e_{2}
) because
\langle f_{0},e_{0}\rangle\neq\langle f_{0},e_{1}\rangle
(resp.
\langle f_{2},e_{2}\rangle\neq\langle f_{2},e_{1}\rangle
). Every pair of distinct non-zero vectors are independent. Thus, if there is a dependency then
e_{1}=e_{0}+e_{2}
, and so the first two equations imply that
\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle=0
, so there is no solution.

We conclude that the number of paths from
(e_{0},f_{0})
to
(e_{2},f_{2})
is one of the following.

1.

(2^{\ell-2}-1)2^{\ell-3}=2^{2\ell-5}-2^{\ell-3}
- if
e_{0}+e_{2}
is not a valid solution in the first step, i.e. either
\langle f_{0},e_{0}+e_{2}\rangle\neq 0
or
\langle f_{2},e_{0}+e_{2}\rangle\neq 0
.

2.

(2^{\ell-2}-2)2^{\ell-3}=2^{2\ell-5}-2^{\ell-2}
otherwise.

We have that for every such pair,

\mu(e_{2},f_{2})=\frac{2^{2\ell-5}}{((2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2})^{2}}-\frac{2^{\ell-b}}{((2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2})^{2}},

for
b\in\{2,3\}
.
This implies that
\mu(e_{2},f_{2})=\frac{1}{|V|}+\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2}
for

\lvert\varepsilon_{2}\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{2^{\ell-b}}{((2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2})^{2}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2^{6-3\ell}

and

\displaystyle\lvert\varepsilon_{1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\lvert\frac{2^{2\ell-4}}{((2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2})^{2}}-\frac{1}{|V|}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{2(3\cdot 2^{\ell}-5)}{(2^{\ell}-2)^{2}(2^{\ell}-1)2^{\ell}}\leqslant 2^{6-3\ell}

Thus in particular,

\displaystyle\lVert\mu-J_{(e_{0},e_{2})}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{2}=e_{0}\vee f_{2}=f_{0}}\lvert\mu(e_{2},f_{2})-\frac{1}{|V|}\rvert+\sum_{e_{2}\neq e_{0}\;\hbox{and}\;f_{2}\neq f_{0}}\lvert\mu(e_{2},f_{2})-\frac{1}{|V|}\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2^{\ell}}{(2^{\ell-1}-1)2^{\ell-2}}+2^{2\ell}(\varepsilon_{1}+\varepsilon_{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{-\ell}(2^{4}+2^{7})\leqslant 2^{8}2^{-\ell}.

The claim now follows from
Corollary
2.4
.
∎

Now we prove
Claim
D.9
. As mentioned before, the proof is by induction on
i+j
. A similar induction was done in
[
DD19
]
when analyzing the so-called
swap walk
, a generalization of the Kneser graph to high dimensional expanders. In the inductive step, one uses
Lemma
2.9
to reduce the analysis of
(i,j)
to that of
(i-1,j)
(which holds true by the inductive assignment). Details follow.

Proof of
Claim
D.9
.

We show that if
i>1
then

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(i,j))\leqslant\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell-1}(i-1,j))+\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(1,j)).

(D.1)

By using this inequality we eventually get that

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(i,j))\leqslant(i\cdot j)\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell-i-j}(1,1))=O\left((i\cdot j)2^{-\frac{1}{2}(\ell-i-j)}\right),

where the final equality holds by
Claim
D.12
.

To prove (
D.1
) we use
Lemma
2.9
. In this case our local decomposition is
T=\left\{G_{B}\left|\;\vphantom{G_{B}B\in\mathcal{M}^{U}(1)}\right.B\in\mathcal{M}^{U}(1)\right\}
. The graph
G_{B}=(L_{B},R_{B})
is the induced bipartite subgraph where
L_{B}=\left\{A\in L\left|\;\vphantom{A\in LB\leqslant A\leqslant U}\right.B\leqslant A\leqslant U\right\}
and
R_{B}=\left\{A\in R\left|\;\vphantom{A\in R(A,B)\in\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,j)}\right.(A,B)\in\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,j)\right\}
. We claim that
G_{B}\cong\mathcal{DS}^{U-B}(i-1,j)
: the isomorphism
\phi:G_{B}\to\mathcal{DS}^{U-B}(i-1,j)
maps

A\in R_{B}\mapsto\phi(A)=A\;\;\;\hbox{and}\;\;\;A\in L_{B}\mapsto\phi(A)=A-B.

For the right sides, we note that
(A,B)\in\mathcal{DS}^{U}(1,j)
if and only if
A\leqslant U-B
so this isomorphism maps the right side of
G_{B}
to that of
\mathcal{DS}^{U-B}(i-1,j)
. For the left sides,
Proposition
2.21
gives us that
B\leqslant A\leqslant U
if and only if
A-B\leqslant U-B
. Thus
\phi
maps matrices
A\in R_{B}
isomorphically to matrices on the left side of
G^{0}_{m-1}(U-B,a_{1},a_{2}-1)
. Checking that
A_{1}\sim A_{2}
in
\mathcal{DS}^{U-B}(i-1,j)
if and only if
\phi(A_{1})\sim\phi(A_{2})
in
G_{B}
is direct. Therefore for every
B
, the second largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}(G_{B})=\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{DS}^{\ell-1}(i-1,j))
.

We note that by definition the graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau,L}=\mathcal{DS}^{\ell}(1,j)
. Thus (
D.1
) follows from
Lemma
2.9
.
∎

D.3
Bounding the expansion of the relation graphs

We start by proving the claim in the following special case.

Claim D.15
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
and let
i,j
be such that
i\leqslant\frac{j}{3}
, then

\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,j))\leqslant\gamma^{j}.

After proving
Claim
D.15
, we prove
Claim
D.11
by using
Lemma
2.9
again.

Proof of
Claim
D.15
.

Fix
D,i,j
. For this case we intend to use
Corollary
2.4
on the two step walk on
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
. We prove that

\max_{B_{1}\in V}\lVert\mu_{B_{1}}-J\rVert_{1}\leqslant\gamma^{\prime\prime j}

(D.2)

where
\mu_{B_{1}}
is the distribution of the two step walk starting from
B_{1}
, and the distribution
J
is the uniform distribution over
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
. Fix
B_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
and from now on we just write
\mu
instead of
\mu_{B_{1}}
for short.

We will show that there exists a set
V^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
such that both
\ProbOp_{{B_{2}\sim\mu}}\left[B_{2}\in V^{\prime}\right],\ProbOp_{{B_{2}\sim J}}\left[B_{2}\in V^{\prime}\right]\geqslant 1-p
for a sufficiently small
p\leqslant(\gamma^{\prime})^{j}
. Then we will also prove that the distribution
\mu
conditioned on this event
V^{\prime}
is uniform, and this will give us (
D.2
). Here

V^{\prime}=\left\{B_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)\left|\;\vphantom{B_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)\row(B_{2})\cap(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))=\{0\},\col(B_{2})\cap(\col(B_{1})\oplus\col(D))=\{0\}}\right.\row(B_{2})\cap(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))=\{0\},\col(B_{2})\cap(\col(B_{1})\oplus\col(D))=\{0\}\right\}.

A quick calculation will show that in this case
\lVert\mu-J\rVert_{1}\leqslant 2p\leqslant\gamma^{j}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

First we show that
\ProbOp_{{B_{2}\sim J}}\left[V^{\prime}\right]\geqslant 1-O(2^{2i-j})
: Let us show that the probability of choosing a uniform
B_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)
such that
\row(B_{2})\cap(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))\neq\{0\}
is at most
i2^{2i-j}
(the same also holds for the column spaces). The row span of
B_{2}
is sampled uniformly as an
i
dimensional subspace inside a
n
dimensional space, conditioned on
\row(B_{2})\cap\row(D)=\{0\}
. So this probability is

\ProbOp_{{\row(B_{2})}}\left[(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))\cap\row(B_{2})\neq\{0\}\left|\;\vphantom{(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))\cap\row(B_{2})\neq\{0\}\row(B_{2})\cap\row(D)=\{0\}}\right.\row(B_{2})\cap\row(D)=\{0\}\right]\\
\leqslant\frac{\ProbOp_{{\row(B_{2})}}\left[(\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D))\cap\row(B_{2})\neq\{0\}\right]}{\ProbOp_{{\row(B_{2})}}\left[\row(B_{2})\cap\row(D)=\{0\}\right]}.

The ratio is bounded by
\frac{i2^{2i+\ell-n-1}}{1-i2^{\ell-n}}\leqslant O(i2^{2i+(\ell-n)})
via standard calculations. The same probability holds for the columns spaces, therefore
\ProbOp_{{B_{2}\sim J}}\left[V^{\prime}\right]\geqslant 1-O(i2^{i+(\ell-n)})=1-O(\gamma^{(2i-j)})
. The last equality is by
j\leqslant n-\ell
.

Next we claim that
\ProbOp_{{B_{2}\sim\mu}}\left[V^{\prime}\right]\geqslant 1-O(2^{2i-j})
. Indeed, the two step walk from
B_{1}
traverses to some
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)
(whose row space intersects trivially with
\row(D)
), and from
U
we independently choose a matrix
B_{2}\leqslant U
. As
\row(U)\cap\row(D)=\{0\}
, it follows that
\row(B_{2})\cap\row(B_{1})\oplus\row(D)\neq\{0\}
if and only if
\row(B_{1})\cap\row(B_{2})\neq\{0\}
. For any fixed
U\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)
, the probability that
\row(B_{1})\cap\row(B_{2})\neq\{0\}
is at most
O(i2^{2i-j})
(repeating the calculation above).

Now let us show that conditioned on
V^{\prime}
, for any two neighbors
B_{2},B_{2}^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
of
B_{1}
in the two step walk, the probability of sampling
B_{2}
is the same as sampling
B_{2}^{\prime}
. To show this it is enough to show that there exists a graph isomorphism of the original “one-step graph”, that fixes
B_{1}
and sends
B_{2}
to
B_{2}^{\prime}
. Let us denote by
GL(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n})
the general linear group, or equivalently the group of
n\times n
invertible matrices. We will find
E_{1}\in GL(w_{1}),E_{2}\in GL(w_{2})
such that:

1.

E_{2}A=AE_{1}=A
.

2.

E_{2}B_{1}E_{1}=B_{1}
.

3.

E_{2}B_{2}E_{1}=B_{2}^{\prime}
.

Assume we found such
E_{1},E_{2}
. We claim that
X\mapsto E_{2}XE_{1}
is an isomorphism of the one-step graph. Indeed, note that if
A\oplus B
then as multiplying by an invertible matrix maintains rank, it follows that
E_{2}AE_{1}\oplus E_{2}BE_{1}=A\oplus E_{2}BE_{1}
. Therefore,
X\mapsto E_{2}XE_{1}
is a bijection on the vertices of the graph. It is easy to check that this multiplication also maintains the
\leqslant
relation so this is indeed an isomorphism. Finally, it is also easy to see that
(B_{1},M,B_{2})
is mapped to a path
(B_{1},E_{2}ME_{1},B_{2}^{\prime})
, so in particular such an isomorphism implies that the probability of sampling
B_{2},B_{2}^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
is the same.

To find such
E_{1},E_{2}
recall that
E_{2}(e\otimes f)E_{1}=(E_{2}e)\otimes(E_{1}f)
. Thus we can decompose
A=\sum_{t=1}^{\ell}e_{t}\otimes f_{t}
,
B_{1}=\sum_{t=1}^{i}e^{\prime}_{t}\otimes f^{\prime}_{t}
,
B_{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{i}h_{t}\otimes k_{t}
and
B_{2}^{\prime}=\sum_{t=1}^{i}h^{\prime}_{t}\otimes k^{\prime}_{t}
. By assumption on the intersections of the row (and column) spaces, the sets
\{e_{t}\}_{t=1}^{\ell}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{e^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{i}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{h_{t}\}_{t=1}^{i}
and
\{e_{t}\}_{t=1}^{\ell}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{e^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{i}\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\{h^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{i}
are independent and have the same size. The same holds for the corresponding sets of column vectors (replacing
e,h
with
f,k
). Thus we just find any
E_{1},E_{2}
such that

E_{1}e_{t}=e_{t},\;E_{1}e^{\prime}_{t}=e^{\prime}_{t},\;E_{1}h_{t}=h^{\prime}_{t}

E_{2}f_{t}=f_{t},\;E_{1}f^{\prime}_{t}=f^{\prime}_{t},\;E_{1}k_{t}=k^{\prime}_{t}.

These will have the desired properties. By the arguments above, the claim is proven.
∎

We comment that the above proof is one place where an analogous argument fails to hold for higher dimensional tensors. We heavily relied on the fact that for any fixed matrix
U
, the row span and column span of an
i
rank matrix
B\leqslant U
is uniformly distributed over
i
dimensional subspaces inside
\row(U)
and
\col(U)
, even if these marginals are not independent.

We move on to the general case.

Proof of
Claim
D.11
.

Fix
i,j,\ell
and
D\in\mathcal{M}(\ell)
. The case where
i\leqslant\frac{j}{3}
is by
Claim
D.15
so assume that
\frac{j}{3}<i\leqslant\frac{j}{2}
. Towards using
Lemma
2.9
, we decompose
\mathcal{R}_{\cap D}(i,j)
to components
\{G_{B}\}_{B\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(\frac{j}{3})}
where
G_{B}
is the graph induced by all edges
\{M,C\}
such that
B\leqslant C\leqslant M
. We claim that
G_{B}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}_{\cap(D\oplus B)}(i-\frac{j}{3},\frac{2j}{3})
. The isomorphism
\phi:G_{B}\to\mathcal{R}_{\cap(D\oplus B)}(i-\frac{j}{3},\frac{2j}{3})
is
\phi(M)=M-B
. Let us start by showing that this maps vertices to vertices.

Indeed, fix any
B\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(\frac{j}{3})
and let
G_{B}=(L_{B},R_{B},E_{B})
. Explicitly,

L_{B}=\left\{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)\left|\;\vphantom{M\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(i)M\geqslant B}\right.M\geqslant B\right\},R_{B}=\left\{C\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)\left|\;\vphantom{C\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)C\geqslant B}\right.C\geqslant B\right\}.

Note that every
M\in L_{B}
is a direct sum
M\oplus D
, and as
B\leqslant M
this implies that
(B\oplus(M-B))\oplus D
. As we saw before, this implies that
(B\oplus D)\oplus(M-B)
, therefore
\phi(M)=M-B\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap(D\oplus B)}(i-\frac{j}{3})
. Conversely, if
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap(D\oplus B)}(i-\frac{j}{3})
then
\phi^{-1}(M^{\prime})=M^{\prime}\oplus B
has
\rank(\phi^{-1}(M^{\prime}))=i
. Moreover, as
M^{\prime}\oplus(D\oplus B)
then
(M^{\prime}\oplus B)\oplus D
, i.e.
\phi^{-1}(M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(j)
. It is also clear that
B\leqslant\phi^{-1}(M^{\prime})
hence
\phi^{-1}(M^{\prime})\in L_{B}
. Of course, a similar argument holds for the right side
R_{B}
, then
\phi
is a bijection of the vertex set. Finally, by
Claim
C.3
,
C\leqslant M
if and only if
C-B\leqslant M-B
, so this is indeed an isomorphism of graphs.

We note that if
i\leqslant\frac{j}{2}
then
i-\frac{j}{3}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2j}{3}
and therefore we can use
Claim
D.15
and deduce that for every
B\in\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(\frac{j}{3})
,
\lambda_{2}(G_{B})\leqslant\gamma^{j}
. Moreover, we observe that the bipartite local to global graph for the left side,
\mathcal{H}_{\tau,L}
is
\mathcal{M}_{\cap D}(\frac{j}{3},j)
, thus by
Claim
D.15
this also has
\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\tau,L})\leqslant\gamma^{j}
and so by
Lemma
2.9
the lemma is proven.
∎

We comment that the requirement that
i\leqslant\frac{j}{2}
is just for convenience. Multiple uses of the same decomposition with
Lemma
2.9
, would yield a similar bound whenever
i\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)j
for any fixed constant
\varepsilon>0
. As we only need the claim for
i=\frac{j}{2}
, we merely mention this without proof.

D.4
Short paths in
\mathcal{T}^{m}

This subsection of the appendix is devoted to the proof of
Claim
6.32
which is necessary to analyze the coboundary expansion of the complexes in
[
Gol23
]
.

We begin with the following auxiliary claim proven in the end of the subsection.

Claim D.16
.

Let
Z_{1}
be a rank
4m
matrix and let
Z_{2}
be a rank
3m
matrix.

1.

Let
A,B\leqslant Z_{1}
be such that
\rank(A)=\rank(B)=m
. Then there exists a matrix
T
with
\rank(T)=m
such that
A\oplus T,T\oplus B\leqslant Z_{1}
.

2.

Let
A,B\leqslant Z_{2}
be such that
\rank(A)=\rank(B)=m
then there exists
M^{1},M^{2},M^{3}
such that

A\oplus M^{1},M^{1}\oplus M^{2},M^{2}\oplus M^{3},M^{3}\oplus B\leqslant Z_{2}.

Phrasing this claim differently, the first item claims that any two matrices in one side of
\mathcal{DS}^{4m}(m,m)
have a length
2
path between them. The first item claims that any two matrices in one side of
\mathcal{DS}^{3m}(m,m)
have a length
4
path between them.

Proof of
Claim
6.32
.

We proceed as follows. We first decompose
C,D\in\mathcal{T}^{Z}
into
m
ranked matrices such that
C=C^{1}\oplus C^{2}
and
D=D^{1}\oplus D^{2}
. Then we find some
T
of rank
m
such that
T\oplus C^{1},T\oplus D^{1}\leqslant Z
. This is possible due to the first item in
Claim
D.16
.

Then we will prove that there exists a
12
-path composed of three paths of length
4
: one from
C^{1}\oplus C^{2}
to
C^{1}\oplus T
, one from
C^{1}\oplus T
to
D^{1}\oplus T
and one from
D^{1}\oplus T
to
D^{1}\oplus D^{2}
.

Let us show how to construct the first path, the other two are constructed similarly. Note that
C^{1}\leqslant C^{1}\oplus C^{2},C^{1}\oplus T
and that they are both
\leqslant Z
. Thus if we find
M^{1},M^{2},M^{3}\leqslant Z-C^{1}
such that

C^{2}\oplus M^{1},M^{1}\oplus M^{2},M^{2}\oplus M^{3},M^{3}\oplus T\leqslant Z-C^{1},

Then by
Proposition
2.21
, adding
C^{1}
to all three
M^{1},M^{2},M^{3}
will give us the desired path in
\mathcal{T}^{Z}
:

(C^{1}\oplus C^{2},C^{1}\oplus M^{1},C^{1}\oplus M^{2},C^{1}\oplus M^{3},C^{1}\oplus T).

But observe that
Z-C^{1}
has rank
3m
, and thus existence of such matrices
M^{1},M^{2},M^{3}
is promised by
Claim
D.16
so we can conclude.
∎

Proof of
Claim
D.16
.

Without loss of generality let us assume that
Z_{1}
is the identity matrix of rank
4m
and that
Z_{2}
is the identity matrix of rank
3m
. We wish to use the criterion for
A\leqslant I
given in
Claim
D.13
.

For this proof we also require the following observation which follows from elementary linear algebra. We leave its proof to the reader.

Observation D.17
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a subspace of dimension
j
. Let
E=\{e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{k}\}
be
k
independent vectors. Assume that
W\cap\mathrm{span}(E)=\{0\}
. Then for every
b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{k}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
there exists a solution
x
to the set of linear equations:

1.

For every
w\in W
,
\langle x,w\rangle=0
, and

2.

For every
i=1,2,\dots,k
,
\langle x,e_{i}\rangle=b_{i}
.

Let us begin with the first item. Given
A,B
of rank
m
we need to find some
T
of tank
m
such that
A\oplus T,B\oplus T\leqslant Z_{1}
. Equivalently, we will find
T
such that
A\oplus T
and
B\oplus T
satisfies the criterion in
Claim
D.13
.

Let us write
A=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(A)\otimes f_{j}(A),B=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(B)\otimes f_{j}(B)
where these decompositions satisfy the property in
Claim
D.13
. Let
W=\left\{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4m}\left|\;\vphantom{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4m}\forall j=1,2,\dots,m\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0}\right.\forall j=1,2,\dots,m\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0\right\}
. As
W
is a solution space of
2m
linear equations over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{4m}
, it is a subspace of dimension
\geqslant 2m
. We will select the rows of
T
from
W
.

However, it is not enough to just select arbitrary rows from
W
, since we need to satisfy the condition that if
e_{i},f_{j}
participates in the decomposition of
T
then
\langle e_{i},f_{j}\rangle=1
if and only if
i=j
. In addition, we will also require that
f_{j}
will be perpendicular to all the
e_{i}(A),e_{i}(B)
’s. This will define a set of linear equations which we will solve using
Observation
D.17
. To do this we will find an
m
-dimensional subspace in
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
that intersects trivially with the span of the
e_{j}(A),e_{j}(B)
’s (the sum of row spaces of
A
and
B
).

We note that
W\cap\row(A)=W\cap\row(B)=\{0\}
. Let us show this for
\row(A)
: any non-zero vector
x\in\row(A)
is a sum of
\sum_{j=1}^{m}b_{j}e_{j}(A)
where at least on of the
b_{j}
’s are not zero. On the other hand,
\langle x,f_{j}(A)\rangle=b_{j}
, so there is at least one
\langle x,f_{j}(A)\rangle\neq 0
, implying that
x\notin W
. Thus we conclude that
\mathrm{dim}(W\cap(\row(A)+\row(B)))\leqslant m
, and in particular there exists some
m
dimensional subspace
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
such that
W^{\prime}
intersects trivially with
\row(A)+\row(B)
. Let
E=\{e_{1}(T),\dots,e_{m}(T)\}
be a basis of
W^{\prime}
.

Thus by
Observation
D.17
, for every
i=1,2,\dots,m
there exists some
f_{i}(T)
such that

1.

For every
j=1,2,\dots,m
,
\langle e_{j}(A),f_{i}(T)\rangle=\langle e_{j}(B),f_{i}(T)\rangle=0
for all
j=1,2,\dots,m
, and

2.

In addition

\langle e_{j}(T),f_{i}(T)\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}.

By taking
T=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(T)\otimes f_{j}(T)
we observe that both
A\oplus T
and
B\oplus T
satisfy
Claim
D.13
, and therefore
A\oplus T,B\oplus T\leqslant Z_{1}
.

Let us turn to the second item. We continue with the notation
A=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(A)\otimes f_{j}(A),B=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(B)\otimes f_{j}(B)
. The strategy here will be similar. Let
W^{1}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3m}\left|\;\vphantom{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3m}\forall j=1,2,\dots,k\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0}\right.\forall j=1,2,\dots,k\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0\right\}
. We observe that this is the solution set of
2m
linear equations and therefore its dimension is
\geqslant m
. Let
e_{1}(M^{1}),e_{2}(M^{1}),\dots,e_{m}(M^{1})\in W^{1}
be
m
independent vectors, and set
e_{j}(M^{3})=e_{j}(M^{1})
. We observe that as before
W^{1}\cap\row(A)=\{0\}
so by
Observation
D.17
, for every
j=1,2,\dots,m
there exists some
f_{j}(T)
such that

1.

For every
e_{i}(A)
,
\langle e_{i}(A),f_{j}(M^{1})\rangle=0
.

2.

For every
i,j
,

\langle e_{i}(M^{1}),f_{j}(M^{1})\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}.

We note that the linear equations for
f_{j}(M^{1})
’s do not consider the
e_{j}(B)
’s.

Similarly, we find
f_{j}(M^{3})
ignoring the
e_{j}(A)
’s. That is, for every
j=1,2,\dots,m
we find
f_{j}(M^{3})
such that

1.

For every
e_{i}(B)
,
\langle e_{i}(B),f_{j}(M^{3})\rangle=0
.

2.

For every
i,j
,

\langle e_{i}(M^{3}),f_{j}(M^{3})\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}.

Then we set
M^{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(M^{1})\otimes f_{j}(M^{1})
and
M^{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(M^{3})\otimes f_{j}(M^{3})
. It is easy to verify that as before
A\oplus M^{1},B\oplus M^{3}\leqslant Z_{2}
.

Now we find
M^{2}
such that
M^{1}\oplus M^{2},M^{2}\oplus M^{3}\leqslant Z_{2}
. The upshot of the previous step is that now
M^{1}
and
M^{3}
have the same row space
\mathrm{span}(\{e_{1}(M^{1}),e_{2}(M^{1}),\dots,e_{m}(M^{1})\})
. Thus we find
M^{2}
as follows. Let
W^{2}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3m}\left|\;\vphantom{w\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{3m}\forall j=1,2,\dots m\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0}\right.\forall j=1,2,\dots m\;\langle w,f_{j}(A)\rangle=\langle w,f_{j}(B)\rangle=0\right\}
. This subspace also has dimension
\geqslant m
so let
e_{1}(M^{2}),e_{2}(M^{2}),\dots,e_{m}(M^{2})\in W^{2}
be a set of independent vectors from
W^{2}
.

As before, we observe that
W^{2}\cap\row(M^{1})=\{0\}
but this time
\row(M^{1})+\row(M^{3})=\row(M^{1})
. Thus by
Observation
D.17
for every
j=1,2,\dots,m
, we can find
f_{j}(M^{2})
such that

1.

For every
i=1,2,\dots,m
,
\langle e_{i}(M^{1}),f_{j}(M^{2})\rangle=\langle e_{i}(M^{3}),f_{j}(M^{2})\rangle=0
, and

2.

In addition,

\langle e_{i}(M^{2}),f_{j}(M^{2})\rangle=\begin{cases}1&i=j\\
0&i\neq j\end{cases}.

Then we set
M^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}(M^{2})\otimes f_{j}(M^{2})
. It is direct to verify that
M^{1}\oplus M^{2},M^{2}\oplus M^{3}\leqslant Z_{2}
by
Claim
D.13
.
∎

Appendix E
Second largest eigenvalue of
J(n,k,k/2)

Proof of
Lemma
4.10
.

Let
n=(2+\eta)k
. For the Johnson graph
J(n,k,k/2)
, its largest unnormalized eigenvalue

\lambda_{0}=\binom{k}{k/2}\binom{n-k}{k/2}\penalty\ .

Therefore by
Theorem
4.8
the absolute values of the normalized eigenvalues are

\displaystyle\frac{\lvert\lambda_{t}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lvert\sum_{i=0}^{t}(-1)^{t-i}\binom{k-i}{k/2-i}\binom{n-k+i-t}{k/2+i-t}\binom{t}{i}\rvert}{\binom{k}{k/2}\binom{n-k}{k/2}}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\min(t,k/2)}(-1)^{t-i}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\frac{k/2\times\dots\times(k/2-i+1)}{k\times\dots\times(k-i+1)}\cdot\frac{k/2\times\dots\times(k/2+i-t+1)}{(n-k)\times\dots\times(n-k+i-t+1)}\right|\penalty\ .

Here the convention is that when we write
\binom{a}{b}
for
b>a
or
b<0
, then the term is equal to
0
. Thus, the terms corresponding to
i<t-k/2
or
i>\min\{t,k/2\}
vanish since the product of the three binomial coefficients is
0
for these choices of
i
. We first show the statement for odd
t\in[k]
. In this case, we group terms
\binom{t}{i}
and
\binom{t}{t-i}
together to get

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\left|\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}-\prod_{j=0}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\cdot\left|\prod_{j=i}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}-\prod_{j=i}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\right|\penalty\ .

To simplify the products we first observe that for any
0\leqslant j<k/2
,
\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\leqslant\frac{k/2}{k}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\leqslant\frac{k/2}{n-k}=\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}
.

\leqslant\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{i}\cdot\left|\prod_{j=i}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}-\prod_{j=i}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\right|\penalty\ .

(E.1)

Furthermore we use the following claim to bound the term in absolute value:

Claim E.1
.

For any constant
\eta>0
and any integers
1\leqslant m<C
and
0\leqslant s\leqslant m
,

\prod_{j=s}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2C-j}-\prod_{j=s}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2(1+\eta)C-j}\leqslant\left(\frac{C-s}{2C-s}\right)^{m-s+1}-\left(\frac{C-s}{2(1+\eta)C-s}\right)^{m-s+1}\penalty\ .

A proof is given later in the appendix. By the claim the expression in
(
E.1
)
can be bounded by a binomial sum:

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{t-2i}-\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t-2i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\lfloor t/2\rfloor}\left|(-1)^{i}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t-i}+(-1)^{t-i}\binom{t}{t-i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{t-i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\sum_{i=0}^{t}(-1)^{i}\binom{t}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t-i}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}\penalty\ .

Next consider the case of even
t\in[k]
. First use the binomial identity
\binom{t}{i}=\binom{t-1}{i}+\binom{t-1}{i-1}
to obtain the following bound

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\sum_{i=\max(0,t-k/2)}^{\min(t,k/2)}\left((-1)^{t-1-(i-1)}\binom{t-1}{i-1}-(-1)^{t-1-i}\binom{t-1}{i}\right)\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\right|

\displaystyle=|\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\min(t-1,k/2)}(-1)^{t-1-i}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-i-2}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathbb{1}[t-1>k/2]\cdot(-1)^{t-1-k/2}\binom{t-1}{k/2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{k/2}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-k/2-2}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\min(t-1,k/2)}(-1)^{t-1-i}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\mathbb{1}[t-1\geqslant k/2]\cdot(-1)^{k/2}\binom{t-1}{k/2}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-2-k/2}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{k/2}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}|\penalty\ .

First note that the second term and the fourth term always evaluate to
0
. Thus we can simplify the expression as

\displaystyle\leqslant\left|\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\min(t-1,k/2)}(-1)^{t-1-i}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\frac{(k/2-i)}{(k-i)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\right|

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\left|\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\min(t-1,k/2)}(-1)^{t-1-i}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\frac{k/2-(t-i-1)}{n-k-(t-i-1)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{(k/2-j)}{(k-j)}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\right|\penalty\ .

These two terms resembles the expression of
\frac{\lvert\lambda_{t-1}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}
and can be bounded by grouping terms
\binom{t-1}{i}
and
\binom{t-1}{t-1-i}
.

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\lfloor(t-1)/2\rfloor}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\cdot\left|\prod_{j=i}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}-\prod_{j=i}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j-1}{k-j-1}\right|

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\lfloor(t-1)/2\rfloor}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{i}\frac{k/2-j}{n-k-j}\cdot\left|\prod_{j=i}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j-1}{n-k-j-1}-\prod_{j=i}^{(t-1)-i-1}\frac{k/2-j}{k-j}\right|

Using
Claim
E.1
again yields

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\lfloor(t-1)/2\rfloor}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i+1}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{(t-1)-2i}-\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{(t-1)-2i}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i=\max(0,t-1-k/2)}^{\lfloor(t-1)/2\rfloor}\binom{t-1}{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{i}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{i+1}\cdot\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{(t-1)-2i}-\left(\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{(t-1)-2i}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)^{t-1}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}\penalty\ .

We thus conclude that for all
t\in[k]
,
\frac{\lvert\lambda_{t}\rvert}{\lambda_{0}}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{2(1+\eta)}
.
∎

Proof of
Claim
E.1
.

We prove the statement by induction on
s
. The case of
s=m
holds trivially. Suppose the statement holds for all
s>s^{*}
, then we are going to show that the inequality also holds for
s=s^{*}
.
Note that by the induction hypothesis

\prod_{j=s^{*}+1}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2C-j}-\prod_{j=s^{*}+1}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2(1+\eta)C-j}\leqslant\left(\frac{C-(s^{*}+1)}{2C-(s^{*}+1)}\right)^{m-s^{*}}-\left(\frac{C-(s^{*}+1)}{2(1+\eta)C-(s^{*}+1)}\right)^{m-s^{*}}\penalty\ .

(E.2)

To simplify the expressions we define

X(s)=\prod_{j=s}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2C-j}\penalty\ \penalty\ x(s)=\frac{C-s}{2C-s}

and also

Y(s)=\prod_{j=s}^{m}\frac{C-j}{2(1+\eta)C-j}\penalty\ \penalty\ y(s)=\frac{C-s}{2(1+\eta)C-s}.

Using this notation, (
E.2
) can be rewritten as

X(s^{*}+1)-Y(s^{*}+1)\leqslant x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}.

Rearranging the inequality we get that

\displaystyle x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-X(s^{*}+1)

\displaystyle\geqslant y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-Y(s^{*}+1)

\displaystyle x(s^{*})\cdot(x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-X(s^{*}+1))

\displaystyle\geqslant y(s^{*})\cdot(y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{1}}-Y(s^{*}+1))

\displaystyle x(s^{*})\cdot x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-X(s^{*})

\displaystyle\geqslant y(s^{*})\cdot y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-Y(s^{*})

\displaystyle X(s^{*})-Y(S^{*})

\displaystyle\leqslant x(s^{*})\cdot x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*})\cdot y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}

(E.3)

Now we bound this difference

\displaystyle\left(x(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}+1}-y(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}+1}\right)-\left(x(s^{*})\cdot x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*})\cdot y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x(s^{*})\cdot\left(x(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}\right)-y(s^{*})\cdot\left(y(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}\right)

(E.4)

We note that for fixed constants
\alpha,\beta>1
, and
\gamma>0
, the function
p(x)=\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta(1+x)}\right)^{\gamma}-\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{\beta(1+x)-1}\right)^{\gamma}
is decreasing when
x\geqslant 0
. If we set
\alpha=C-s^{*},\beta=2C-s^{*}
, and
\gamma=m-s^{*}
, then

x(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}=p(0),\text{ while }y(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}=p\left(\frac{2\eta C}{2C-s^{*}}\right).

Therefore

x(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}\geqslant y(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}},

and together with the fact that
x(s^{*})\geqslant y(s^{*})
we can deduce that (
E.4
)
\geqslant 0
. Equivalently,

x(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}+1}-y(s^{*})^{m-s^{*}+1}\geqslant x(s^{*})\cdot x(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}-y(s^{*})\cdot y(s^{*}+1)^{m-s^{*}}\geqslant X(s^{*})-Y(s^{*}),

where the second inequality holds by (
E.3
). Note that this inequality is exactly the claim statement for
s=s^{*}
. Thus we finish the induction step and conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Sparsifying the Johnson complexes

While both the Johnson complexes and the RGCs over
N=2^{n-1}
vertices have vertex degree
\mathsf{N}
, the Johnson complexes have much denser vertex links, or equivalently more
2
-faces. The degree of the Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
’s vertex links is

d_{link}={\binom{\varepsilon n}{\varepsilon n/2}}\cdot{\binom{(1-\varepsilon)n}{\varepsilon n/2}}\gg N^{\varepsilon/2},

while that of the RGCs is
\mathrm{polylog}(N)
. In fact we can sparsify the links and reduce the number of
2
-faces in Johnson complexes by keeping each
2
-face with probability
p
, and set
p
so that the link degree becomes
\mathrm{polylog}(N)
. Then we shall use the following theorem to show that the link expansion is preserved after subsampling.

Theorem F.1

(Theorem 1.1
[
CH07
]
)
.

Suppose
G
is a graph on
n
vertices with second largest eigenvalue in absolute value
\lambda_{2}(G)
and minimum degree
d_{\min}
. A random subgraph
H
of
G
with edge-selection probability
p
has a second eigenvalue
\lambda_{2}(H)
satisfying

\lambda_{2}(H)\leqslant\lambda_{2}(G)+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{pd_{\min}}}+\frac{(\log n)^{3/2}}{pd_{\min}(\log\log n)^{3/2}}\right)

with probability
>1-f(n)
where
f(n)
tends to
0
faster than any polynomial in
\frac{1}{n}
.

From this eigenvalue preservation result we can get the following sparsification.

Corollary F.2
.

Consider a random subcomplex
X^{\prime}
of a
2
-dimensional Johnson complex
X_{\varepsilon,n}
constructed by selecting each
2
-face i.i.d. with probability
p\geqslant\frac{(h(\varepsilon)\log N)^{3/2}}{d_{link}}
where
N=2^{n-1}
, and
d_{link}
is the degree of
X_{\varepsilon,n}
’s vertex links. Then with high probability every vertex link of
X^{\prime}
is a
\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)(1+o_{n}(1))
-spectral expander.

Here we recall that
h(x)
is the binary entropy function.

Proof.

We first argue that almost surely
X^{\prime}(1)=X_{\varepsilon,n}(1)
so that for every
v\in X^{\prime}(0)
the vertex link
X^{\prime}_{v}
has the same vertex set as
(X_{\varepsilon,n})_{v}
. From the subsampling process we know that for any edge
\{v,v+s\}\in X_{\varepsilon,n}(1)
, the degree of
v+s
in the link of
v
follows a binomial distribution with

\E[\textrm{degree of }v+s\textrm{ in }X^{\prime}_{v}]=p\cdot d_{link}=(h(\varepsilon)\log N)^{3/2}.

By the Chernoff bound for binomial distributions we know that

\ProbOp[\textrm{degree of }v+s\textrm{ in }X^{\prime}_{v}\leqslant 0.1(h(\varepsilon)\log N)^{3/2}]\leqslant e^{-0.45(h(\varepsilon)\log N)^{3/2}}=N^{-\Theta(\sqrt{\log N})}.

So by a simply union bound over all pairs of
\{v,v+s\}
we get that with probability
1-N^{1+h(\varepsilon)}\cdot N^{-\Theta(\sqrt{\log N})}=1-o(1)
every link in
X^{\prime}_{v}
has the same vertex set as
(X_{\varepsilon,n})_{v}
.

Next, recall by
Lemma
4.4

X_{\varepsilon,n}
’s vertex links have second eigenvalue
<\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)
. Since the choice of
p
ensures that
\sqrt{\frac{\log N^{h(\varepsilon)}}{p\cdot d_{link}}}+\frac{(\log h(\varepsilon))^{3/2}}{p\cdot d_{link}(\log\log h(\varepsilon)N)^{3/2}}=o(1)
, each link of
X^{\prime}
is a
\frac{1}{2}(1-\varepsilon)(1+o(1))
expander. Finally, applying an union bound over all links of
X^{\prime}
completes the proofs.
∎

Appendix G
Proof of the van Kampen lemma

To prove this lemma, we would like to contract
C_{0}
using the contractions of
\{C_{i}\}_{i>0}
so the proof should give us an order in which
C_{i}
-s are contracted. This order is given by the order in which the set
\{f_{i}\}_{i>0}
are contracted to reduce
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus f_{0}
to a point. Therefore, for every such diagram, it suffices to find a single face
f_{j}
such that we can contract to obtain a diagram of a cycle with one less interior face. The property we need from
f_{j}
is that the shared boundary of
f_{j}
and
f_{0}
is a contiguous path. This requirement is captured in the following definition.

Definition G.1

(valid face and path)
.

Let
H
be a van Kampen diagram. We say that an interior face
f_{j}
is
valid
if we can write its boundary cycle
\tilde{C}_{j}=P_{j}\circ Q
such that the exterior cycle can be written as
\tilde{C}_{0}=P_{j}\circ Q^{\prime}
where
P_{j}
contains at least one edge and the vertices of
Q,Q^{\prime}
are disjoint (except for the endpoints). In this case we call
P_{j}
a
valid path
.

Lemma
5.7
will easily follow from the following three claims.

Claim G.2
.

Every
2
-connected plane graph
H
has at least one valid face.

Claim G.3
.

Let
P_{j}=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
be a valid path in
H
. Then for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant m-1
,
\deg(v_{i})=2
.

Claim G.4
.

Let
H=(V,E)
be a van Kampen diagram, let
P_{j}=(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
and let
H_{j}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be the induced subgraph on
V^{\prime}=V\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m-1}\}
. Then
H_{j}
is
2
-vertex connected.

Given the three claims, we can prove the van Kampen lemma as follows.

Figure 12
:
Contracting a face in
H
to obtain
H_{j}
in
Lemma
5.7

Proof of
Lemma
5.7
.

Fix
X,C_{0},H,F,\psi
be as defined in
Section
5.3
. We prove this lemma by induction on the number of inner faces of
H
. If
H
has exactly one inner face
f_{1}
, then its boundary
\tilde{C}_{1}
is also the boundary
\tilde{C}_{0}
of the outer face
f_{0}
. Then we can deduce that
C_{0}=\psi(\tilde{C}_{0})=\psi(\tilde{C}_{1})=C_{1}
. So indeed there is a contraction of
C_{0}
using
m_{0}=m_{1}
-triangles.

Assuming the lemma is true for all van Kampen diagrams with at most
\ell
inner faces, we shall prove it for van Kampen diagrams
H,F
with
\ell+1
inner faces.
By
Claim
G.2
, we can always find a valid face
f_{j}
in
H
such that
\tilde{C}_{0}=P_{j}\circ Q
and
\tilde{C}_{j}=P_{j}\circ P^{\prime}
where
P_{j}
is the corresponding valid path. The decompositions are illustrated in
Figure
12
.

Using
Claim
5.13
, we can contract
C_{0}
(the image of the outer boundary of
H
under
\psi
) to
\psi(Q\circ P^{\prime})
using
m_{j}
triangles. Correspondingly, we can construct an induced subgraph
H_{j}
of
H
by removing all edges and intermediate vertices in
P_{j}
.

Note that the the outer boundary of
H_{j}
is
\tilde{C}_{0}^{\prime}=Q\circ P^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, since the intermediate vertices in
P_{j}
all have degree
2
in
H
(by
Claim
G.3
), removing these vertices only removes the inner face
f_{j}
. Therefore,
H_{j}
has
\ell
inner faces.
Finally by
Claim
G.4
,
H_{j}
is still a
2
-connected plane graph.

Thereby
H_{j},F\setminus\{f_{j}\}
is a van Kampen diagram of the cycle
\psi(\tilde{C}_{0}^{\prime})
in
X
with
\ell
inner faces, and so the induction hypothesis applies.
Thus we complete the inductive step and the lemma is proven.
∎

Next we shall prove the three supporting claims. Before doing so, we define the cyclic ordering of vertices in a cycle which will later be used in the proofs.

Definition G.5

(Cyclic ordering)
.

Let
H=(V,E)
be a graph.
For a given cycle
C
in
H
, a cyclic ordering of the vertices in
C
is such that two consecutive vertices in the ordering share an edge in
C
.

Proof of
Claim
G.2
.

We will show that either there exists a valid face, or that there exists two inner faces
f_{i}\neq f_{j}
and four vertices
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
in the cycle
C_{0}
such that
v_{1},v_{3}\in f_{i}
,
v_{2},v_{4}\in f_{j}
(that is, on the boundaries of the respective faces), and such that in the cyclic ordering of
C_{0}
,
v_{1}\leqslant v_{2}\leqslant v_{3}\leqslant v_{4}
. Then we will show that the latter case implies there is an embedding of a
5
-clique on the plane, reaching a contradiction. We mention that these points do not need to be vertices in
H
, only points on the plane.

Indeed, take a face
f_{i}
that shares an edge with
f_{0}
. If it is not valid, it partitions
C_{0}\setminus\partial f_{i}
into disconnected segments (and this partition is not empty since otherwise
f_{i}
was valid). If there exists some
f_{j}
that touches two distinct segments, we can take
v_{1},v_{3}\in f_{i}
and
v_{2},v_{4}\in f_{j}
as above. Otherwise, we take some
S\subseteq C_{0}\setminus\partial f_{i}
, that contains an edge. We now search for a valid face whose path is contained in
S
, and continue as before. That is, take some
f_{i^{\prime}}
that shares an edge in
S
with the outer face. If it is valid we take
f_{i^{\prime}}
and if not we consider the partition of
S\setminus f_{i^{\prime}}
as before. We note that this process stops because the number of edges in the segment in every step strictly decreases - and when we stop we either find a valid face or four points as above.

Now let us embed the
5
-clique. Indeed, we fix a point on the outer face
v_{0}
in the interior of
f_{0}
, and take
\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
to be the vertices of the clique. The edges are drawn as follows.

1.

From
v_{0}
to every other vertex we can find four non intersecting curves that only go through
f_{0}
.
27
27

27

For this one can use the Riemann mapping theorem to isomorphically map the outer face to the unit disc and
C_{0}
to its boundary. Without loss of generality we can choose
v_{0}
so that it is mapped to the center. Then we draw straight lines from the image of
v_{0}
to the rest of the points in the boundary (and use the inverse to map this diagram back to our graph).

2.

The edges
\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{2},v_{3}\},\{v_{3},v_{4}\},\{v_{4},v_{1}\}
are the curves induced by the cycle
C_{0}
. These curves do not intersect the previous ones (other than in
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3},v_{4}
) since the curves in the first item lie in the interior of
f_{0}
.

3.

By assumption, the pairs
v_{1},v_{3}\in f_{i}
and
v_{2},v_{4}\in f_{j}
so we can connect these pairs by curves that lie in the interiors of
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
respectively, and in particular they do not intersect any of the other curves.

∎

Proof of
Claim
G.3
.

Let
f_{j}
be the valid face. Let us denote by
e_{0},e_{2}
the edges adjacent to
v
in
P_{0}
which is not an endpoint. Assume towards contradiction that there is another edge
e_{1}
adjacent to
v
. Thus
e_{1}
either lies in the interior or the exterior of
f_{j}
.

If it lies in the interior, then by
2
-vertex connectivity there exists a path starting at
e_{1}
connecting to another point in the boundary of
f_{j}
, and other than
v
and the end point, the path lies in the interior of
f_{j}
. This is a contradiction, since any such path disconnects
f_{j}
to two faces. Otherwise it lies in the exterior, but the argument is the same only with
f_{0}
instead of
f_{j}
. ∎

Proof of
Claim
G.4
.

Let
v_{0},v_{1}
the end points in
P_{1}
, and notice that
v_{0},v_{1}
participate in a cycle in
H
- the cycle bounding the outer face. In particular
H_{j}
is connected. Remove any vertex
z
from
H_{j}
. Let
P_{x,y}
be a path that connects two vertices
x,y
in
H\setminus\{z\}
(that may contain some vertices in
P_{j}
). Let us show that we can modify
P_{x,y}
to some
P^{\prime}
in
H_{j}\setminus\{z\}
. By
Claim
G.3
every vertex in
P_{j}
which we removed had degree
2
. Therefore any path containing a vertex in
P_{j}
, contains
all of

P_{j}
(or its reverse). Thus to mend
P_{x,y}
we just need to show that and
v_{0},v_{1}
are in a simple cycle in
H_{j}
. Indeed, this is true from the definition of a valid path:
C_{j}=P_{j}\circ Q
and
C_{0}=P_{j}\circ Q^{\prime}
such that all vertices in
Q,Q^{\prime}
are disjoint besides the end points. The paths
Q,Q^{\prime}
participate in simple cycles therefore
Q^{\prime}\circ Q^{-1}
is a simple cycle and we conclude.
∎

Appendix H
Expansion of containment walks in the Grassmann poset

To stay self contained we reprove
Claim
2.14
in a similar manner as in
[
Dik+18
]
.

Proof of
Claim
2.14
.

Although all the claims below apply for
Y_{w}
for any
w\in Y
, for a simple presentation let us assume that
w=\{0\}
(the same idea works for arbitrary
w
). Let us denote by
U_{i,j}
the bipartite graph operator from
Y(i)
to
Y(j)
. We note that
U_{i,j}=U_{j-1,j}\dots U_{i+1,i+2}U_{i,i+1}
, and therefore it is sufficient to prove that
\lambda_{2}(U_{i,i+1})\leqslant\left(0.61+i\lambda\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. We do so by induction on
i
.

In fact, we prove a slightly stronger statement, that
\lambda_{2}(U_{i,i+1})=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+i\lambda\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This suffices because
\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}\leqslant\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}\leqslant 0.61
. Let
D_{i+1,i}
be the adjoint operator to
U_{i,i+1}
, and denote by
\lambda_{i}=\lambda(D_{i+1,i}U_{i,i+1})
be the second largest eigenvalue of the two step walk from
Y(i)
to
Y(i)
. To show that
\lambda_{2}(U_{i,i+1})=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+i\lambda\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
it suffices to prove that
\lambda_{i}=\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+i\lambda\right)
.

The base case for the induction is for
U_{1,2}
. In this case

\lambda_{1}=\lambda(D_{2,1}U_{1,2})=\frac{1}{3}I+\frac{2}{3}M^{+}

where
M^{+}
is the non-lazy version of the walk (namely, where we traverse from
w_{1}
to
w_{2}
if
w_{1}+w_{2}\in Y(2)
). The adjacency operator
M^{+}
is the link of
\{0\}
, and thus by
\lambda
-expansion we have that
\lambda_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{3}+\frac{2}{3}\lambda\leqslant\frac{1}{2^{2}-1}+\lambda
.

Assume that
\lambda_{2}(U_{i,i+1})\leqslant\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+i\lambda\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
and consider the two step walk on
Y(i+1)
. As above, we note that

\lambda_{i+1}=\lambda(D_{i+2,i+1}U_{i+1,i+2})=\frac{1}{2^{i+2}-1}I+\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{i+2}-1}\right)M_{i+1}^{+}

where
M^{+}
is the non-lazy version of the walk. We note that the laziness probability
\frac{1}{2^{i+2}-1}
is because every subspace
w\in Y(i+2)
has exactly
2^{i+2}-1
subspaces of codimension
1
(so this is the regularity of the vertex). Thus we will prove below that
\lambda(M_{i+1}^{+})\leqslant\lambda+\lambda_{i}\leqslant\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+(i+1)\lambda\right)
and this will complete the induction.

Let us decompose the non-lazy walk
M^{+}
via
Lemma
2.8
. Our components are
T=\{Y_{W}\}_{W\in Y(i)}
. To be more explicit, note that
W_{1}\sim W_{2}
in the two step walk if and only if
W_{1}+W_{2}\in Y(i+2)
. We can choose such a pair by first choosing
W\in Y(i)
and then choosing an
\{W_{1},W_{2}\}
in the link
Y_{W}
. The distribution of the link if the conditional distribution, hence taking
\mu
to be the
Y(i)
-marginal of the distribution over flags in
Y
, we have that
T
is a local-to-global decomposition. The local-to-global graph
\mathcal{H}_{\tau}
connects
W\in Y(i)
to
W\in Y(i+1)
- so this is just the containment graph
(Y(i),Y(i+1))
which by induction is a
\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
-expander. By
Lemma
2.8
,

\lambda(M_{i+1}^{+})\leqslant\lambda+\lambda_{i}\leqslant\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{i}\frac{1}{2^{\ell+1}-1}+(i+1)\lambda\right)
and we can conclude.
∎

Appendix I
A degree lower bound

A classical result in graph theory says that a connected Cayley graph over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
has degree at least
n
. Moreover, if the Cayley graph is a
\lambda
-expander then it has degree at least
\Omega(\frac{n}{\lambda^{2}\log(1/\lambda)})

[
Alo+92
]
. Are there sharper lower bounds on the degree if the Cayley graph is also a Cayley local spectral expander?

In this section we consider the ‘inverse’ point of view on this question. Given a graph
G
with
m
vertices, what is the maximal
n
such that there exists a connected Cayley complex over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
whose link is isomorphic to
G
. Using this point of view, we prove new lower bounds for Cayley local spectral expanders, that depend on properties of
G
. Specifically, we prove that if
G
has no isolated vertices then
m>1.5(n-1)
; if
G
is connected we prove that
m>2n-1
. Finally, if the link is a
d
-regular edge expander, we prove a bound of
\Omega(d^{1/2}n)
. For all these bounds, we use the code theoretic interpretation of the links given in
[
Gol23
]
.

We begin by describing the link of a Cayley complex, and using this description we give a lower bound on the degree of the Cayley complex in terms of the rank of some matrix. This linear algebraic characterization of a degree lower bound is already implicit in
[
Gol23
, Section 6]
.

After that, we will lower bound the rank of the matrix, and through this we will get lower bounds on the degree of such Cayley complexes.

A link in a Cayley complex
X
naturally comes with labels on the edges which are induced by the generating set (i.e. an edge between
x+s_{1}
and
x+s_{2}
in the link of
x
will be labeled
s_{1}+s_{2}
which by assumption is a generator in the generating set). Note that if
x+s_{1},x+s_{2}
is labeled
s_{1}+s_{2}
then there is a
3
-cycle in the link between
x+s_{1},x+s_{2}
and
x+s_{1}+s_{2}
where every edge whose endpoints correspond to two generators is labeled by the third generator.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with vertex set
V=\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{m}\}
and a labeling
\ell:E\to V
. The graph
G
is
nice
if for every
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}
that is labeled
v_{k}
there is a three cycle
v_{i},v_{j},v_{k}
in the graph where
\{v_{i},v_{k}\}
is labeled
v_{j}
and
\{v_{j},v_{k}\}
is labeled
v_{i}
. We would like to understand for which
n
does there exist a Cayley complex
X
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
so that the vertex links of
X
are isomorphic to
G
. That is, for which
n
can we find generators
s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
such that the map
\phi(s_{i})=v_{i}
is a graph isomorphism between the vertex link and
G
. Observe that for this to hold, whenever there is an edge
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
labeled by
v_{k}
, we require that
s_{i}+s_{j}+s_{k}=0
. Thus we can define the matrix
H_{G}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|E|\times|V|}
to have in every row the indicator vector of
i,j,k
for every
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
labeled by
v_{k}
. Let us show that this is a sufficient condition.

Claim I.1
.

Let
G
be a nice graph with
m
vertices. Let
S=\{s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{m}\}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and let
M_{s}
be the matrix whose rows are the
s_{i}
’s. Then the following is equivalent.

1.

The columns of
M_{s}
are in the kernel of
H_{G}
.

2.

There exists a Cayley complex
X
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and whose link is isomorphic to
G
whose underlying graph is
Cay(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},S)
.

In particular, if
X
is connected then

m\geqslant n+\rank(H_{G})
.

This equivalence is essentially what was proven in
[
Gol23
, Lemma 77]
. The degree bound is a direct consequence of it. Nevertheless, we prove it in our language for clarity.

Proof.

If the first item occurs, this means that for every
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
labeled by
v_{k}
,
s_{i}+s_{j}+s_{k}=0
and therefore the
3
-cycles
(x,x+s_{i},x+s_{i}+s_{j}=x+s_{k},x)
are in
Cay(\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n},S)
so add
\{x+s_{i},x+s_{j},x+s_{k}\}
into the triangle set of
X
. Then it is straightforward to verify that the link of
X
